RVPO also maintains competitive accuracy on GPQA-Diamond while reducing late-stage degradation observed in other multi-reward methods.
  </details>

- **[Think Twice: The Metis-1.6 Technical Report](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37903)**  `arXiv:2610.37903`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Giulianno Vollmer_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Metis is a bet that better data, better training methods and new architectures can buy what a bigger parameter count cannot, at a fraction of the cost. Metis-1.6 is its latest step: two language models built from scratch by a single researcher, Praxis (2.94B parameters, 0.46B active per pass) and Logos (10.23B parameters, 1.18B active per pass). Both run a hybrid Mamba-2 and attention mixture-of-experts stack twice for every token, so each token gets a second look. Each was trained on 1.02T tokens in total: 804.76B of pretraining, 18B of context extension to 131,072 tokens, and 200B of supervised fine-tuning. Metis-1.6 was never meant to challenge frontier models, and across 40 post-trained benchmarks it mostly does not: Logos averages 52.93, seventh of 17 models in its comparison set and within a point of Qwen3-4B. But it gets there on roughly a twelfth of the median training data of the comparators that disclose it, and with fewer active parameters per token than any model that beats it, which puts it on the Pareto frontier for both. In mathematics it ranks second of 30 models, with 67.50% on AIME 2025 and 63.64% on HMMT February 2026. It is weak at code, instruction following and long context. The rest of the report is the part I found most valuable: what went wrong. The optimizer I configured never ran, and attention trained with AdamW for all of pretraining. A 1.77B-parameter n-gram memory learned to switch itself off. Five fine-tuning choices cost capability. And a length reward under group-mean normalization punished correct solutions for being long once a group's pass rate passed 2/3, a sign inversion I prove and fix with a one-line clamp. I close with the 10B-token ablations behind MoRE (Mixture of Recursive Experts), the looped architecture of Metis-1.7.
  </details>

- **[Fine-tuning Multi-modal LLMs with ART: Art-based Reinforcement Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.11854)**  `arXiv:2606.11854`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Michal Chudoba, Sergey Alyaev, Petra Galuscakova, Tomasz Wiktorski_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Adapting Large Language Models (LLMs) to specific tasks usually involves fine-tuning: Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) edits the weights, while soft prompting prepends trainable virtual tokens. Both change how an already-optimized model is served, so serving many task-specific adapters at high throughput requires adapter-aware infrastructure. We propose Art-Based Reinforcement Training (ART), which optimizes a single auxiliary input image to specialize a frozen Multimodal Large Language Model (MLLM) for a specific task. At inference time, ART simply includes this image in each standard multimodal prompt, requiring no changes to the serving engine. We compare ART with LoRA and soft prompting across five reasoning benchmarks for four small MLLMs from three architectures. While different methods perform best for different tasks, ART ranks first or second in 10 of 20 benchmark-MLLM pairs, with its best results on the two math word problem benchmarks (GSM8k and SVAMP). It is significantly ahead of the next best adapter in 2 cases, within the noise of the next best adapter in 6, and significantly above the text-only baseline in 18. ART therefore reaches accuracy close to LoRA and soft prompts while serving faster than LoRA. In the vLLM serving stack, ART reduces throughput by 6% to 14% relative to text-only requests, compared to LoRA's 13% to 93% slowdown. Beyond efficient serving, ART artifacts are plain images, making them easy to store, distribute, and share. This approach keep task-specific adaptation outside the model weights, so the same frozen model can support different tasks without separate model variants.
  </details>

- **[ROM: Real-time Overthinking Mitigation via Streaming Detection and Intervention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.22016)**  `arXiv:2603.22016`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Xinyan Wang, Xiaogeng Liu, Ming Pei, Chaowei Xiao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large reasoning models (LRMs) often suffer from overthinking, continuing with redundant verification and repeated attempts even after reaching a correct solution. This wastes computation and can overturn an initially correct answer. We show that the transition from productive to redundant reasoning is decodable from late-layer hidden states. These representations separate the two phases around first-correct-solution boundaries beyond what position and lexical controls explain, and distinguish correct from incorrect solution endings. Building on this finding, we propose ROM (Real-time Overthinking Mitigation), which uses a lightweight streaming detector (0.07--0.25\% of backbone parameters) to monitor a frozen LRM and intervene at well-formed reasoning boundaries without answer extraction or probe decoding. Counterfactual Self-Correction (CSC) augments detector training with wrong$\rightarrow$correct trajectories to preserve useful self-correction. Heads are trained separately for each backbone using shared MATH500 supervision and transfer zero-shot to four unseen benchmarks. Evaluated against ten baselines across five backbones from three families and five benchmarks, ROM$_{\text{CSC}}$ reduces response length by 28--77\% (45\% on average), with significant reductions in all 25 settings and accuracy matching or exceeding vanilla decoding in 22. Measurements on two backbones further show 20--70\% lower end-to-end latency with less than 0.5\% detector overhead per decoding step. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[ECG-Reasoning-Benchmark: A Benchmark for Evaluating Clinical Reasoning Capabilities in ECG Interpretation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.14326)**  `arXiv:2603.14326`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Jungwoo Oh, Hyunseung Chung, Junhee Lee, Min-Gyu Kim, Hangyul Yoon, Ki Seong Lee, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) show promising performance in automated electrocardiogram interpretation, it remains unclear whether they perform actual step-by-step reasoning or just rely on superficial visual cues. To investigate this, we introduce ECG-Reasoning-Benchmark, a novel multi-turn evaluation framework comprising over 6,400 samples to systematically assess step- by-step reasoning across 17 core ECG diagnoses. Our comprehensive evaluation of state-of-the-art models reveals a systematic shortfall in executing multi-step logical deduction. Although models possess the medical knowledge to retrieve clinical criteria for a diagnosis, they exhibit consistently low success rates in maintaining a complete reasoning chain, primarily failing to ground the corresponding ECG findings to the actual visual evidence in the ECG signal. These results demonstrate that current MLLMs bypass actual visual interpretation, highlighting a limitation in their visual grounding capabilities and motivating robust, reasoning-centric medical AI. The code and data are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Escaping Path Mirages in Offline Goal-Conditioned Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40130)**  `arXiv:2610.40130`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Junhyeon Bae, Jaebak Hwang, Gwanwoo Choi, Minung Kim, Seungyul Han_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Offline goal-conditioned reinforcement learning remains challenging in stochastic long-horizon settings, where compounding value estimation errors hinder reliable goal reaching. While prior methods have sought to address this challenge through various approaches, a key challenge remains path mirage, where agents overcommit to spuriously successful trajectories in offline datasets induced by stochasticity, often leading to failure on difficult tasks. To address this issue, we propose Branch-aware Graph Planning (BGP), which captures stochastic branching structures by identifying high-variability points as graph nodes and designing goal-conditioned edges with segmented learning to avoid unreliable branches and reduce unnecessary stochasticity along paths. As a result, BGP enables reliable goal reaching even in highly stochastic long-horizon environments, and experiments on diverse OGBench tasks show that it substantially outperforms prior state-of-the-art offline goal-conditioned RL methods. Our implementation is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Turing LLM Post-Training: Calibrated Distillation and Efficient Dynamic-MoE Execution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39876)**  `arXiv:2610.39876`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Yue He, Jiahui Hu, Shaman Tang, Hua Zhou, Qiman Wu, Menglin Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We study post-training for dynamically routed mixture-of-experts (MoE) models, focusing on integrating independently optimized specialists while keeping consolidation efficient. Our approach combines calibrated multi-teacher on-policy distillation (C-MOPD), which matches each selected teacher on a local Top-$K$ support with teacher-wise loss normalization and clipping, with an execution path that uses post-capacity bounded-width dispatch, final-route replay, and asynchronous rollout--training overlap. On Turing-20B-A2B, the selected model achieves a four-domain normalized integration score of 0.9988 and retains at least 90.16\% of the measured specialist gain in every domain. At matched $K=32$, the full calibration objective scores 0.9962, compared with 0.9724 without calibration and 0.9834 without clipping. At a matched 8K response cap, the model exceeds both shared SFT and Qwen3-8B on all four reasoning-enabled mathematics and code benchmarks (unweighted mean: 85.92, versus 80.33 for Qwen3-8B and 74.21 for SFT), and improves over SFT on ten of twelve broader evaluation metrics. In a PPU-based profile, the combined execution configuration reduces consolidation step time from 398.7 to 139.3 seconds, an observed 2.86$\times$ speedup over serial execution. These results demonstrate strong specialist integration alongside more efficient consolidation, while reflecting the complete training and execution framework rather than any single component.
  </details>

- **[RetroChessBench: A Benchmark for Reconstructing and Proving Chess Histories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39341)**  `arXiv:2610.39341`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Tidor-Vlad Pricope_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Frontier language models such as GPT 6 Astra have contributed to research mathematics, demonstrating substantial reasoning capabilities. Yet serious reasoning failures persist even in tasks governed by explicit rules. We demonstrate this in retrograde chess which is a playground for pure reasoning, where solvers reconstruct legal histories and distinguish possible events from forced ones. We introduce RetroChessBench, a 12-task benchmark covering exact-length constructions, unique endings, and exhaustive answer sets, with references, rubrics, and a terminal-reward environment. In two trials per task and condition, GPT 6 Astra High fails to produce the required final answer in 22/24 attempts without tools and 18/24 with code execution; mean terminal RL rewards are 14.75% and 27.19%, respectively. The examples identify faulty deductions: failed responses reject legal histories and treat temporary obstructions as permanent. Online retrograde archives often provide puzzles and final move sequences without detailed derivations. We hypothesize that limited exposure to these missing explanations in web-derived training data contributes to the observed reasoning failures. This questions how far frontier mathematical reasoning really generalizes beyond familiar solution patterns. Claims of general intelligence require evidence that models can transfer learnt principles to areas with few worked examples.
  </details>

- **[Learning to Bound: Monotonic, Calibrated Neural Upper Bounds for MPE Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39305)**  `arXiv:2610.39305`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Akshay Vyas, Hailiang Dong, Shivvrat Arya, Brij Malhotra, Tahrima Rahman, Vibhav Gogate, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Probabilistic graphical models (PGMs) compactly encode structured joint distributions, but Most Probable Explanation (MPE), which finds the highest-probability assignment to unobserved variables, remains computationally intractable in large, densely structured models. Anytime systematic search, such as branch-and-bound (B&B), is a popular approach, but its efficiency depends on tight admissible upper bounds that are often prohibitively expensive to compute using classical methods like Mini-Bucket Elimination (MBE). We introduce Learning to Bound (L2B), a data-driven framework that amortizes bound computation cost by training a neural predictor. L2B targets two key properties required for effective search bounds: admissibility, which is achieved via statistical calibration techniques, and progressive tightening as evidence accumulates, which is enforced through structural architectural constraints that ensure monotonicity by construction. Across challenging benchmarks, L2B delivers tighter, monotonic bounds at orders-of-magnitude lower cost on every PGM family, and yields measurable improvements when integrated with branch-and-bound on high-treewidth instances.
  </details>

- **[From Local Recovery to Unified Generation: Discovering Triple Set in Knowledge Graphs via Observation-Anchored Latent Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39114)**  `arXiv:2610.39114`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Jiaqi Wang, Yisen Gao, Huayang Shi, Junhao Hou, Wengen Li, Jihong Guan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Triple set prediction (TSP) is to recover missing relational triples in a knowledge graph by predicting the triple set instead of isolated links. Existing approaches predominantly rely on the subgraph-based completion paradigm, where the original graph is first partitioned into local subgraphs which are then independently completed. Such a paradigm suffers from fragmented structural reasoning, limited cross subgraph dependency modeling, and missing predictions across subgraphs. This paper proposes LatentDiff, a novel unified latent generative framework for TSP, which reformulates knowledge graph completion as latent space graph generation. LatentDiff first learns a compact latent representation using a relation-aware residual-quantized knowledge graph autoencoder, which encodes the whole graph into relation-aware latent embeddings, quantizes them with chunked residual codebooks, and reconstructs triples through a factorized decoder. On top of this latent space, we introduce an observation-anchored latent graph diffusion model, where the target latent graph representation is perturbed toward the observed latent. The reverse process recovers missing triples through progressive latent denoising. Experiments on three benchmark datasets show that LatentDiff outperforms the SOTA in terms of F_{tsp} by 5.4 percentage points on average. Code:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Generating Critic-Guided Synthetic Experience in Latent World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39100)**  `arXiv:2610.39100`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Jorge de Freitas, Christos Ziakas, Alessandra Russo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Learning from offline datasets is limited in scalability, as collecting new experience is often costly or unsafe in the real world. World models offer an alternative way to generate new experience through a learned dynamics model, yet learning policies in latent space is limited due to compounding errors in imagined rollouts. To this end, we propose using the world model as a local transition generator, leveraging critic guidance to choose actions inside that model to generate synthetic experiences. We study action refinement methodologies, introducing Critic-Guided Synthetic Experience (CGSE) generation in world models. Specifically, CGSE augments an existing offline dataset by sampling candidate actions using an offline policy and refining each to maximize a first-order approximation of the $Q$-function within a trust region. We then use a frozen latent world model to imagine the future state given each updated action, and use the critic to choose the best one, appending this synthetic transition to the replay buffer. Our method requires no additional real-environment interaction or test-time planner, as it generates synthetic experience offline using the world model. On three OGBench manipulation tasks, CGSE improves an offline policy by a relative improvement of $7.1\%$ on the average success rate.
  </details>

- **[Rethinking GFlowNet Training: Negative Reinforce Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38994)**  `arXiv:2610.38994`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Pedro Dall'Antonia, Daniel Csillag, Diego Mesquita_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Generative Flow Networks (GFlowNets) are models that learn to sample discrete objects proportional to a target reward. They are trained by minimizing balance errors over sampled trajectories, which inherently ties correctness to exploration: if a region is not visited, balance cannot be enforced there. Adaptive exploration is therefore crucial, yet standard training relies on a semi-gradient update that treats the sampling distribution as fixed at each optimization step. On the other hand, the full gradient reveals a second mechanism that reduces the observed errors in an anti-exploratory manner. In this paper, we establish a connection between the full gradient and min-min dynamics between the GFlowNet and a decoupled sampler, showing that the sampler reduces observed violations by avoiding high-error regions. This insight motivates a bridge between two-player games and single-policy dynamics, which underpins the design of our proposed method, Negative Reinforce Dynamics (NRD). By tying the parameters of an adversarial game together, we recover a single-policy dynamic that extends exploration while maintaining correctness. In standard benchmarks, our method outperforms existing approaches in both convergence speed and exploration efficiency, without training a separate teacher or auxiliary exploration policy.
  </details>

- **[Benchmarking Graph Neural Networks for AC Power Flow: The Role of Inductive Biases and Physical Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38985)**  `arXiv:2610.38985`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Federico Clerici, Ferran Bohigas-Daranas, Hamid Latif-Martínez, Eduardo Prieto-Araujo, Oriol Gomis-Bellmunt, Pere Barlet-Ros_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Fast and accurate models for AC power flow (ACPF) are increasingly important as modern electrical grids require more frequent system analyses. Graph neural networks (GNNs) naturally exploit electrical grid topology, yet the effects of architectural design choices on predictive performance remain poorly understood given differences in graph representations and evaluation settings across studies.We present a unified benchmark of representative GNN architectures for ACPF prediction under a common experimental framework. All models use identical preprocessing and training procedures and comparable parameter budgets, isolating the impact of message-passing mechanisms and inductive biases. We also analyze errors by distance from the slack bus and systematically ablate key design components. We evaluate predictive accuracy and physical consistency on the IEEE 14-bus and IEEE 118-bus systems.Results indicate that anisotropic message-passing architectures offer the most favorable trade-off between predictive accuracy and physical consistency under the studied parameter and topology constraints. Explicit edge features encoding line impedances and global context aggregation are particularly important for representing electrical interactions and system-wide dependencies, especially for voltage-angle predictions on the topologically bottlenecked IEEE-118 grid. Under a budget with approximately fixed parameters, two or three message-passing layers generally provide the best balance between depth and representation width, while additional depth offers no consistent improvement. Ablations also show that standard residual and root connections are not necessarily beneficial in shallow models, and that aggregation operators involve trade-offs between statistical accuracy and physical consistency. These results provide practical guidelines for designing graph-based power system models.
  </details>

- **[Latent Decision Flow Rewards for Clinical Guideline Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38709)**  `arXiv:2610.38709`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Gaurav Bhatt, Marinka Zitnik_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Clinical practice guidelines encode evidence-based recommendations as conditional decision flows, which makes them a natural source of process supervision for training reasoning models. Two properties of clinical reasoning make this supervision difficult to use. A decision flow consistent with a guideline does not by itself determine the correct answer for a given patient, which we call the path--answer gap, and several valid decision flows can support the same clinical decision, which we call path multiplicity. Current training approaches address parts of this problem. Answer rewards supervise the final decision, and process rewards computed against a reference flow supervise one valid decision flow per case. Supervised fine-tuning on annotated flows teaches guideline-consistent reasoning but constrains the model to one trajectory per case and can reduce answer accuracy. We introduce GUIDER, a method that jointly optimizes a decision flow controller and a reasoning policy. The controller learns to generate plausible decision flows from guideline-derived supervision, and the policy learns to generate a decision flow and a final answer for each patient case. GUIDER computes the process reward for the policy over several controller-generated flows, which accounts for path multiplicity, and gates this reward on answer correctness, which addresses the path--answer gap. Joint training allows the process supervision to evolve with the policy. On MedGUIDE, GUIDER with Qwen3-14B achieves 82.5 weighted accuracy and 73.5 guideline adherence, 3.3 and 3.1 points above the strongest clinical reinforcement learning baseline. With Qwen3-14B and Gemma4-12B backbones, GUIDER also improves over CRPO, MRPO, and FaithMed on the out-of-domain benchmarks AMEGA-LLM, RealMedQA, and CancerGUIDE.
  </details>

- **[MUSE: Multi-Level Memory with Selective Runtime Injection for Scientific Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38332)**  `arXiv:2610.38332`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Haoran Jin, Defu Lian, Xiting Wang, Hong Xie_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Historical experience can guide scientific coding at multiple levels, but task-level retrieval may miss implementation lessons that become relevant during execution. We introduce MUSE, a memory management framework combining multi-level memory with selective runtime injection for scientific coding agents. MUSE builds global, approach-level, and implementation-level memory from historical trajectories. At initialization, the agent receives global memory and problem-matched approach-level memory. During execution, current interactions are used to retrieve implementation-level memory. An LLM gate selects relevant experience and generates specific review advice, or skips injection. Across four benchmarks spanning numerical problem solving, kernel optimization, and scientific software repair, MUSE outperforms retrieval and memory baselines on all evaluation metrics. Ablations support the multi-level design and gated injection, while transfer experiments show that memory remains useful across kernel benchmarks. These results show that multi-level memory and selective runtime injection enable effective reuse of experience in scientific coding. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Towards Generalizing Pretrained All-Atom Representations to Unified Binder Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37994)**  `arXiv:2610.37994`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Maojiang Su, Jiale Chen, Qijie Zhu, Shang Wu, Jiayi Wang, Jihai Zhao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  PairFormer-based all-atom models demonstrate strong capabilities in biomolecular binder design. However, existing methods often use all-atom representations for modality-specific binder design, such as protein and peptide design. In contrast, recent geometric latent diffusion models pursue unified binder design by representing molecular blocks from different modalities in a shared latent space. These complementary strengths motivate us to investigate how to generalize the use of pretrained all-atom representations to unified binder design. We introduce PairFuse, a parameter-efficient method that enables this generalization through representation fusion. Specifically, we use lightweight layerwise adapters to integrate pretrained all-atom representations into an independently pretrained geometric latent diffusion model. Our adapters integrate pretrained all-atom representations through two mechanisms: enriching the diffusion model's molecular block features and incorporating pairwise structural information into the geometric diffusion model's denoiser. Theoretically, we prove that the augmented denoiser preserves geometry and pairwise attention fusion provides additional attention expressivity beyond feature-only fusion. Experimentally, across antibody, peptide, and small molecule binder-design benchmarks, PairFuse achieves stronger performance than the pretrained diffusion model and existing representation-fusion baselines.
  </details>

- **[Predicate Neuron: Differentiable Quantization of Predicate Relations in Gradient-based Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37727)**  `arXiv:2610.37727`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Ioannis Konstantoulas, Iliana Loi, Kyriakos Sgarbas_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In machine learning, symbolic reasoning approaches often rely on static architectures that hinder integration into deep neural networks and gradient-based optimization. To address this, we introduce the Native Predicate Neuron, a differentiable logic-reasoning neural module that enriches standard architectures with symbolic reasoning capabilities via first-order logic quantization. The proposed neuron models relational logic by learning to alternate between operations using a single trainable parameter. It features a tailored gradient mechanism with a dual-nature formulation: executing hard logical operations during training while maintaining differentiability for backpropagation. By quantizing both its input and choice state, the Predicate Neuron approximates continuous and non-continuous functions, effectively resolving discontinuities that standard deep learning architectures struggle with. We evaluate the Predicate Neuron across Fully-Connected, Convolutional, Recurrent, and Transformer architectures on benchmarks like MNIST and Wikitext. Results show that incorporating Predicate Neurons accelerates convergence, boosts accuracy, and lowers loss in general, with particularly strong improvements in tasks requiring monotonic reasoning or handling discontinuities. Overall, the Predicate Neuron natively integrates into standard neural networks to enable fuzzy logic operations and is available in Keras and PyTorch.
  </details>

- **[Proposing Better Rollouts for On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36477)**  `arXiv:2610.36477`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Sirui Chen, Yunzhe Qi, Haobo Xu, Jiaru Zou, Mengting Ai, Zihao Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  On-policy distillation (OPD) improves language-model post-training by letting the student learn from its own sampled trajectories under teacher supervision, aligning the training process with inference-time behavior. However, the rollout policy in OPD is usually treated as fixed: each round samples directly from the current student. We show that this choice can create a compounding teacher--student mismatch, because it requires the teacher to provide effective supervision on whichever trajectories the student samples, including ones less reachable by the teacher itself. On such trajectories, teacher supervision can become noisy or even misleading, leading to undesirable student patterns such as repetition. Moreover, since the updated student in turn defines the rollout policy of subsequent rounds, these patterns persist and potentially amplify over training. This failure mode suggests that improving OPD requires treating the rollout policy itself as an optimization target, rather than only modifying the distillation objective. We propose Mixture Proposal On-Policy Distillation (MPOPD), which derives a student-anchored, teacher-guided rollout distribution from a local trust-region objective based on relative teacher support. The resulting target is a geometric mixture of the student and teacher policies, and we introduce a proposal policy that approximates this mixture, enabling rollout generation in a single forward pass. Experiments on 16 math, knowledge, and medical reasoning benchmarks show consistent gains over OPD and recent variants. On math reasoning, our method improves average accuracy by 6.91 over OPD on Qwen3-4B-Base while reducing average generation length by 41%, suggesting that rollout policy design provides an effective way to improve OPD.
  </details>

- **[Value-Rectified Distillation for Flow-based Offline Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29075)**  `arXiv:2610.29075`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Ke Jiang, Wen Jiang, Yoshinobu Kawahara, Xiaoyang Tan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We aim to address the problem of Out-of-Distribution (OOD) Mode Averaging, a critical problem that emerges when distilling Flow Matching (FM) based behavior policies in offline Reinforcement Learning (RL): When learning over multi-modal datasets, standard distillation inadvertently forces the policy to interpolate across different behavioral modes, causing the generated actions to collapse into invalid, OOD regions. To resolve this, we propose Value-Rectified Distillation (VRD), a novel framework that reformulates offline policy distillation as a value-guided generative trajectory alignment problem. By employing a dynamic coupling mechanism, VRD actively rectifies flow dynamics to explicitly decouple distinct behavioral modes, cleanly isolating high-value actions. Specifically, we instantiate VRD through two complementary algorithms: VRD-Q, which utilizes value-quantile behavior distillation to statistically filter out low-value behaviors, and VRD-R, which reorganizes the latent space to geometrically isolate high-value generative trajectories from the FM-based behavior policy. Extensive empirical evaluations demonstrate that VRD improves existing generative baselines across diverse continuous control and planning tasks on the D4RL, OGBench, and Minari benchmarks, especially over those multi-modal, non-expert datasets.
  </details>

- **[A Geometric Approach to Soft Actor-Critic with Zonotopes for Locomotion Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12113)**  `arXiv:2610.12113`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Panagiotis Roditis, Panagiotis P. Filntisis, Petros Maragos_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Actor--critic methods in reinforcement learning control overestimation bias by taking the minimum of two critics. This uses the same aggregation rule everywhere, regardless of how the critics disagree. We propose Geometric Zonotope Soft Actor-Critic (GeZo-SAC), which uses auxiliary geometric representations to adapt critic pessimism to the state and action. Alongside its scalar value, each critic predicts a set of generators defining a zonotope. Probing this zonotope along sampled directions provides a geometric width, subtracted from each critic value as a pessimistic offset, and a measure of disagreement between the two critics, aggregated with log-sum-exp as a smooth approximation to the tropical (max-plus) addition. This disagreement controls how the critics are combined, moving from a width-weighted average toward the usual minimum as disagreement increases. At inference, the deployed policy is an unmodified SAC actor, since the generators are used only on the critic side during training. Across four MuJoCo-v5 locomotion benchmarks and six off-policy baselines, GeZo-SAC achieves the highest mean return on Ant-v5 and Hopper-v5 and remains competitive with other methods on the remaining tasks. Our analysis further shows that GeZo-SAC achieves the lowest average actuator work and action effort per metre among the evaluated methods, while maintaining near-zero measured overestimation frequency across all four environments.
  </details>

- **[Interpretable Synthetic Medical Tabular Data Generation for Clinical Decision Support Using Fuzzy Cognitive Maps](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00391)**  `arXiv:2610.00391`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Michael Vasilakakis, Dimitris K. Iakovidis_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Synthetic medical tabular data generation has become essential for developing and validating computer-based medical systems (CBMSs) when real clinical data is restricted due to privacy, ethical, or data availability limitations. Existing probabilistic and deep generative models often lack interpretability and fail to preserve clinically meaningful dependencies, limiting their suitability for safety-critical applications. This paper proposes a novel application of Fuzzy Cognitive Maps (FCMs) in a framework for synthetic medical tabular data generation with explicit causality and privacy preservation. Clinical features are described using linguistically interpretable fuzzy sets, and inter-feature dependencies are encoded as FCM edge weights computed from fuzzy set intersections. Synthetic patient records are generated by propagating randomly initialized linguistic activation vectors through the FCM until convergence, followed by defuzzification to produce clinically coherent numerical values. The approach natively handles mixed data types, and domain constraints common in health records. Experimental evaluation on UCI medical benchmark datasets demonstrates competitive performance under a Train-on-Synthetic-Test-on-Real (TSTR) protocol. The proposed method achieves accuracy of up to 0.81 and AUROC of up to 0.90 on the Heart Disease dataset, matching or exceeding TVAE and Gaussian Copula baselines while running exclusively on CPU. Fidelity metrics including KS Complement (up to 0.91) and Correlation Similarity (up to 0.95) confirm strong statistical coherence, and DCR Baseline Protection scores consistently exceed those of TVAE, confirming adequate privacy guarantees. These results demonstrate that causally grounded, interpretable fuzzy modeling offers a computationally efficient and transparent alternative to deep generative models for trustworthy synthetic data generation in CBMSs.
  </details>

- **[Learning Multimodal One-step Flow Policy via Value-weighted Optimal Transport](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15883)**  `arXiv:2609.15883`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Jaehun Shon, Jinha Choi, Jongwook Jeon, Jongmin Lee_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Offline reinforcement learning aims to learn a policy solely from fixed datasets, which often contain multimodal action distributions. Flow policies can naturally represent such multimodal behaviors, but learning an efficient one-step flow policy remains challenging: standard value guidance often leads to mode collapse or exploits overestimation bias in out-of-distribution regions. To address this, we introduce One-step Flow policy via Optimal Transport (OptiFlow), a framework for one-step flow policy learning as a structured sample-allocation problem. OptiFlow jointly trains a value-aware reference flow policy and an efficient one-step policy, coupling their action samples through state-wise entropic optimal transport. For each state, critic-estimated values define the priority of distillation target actions, while the action-distance cost ensures geometrically compatible pairings. By avoiding direct critic maximization, our transport-guided approach enables in-distribution exploitation by anchoring the one-step policy to high-value, dataset-supported modes without the risk of out-of-distribution divergence. Experimental results demonstrate that OptiFlow effectively captures optimal multimodal behaviors and achieves strong performance across diverse offline RL benchmarks. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[OutageDiT: A Foundation Model for Power Outage Forecasting and Scenario Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01896)**  `arXiv:2609.01896`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Yunqin Zhu, Feng Qiu, Yao Xie_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Power-outage planning requires scenarios before an event occurs. These scenarios must represent uncertainty in magnitude, timing, and duration while preserving temporal dependence. However, severe events are rare, and data from any single region contain few examples of extreme outage and restoration patterns. To address this challenge, we introduce OutageDiT, a foundation model for generating seven-day outage trajectories at 15-minute resolution, trained on outage and weather records across the United States. Specifically, a condition encoder processes historical context and known future covariates once per forecast, and a shallow flow-matching decoder reuses the resulting horizon-aligned states to sample complete outage trajectories. This enables point forecasting, uncertainty quantification, and conditional scenario generation within one deep generative model. Across outage forecasting benchmarks, OutageDiT improves forecast accuracy and scenario quality over strong baselines and supports zero-shot transfer to held-out regions. Together, these results position conditional outage scenario generation as a bridge from outage forecasting to operational planning under uncertainty.
  </details>

- **[Test-Time Gradient Guidance of Flow Policies in Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.11087)**  `arXiv:2606.11087`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Zhiyuan Zhou, Andy Peng, Charles Xu, Qiyang Li, Jost Tobias Springenberg, Kevin Frans, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Expressive continuous control policies, such as diffusion and flow models, form the backbone of recent advances in scaling imitation learning for simulated and real robot control. While they are known to scale stably in the supervised imitation learning setting, incorporating them into reinforcement learning (RL) pipelines for policy improvement has proven more difficult. It often requires specialized training objectives or backpropagating through denoising processes, which cause well-known issues with stability and affect scalability. In this paper we study the question of whether simple policy improvement schemes at test time alone, leaving stable supervised policy training intact, can be a competitive alternative which sidesteps these issues. To this end, we propose QGF (Q-Guided Flow), an RL algorithm that performs policy optimization entirely at test time. QGF works by pre-training both a reference flow policy (via a standard behavioral cloning objective) and a value function critic and, at test time, using the value gradient to guide the reference policy to generate higher-value actions without any additional policy learning. Empirically, QGF outperforms prior test-time RL methods on single-task and goal-conditioned offline RL benchmarks with high-dimensional action spaces, and is competitive with state-of-the-art training-time algorithms while being much cheaper to run. Moreover, it exhibits favorable scaling with model size by avoiding the instability of actor-critic training, offering a practical and effective alternative RL algorithm with expressive policies.
  </details>

- **[MahaVar: OOD Detection via Class-wise Mahalanobis Distance Variance under Neural Collapse](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.14413)**  `arXiv:2605.14413`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Donghwan Kim, Hyunsoo Yoon_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Out-of-distribution (OOD) detection is a critical component for ensuring the reliability of deep neural networks in safety-critical applications. In this work, we present a key empirical observation: for in-distribution (ID) samples, class-wise Mahalanobis distances exhibit a pronounced sharp minimum structure, where the distance to the nearest class is small while distances to all other classes remain large, resulting in high variance across classes. In contrast, OOD samples tend to exhibit a less pronounced sharp minimum structure, producing comparatively lower variance across classes. We further provide a theoretical analysis grounding this observation in Neural Collapse geometry: under relaxed Neural Collapse assumptions on within-class compactness and inter-class separation, ID samples are shown to structurally exhibit high class-wise distance variance, offering a theoretical basis for its use as an OOD score. Motivated by this observation and its theoretical backing, we propose MahaVar, a simple and effective post-hoc OOD detector that augments the Mahalanobis distance with a class-wise distance variance term. Following the OpenOOD v1.5 benchmark protocol, MahaVar achieves state-of-the-art performance on CIFAR-100 and ImageNet, with consistent improvements in both AUROC and FPR@95 over existing Mahalanobis-based methods across all benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[DUET: Optimize Token-Budget Allocation for Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08441)**  `arXiv:2605.08441`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Haoyu Hu, Xuandong Zhao, Xuhai "Orson'' Xu, Nori Jacoby_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) generates hundreds of thousands of tokens per training step, with rollout generation dominating the computational cost. The overall token budget can be controlled along two main dimensions: (i) deciding which prompts to allocate rollouts to, and (ii) deciding how long each rollout should be. Prior work has generally controlled only one of these dimensions at a time. We show that jointly tuning both decisions under a shared compute budget improves both reasoning quality and wall-clock training time. We instantiate this view as \textbf{DU}al-controlled tok\textbf{E}n alloca\textbf{T}ion (DUET), a computationally efficient layer over GRPO that uses a lightweight pre-rollout surrogate of prompt informativeness to set how many rollouts each prompt receives, and a marker-gated abort rule with importance reweighting to set when to stop them. On Qwen3-1.7B trained on MATH, DUET outperforms full-budget GRPO and the other three budget-aware baseline methods. DUET's advantage further generalizes to other benchmarks across math and coding, and is on par with the best baseline on the scientific Q\&A domain, while also achieving a $1.62\times$ wall-clock speedup. More notably, using only 50\% of the token budget, DUET still outperforms all baseline methods at their full budget, achieving an even higher $2.51\times$ speedup over full-budget GRPO. We verify the high performance of DUET on other backbone LLMs, including Qwen3-4B to 14B and Llama-3.2-3B-Instruct. Notably, the gap between DUET and the strongest baseline \emph{widens} as the budget tightens, contrary to the usual pattern in which efficient methods trade off quality as compute decreases. More broadly, these results suggest that DUET budget-aware control strategies are valuable not only for accelerating training, but also for improving the quality of the learning signal.
  </details>

- **[Validity-Calibrated Reasoning Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.04078)**  `arXiv:2605.04078`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Khouloud Saadi, Di Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reasoning distillation aims to transfer multi-step reasoning capabilities from large language models to smaller, efficient ones. While recent methods have shown promising gains, they typically rely on static teacher-student hierarchies and frame distillation as trajectory imitation. This is misaligned with the structure of reasoning, where intermediate steps are locally under-specified: global correctness constrains the final answer, but does not uniquely determine each intermediate move. We propose validity-calibrated reasoning distillation, a framework that treats reasoning distillation as a problem of local learning-signal allocation rather than path alignment. Instead of enforcing token-level imitation, we compare the student's and teacher's proposed next-step actions under the same prefix and use their relative local validity to modulate the strength of the distillation update. This yields a dynamic, context-dependent supervision that preserves the teacher's structural guidance while adapting update strength to local reasoning quality. Across math, code, and instruction-following benchmarks, our method outperforms strong distillation baselines. These results indicate that effective LLM reasoning distillation is governed not by rigid trajectory imitation, but by locally calibrated allocation of learning signal.
  </details>

- **[Multi-Agent LLMs for Adaptive Acquisition in Bayesian Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.28959)**  `arXiv:2603.28959`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Mohammadsina Almasi, Andrea Carbonati, Hadis Anahideh_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The exploration-exploitation trade-off is central to sequential decision-making and black-box optimization, yet how Large Language Models (LLMs) reason about and manage this trade-off remains poorly understood. Unlike Bayesian Optimization, where exploration and exploitation are explicitly encoded through acquisition functions, LLM-based optimization relies on implicit, prompt-based reasoning over historical evaluations, making search behavior difficult to analyze or control. In this work, we present a metric-level study of LLM-mediated search policy learning, studying how LLMs construct and adapt exploration-exploitation strategies under multiple operational definitions of exploration, including informativeness, diversity, and representativeness. We show that single-agent LLM approaches, which jointly perform strategy selection and candidate generation within a single prompt, suffer from cognitive overload, leading to unstable search dynamics and premature convergence. To address this limitation, we propose a multi-agent framework that decomposes exploration-exploitation control into strategic policy mediation and tactical candidate generation. A strategy agent assigns interpretable weights to multiple search criteria, while a generation agent produces candidates conditioned on the resulting search policy defined as weights. This decomposition renders exploration-exploitation decisions explicit, observable, and adjustable. Empirical results across various continuous optimization benchmarks indicate that separating strategic control from candidate generation substantially improves the effectiveness of LLM-mediated search.
  </details>

- **[Train at Moving Edge: Online-Verified Prompt Selection for Efficient RL Training of Large Reasoning Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.25184)**  `arXiv:2603.25184`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Jiahao Wu, Ning Lu, Shengcai Liu, Kun Wang, Yanting Yang, Baijiong Lin, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning (RL) has become essential for post-training large language models (LLMs) in reasoning tasks. While scaling rollouts can stabilize training and enhance performance, the computational overhead is a critical issue. In algorithms like GRPO, multiple rollouts per prompt incur prohibitive costs, as a large portion of prompts provide negligible gradients and are thus of low utility. To address this problem, we investigate how to select high-utility prompts before the rollout phase. Our experimental analysis reveals that sample utility is non-uniform and evolving: the strongest learning signals concentrate at the ``learning edge", the intersection of intermediate difficulty and high uncertainty, which shifts as training proceeds. Motivated by this, we propose HIVE (History-Informed and online-VErified prompt selection), a dual-stage framework for data-efficient RL. HIVE utilizes historical reward trajectories for coarse selection and employs prompt entropy as a real-time proxy to prune instances with stale utility. By evaluating HIVE across multiple math reasoning benchmarks and models, we show that HIVE yields significant rollout efficiency without compromising performance.
  </details>

- **[Towards Batch-to-Streaming Deep Reinforcement Learning for Continuous Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.08588)**  `arXiv:2603.08588`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Riccardo De Monte, Matteo Cederle, Gian Antonio Susto_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  State-of-the-art deep reinforcement learning (RL) methods have achieved remarkable performance in continuous control tasks, yet their computational complexity is often incompatible with the constraints of resource-limited hardware, due to their reliance on replay buffers, batch updates, and target networks. The emerging paradigm of streaming deep RL addresses this limitation through purely online updates, achieving strong empirical performance on standard benchmarks. In this work, we propose two novel streaming deep RL algorithms, Streaming Soft Actor-Critic (S2AC) and Streaming Deterministic Actor-Critic (SDAC), explicitly designed to be compatible with state-of-the-art batch RL methods, making them particularly suitable for on-device finetuning applications such as Sim2Real transfer. Both algorithms achieve performance comparable to state-of-the-art streaming baselines on standard benchmarks without requiring tedious per-environment hyperparameter tuning. We further investigate the batch-to-streaming transition, showing that a naive transition does not guarantee preservation of pre-trained policy performance, and propose a principled approach to address this challenge.
  </details>

- **[Why Does Counterintuitive Likelihood Behavior Rarely Emerge in Tabular Anomaly Detection?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.09593)**  `arXiv:2602.09593`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Donghwan Kim, Junghun Phee, Hyunsoo Yoon_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deep generative models with tractable likelihoods, exemplified by normalizing flows, offer an effective basis for anomaly detection through likelihood-based scoring. We demonstrate that, unlike in the image domain where deep generative models frequently assign higher likelihoods to anomalous data, such counterintuitive behavior occurs rarely in tabular settings. We first introduce a domain-agnostic formulation that enables consistent detection and evaluation of the counterintuitive phenomenon, addressing the lack of a precise definition in prior work. Through extensive experiments on 47 tabular datasets benchmarked against 13 baseline models and on 10 CV/NLP embedding datasets from ADBench, we show that the phenomenon is consistently rare in general tabular data and that normalizing flow likelihood achieves competitive anomaly detection performance. We further provide theoretical and empirical analyses attributing this disparity to differences in dimensionality and feature correlation between tabular and image data. Therefore, our results indicate that likelihood-based detection with normalizing flows is a practical approach for tabular anomaly detection.
  </details>

- **[Mitigating the Likelihood Paradox in Flow-based OOD Detection: An Entropic Perspective](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.09581)**  `arXiv:2602.09581`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Donghwan Kim, Hyunsoo Yoon_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deep generative models that can tractably compute input likelihoods, including normalizing flows, often assign unexpectedly high likelihoods to out-of-distribution (OOD) inputs. We mitigate this paradox from an entropic perspective with Semantic Proportional Entropy Manipulation (SPEM), a training-free procedure that applies stronger perturbations to inputs less similar to an in-distribution (ID) memory bank. We derive theoretical lower bounds showing that this entropy gap increases the expected log-likelihood difference in favor of the ID, and experiments on standard benchmarks show that this manipulation realigns the likelihood ordering with human intuition. Strikingly, this realignment persists even when the flow is given only visually meaningless noise instead of the original input, and we analyze this phenomenon theoretically to characterize the conditions under which it occurs.
  </details>

- **[PepEVOLVE: Position-Aware Dynamic Peptide Optimization via Group-Relative Advantage](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.16912)**  `arXiv:2511.16912`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Trieu Nguyen, Hao-Wei Pang, Shasha Feng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Macrocyclic peptides are an emerging modality that combines biologics-like affinity with small-molecule-like developability, yet their vast combinatorial space and multi-parameter objectives make lead optimization slow and challenging. Prior generative approaches for peptide lead optimization (e.g., PepINVENT) require chemists to pre-specify mutable positions for optimization, choices that are not always known a priori, and rely on static pretraining and optimization algorithms that limit the model's ability to generalize and effectively optimize peptide sequences. We introduce PepEVOLVE, a position-aware, dynamic framework that learns both where to edit and how to dynamically optimize peptides for multi-parameter improvement. PepEVOLVE (i) augments pretraining with dynamic masking and CHUCKLES shifting to improve generalization, (ii) uses a context-free multi-armed-bandit router that discovers high-reward residues, and (iii) couples a novel evolving optimization algorithm with group-relative advantage to stabilize reinforcement updates. During in silico evaluations, the router policy reliably learns and concentrates probability on chemically tractable sites that influence the desired peptide's properties. On a therapeutically motivated Rev-binding macrocycle benchmark, PepEVOLVE outperformed PepINVENT by reaching higher averaged permeability-weighted optimization scores of approximately 0.8 vs. 0.6, achieving best candidates with score of 0.97 (vs. 0.89), and converging in fewer steps under the task of optimizing permeability and lipophilicity with structure constraints. Overall, PepEVOLVE offers a practical, reproducible path to linear and macrocyclic peptide lead optimization when optimal edit sites are unknown, enabling more efficient chemical space exploration and improving design quality across multiple objectives to accelerate the design cycles.
  </details>

- **[world2vec: Mechanistic world models through latent dynamics learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40637)**  `arXiv:2610.40637`  `cs.LG`  
  _Tobias Schmidt, Rodrigo Gonzalez Laiz, Krisztián Szuppinger, Steffen Schneider_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  World models can achieve strong control performance without faithfully capturing the dynamics of their environment, limiting their potential for mechanistic interpretability and scientific discovery. Here, we introduce world2vec to learn mechanistic world models without reward information directly from observations and actions. World2vec learns latent dynamics that faithfully reproduce the true system over long prediction horizons, with at least one order of magnitude fewer parameters than DreamerV3, TD-MPC2 and LeWM. This enables state of the art performance in controlling continuous environments from pixels and accurate system identification across a benchmark of 71 attractors, demonstrating how world models are amenable to scientific discovery.
  </details>

- **[Reinforcement learning for control of partial differential equations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40449)**  `arXiv:2610.40449`  `cs.LG`  
  _Nicolò Botteghi, Matteo Tomasetto, Andrea Manzoni_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Controlling dynamical systems governed by partial differential equations (PDEs) remains a challenging task in scientific computing, owing to high-dimensional state spaces, distributed actuation, nonlinear dynamics, and stringent real-time constraints. Reinforcement learning (RL) has emerged as a promising paradigm for synthesizing feedback controllers that adapt to varying operating conditions and can be deployed efficiently in real-time. This review provides a unified perspective on RL and PDE-constrained optimization, and surveys model-free, model-based, and multi-agent RL approaches to PDE control, with particular emphasis on sample efficiency, generalization across parametric variations, and scalability to high-dimensional problems. Two demonstrative examples, the stabilization of a chaotic system and a flow control problem, illustrate the performance and pitfalls of RL agents in practice. Finally, we discuss evaluation and benchmarking practices, current limitations, and open research directions toward reliable, data-efficient, and scalable RL for scientific computing.
  </details>

- **[DolphinRouting: Cluster-Level Binary Routing for Disaggregated LLM Inference Serving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40292)**  `arXiv:2610.40292`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yishuo Yuan, Shaoyu Yang, Chunrong Fang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Prefill-decode (PD) disaggregation isolates the two phases of large language model serving, but request-level path selection must account for prefill work, cache reuse, and decode demand. We present DolphinRouting, which learns this choice from end-to-end SLO feedback under a given P/D resource allocation. Requests are clustered by raw prompt length, cache-adjusted input lengths on both worker pools, and output-length information. Offline policy search learns one binary action per cluster. Online feature extraction, cluster assignment, and table lookup select local or remote prefill. We evaluate DolphinRouting on a real PD-disaggregated cluster using three public benchmarks, a mixed workload, and real data. Across the mixed workload and real data, DolphinRouting with $k$-means reduces P90 TTFT by 5.7--7.9\% and P90 TPOT by 2.6--10.1\% relative to Dynamo.
  </details>

- **[Observability-Aligned Evidential Masked Pretraining for Reliable VRU Motion Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39958)**  `arXiv:2610.39958`  `cs.LG`  
  _Shang Gao, Franz Götz-Hahn, Konrad Doll, Bernhard Sick_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vulnerable road user (VRU) prediction must operate under partial observability and distribution shift, so reliable uncertainty matters alongside accuracy. Masked pretraining is attractive in this setting because it naturally exposes the model to missing content during self-supervision, but existing time-series masked autoencoding methods are typically deterministic and do not constrain how confidence should change with observability. We introduce eCoMAE, an evidential masked pretraining method for multivariate time series that explicitly aligns predictive evidence with what is observed. eCoMAE combines block-wise time-step masking with a Normal-Inverse-Gamma decoder and three observability-aware mechanisms: uncertainty-weighted mean consistency on positions visible in both a masked view and a reference pass, evidence suppression on masked positions, and representation consistency across stochastic masks. On IMPTC benchmark for VRU intention prediction under leave-one-environment-out weather shift, eCoMAE improves the strongest baseline from 0.7971 to 0.8068 in OOD accuracy and from 0.0718 to 0.0648 in OOD AURC. Additional evaluation on pedestrian trajectory forecasting across IMPTC, Waymo, nuScenes, and inD further yields the best fine-tuning MSE on all four datasets. These results show that observability-aligned evidential pretraining improves reliability under shift and remains effective beyond the primary benchmark.
  </details>

- **[H$^2$R: Hierarchical Hint-Based Reward for Mathematical Reasoning via Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39934)**  `arXiv:2610.39934`  `cs.LG`  
  _He Tu, Siye Li, Hongyi Wen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards has shown great promise in enhancing mathematical reasoning in large language models. However, existing approaches provide limited intermediate supervision for complex, multi-step reasoning tasks. To bridge this gap, we propose \textbf{Hierarchical Hint-based Reward}, a framework that leverages the intrinsic sub-question structure of mathematical problems as structured intermediate conditioning signals during RLVR training. Unlike chain-of-thought prompting, these hierarchical hints serve as logically grounded intermediate milestones that effectively reduce the reasoning horizon toward the final answer. To validate this framework, we introduce \textbf{HiMath}, a benchmark of hierarchically structured mathematical problems. Across diverse LLMs, hierarchical hints consistently improve reasoning performance even without task-specific training. When integrated into RL training via H$^2$R, they boost Qwen2.5-Math-7B accuracy on HiMath from 30.4\% to 39.1\% using hierarchical hints, and up to 45.6\% with Oracle Hierarchical Hints. This pronounced improvement demonstrates that incorporating reliable intermediate checkpoints into reward design is essential for sustaining complex mathematical reasoning.
  </details>

- **[Optimal Contextual Pricing in Non-Stationary Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39803)**  `arXiv:2610.39803`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yiding Feng, Alireza Rezaeimoghadam_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We study contextual dynamic pricing in a non-stationary environment. In each round, a seller observes a buyer's context, posts a price, and receives only binary purchase feedback. The buyer's value is linear in the context and an unobserved feature vector that may change over time, subject to a known total variation budget $V$. We measure dynamic regret against the first-best benchmark that extracts each buyer's full value.For identical contexts, we develop a deterministic algorithm that combines binary-search exploration, lower-envelope pricing, and periodic and inconsistency-triggered restarts. It achieves regret $O((\sqrt{TV}+1)\log T)$. With heterogeneous contexts, a new geometric difficulty arises: small feature changes can leave a relaxed feasible set narrow but far from the current feature vector. We address this difficulty through a multiscale shallow-pricing algorithm that maintains relaxed feasible sets and enclosing ellipsoids at several variation scales. Larger scales provide reliable localization certificates, while the smallest active scale determines the exploitation price. The algorithm achieves dynamic regret $O((d^2+d^{3/2}\sqrt{TV})\log^2 T)$, where $d$ is the context dimension. For fixed $d$, both guarantees match the lower bound $\Omega(\sqrt{TV}+\log\log T)$ up to logarithmic factors in $T$, establishing near-optimality among deterministic algorithms for every $V\in[0,T]$, including the stationary case.
  </details>

- **[Grounding Predictive Reliability through Masked Observability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39728)**  `arXiv:2610.39728`  `cs.LG`  
  _Shang Gao, Bernhard Sick, Franz Götz-Hahn_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  An uncertainty estimate is meaningful only if its semantics can be tied to observable predictive behavior. Normal-Inverse-Gamma (NIG) evidential regression provides a structured decomposition of predictive variability into data-scale and predictive-mean components, but marginal likelihood does not identify their allocation. We address this semantic ambiguity in masked representation learning. MORSE (Masked Observability for Reliable Structured Encoding) predicts the same hidden target under controlled changes in visible context and uses normalized cross-context variation to ground predictive-mean uncertainty, while a Gaussian objective learns the conditional mean and data scale. The normalized variation plays the role of the NIG factor $1/\nu$, which the marginal leaves unidentified; this yields a policy-relative observability variance that measures dependence on visible evidence, distinct from variation induced by resampling the training set. On a Gaussian-process benchmark with a contextual reference built from analytic Bayes conditional means, MORSE has the lowest log-variance error among compared evidential, ensemble, and dropout estimators in all nine tested settings, while the estimator closest to training-set variation perturbs the model rather than its context in every setting, so the two references separate the estimators empirically. MORSE amortizes observability estimation into one forward pass per observation state. On a heteroscedastic pulse benchmark, its predictor's cross-context variance approaches a large-sample reference as training data increase. MORSE also improves low-label transfer on PTB-XL and in-distribution trajectory prediction on IMPTC.
  </details>

- **[A New AFIB Detection Method with Deep Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39665)**  `arXiv:2610.39665`  `cs.LG`  
  _Sara Gjorgjieva, Ana Angjelevska, Dimitar Trajanov, Marjan Gusev_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Early detection of atrial fibrillation (AFIB), characterized by irregular heart rhythms, can potentially save thousands of lives annually [20]. Numerous studies have attempted to address this challenge using various algorithms and methodologies. This paper introduces a novel and robust method that enhances the precision of AFIB detection. Neural Networks (NN) are widely recognized for their ability to identify complex electrocardiogram (ECG) patterns, facilitating the accurate detection of irregular heart rhythms. This study explores multiple conventional machine learning algorithms alongside neural network approaches to develop a robust AFIB detection system. To evaluate the effective- ness and robustness of the proposed method, the MIT BIH arrhythmia database (MITDB) and the Long-term Atrial Fibrillation Database (LTAFDB), both available on the Physionet site. Our approach presents a novel feature engineering technique, employing specific features such as a time series of the differences in successive heart rates (dBPM) rather than traditional inter-beat intervals. The model analyzes inputs labeled into five distinct classes, using only clean intervals identified through a new labeling method for training while treating others as outliers. This strategy ensures a more accurate and reliable AFIB detection process. The best-performing model, a Deep Neural Network (DNN), achieved an F1 score of 95.18% for binary atrial fibrillation classification on a benchmark dataset distinct from the training set. Models trained using our approach have demonstrated superior performance than previously published models.
  </details>

- **[LatentWeave: Test-Time Latent Optimization for Multimodal Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39557)**  `arXiv:2610.39557`  `cs.LG`  
  _Fanci Meng, Kun Wang, Guibin Zhang, Xinlei Yu, Jiaming Zhang, Ming Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in test-time optimization have shown that the multimodal reasoning performance of large multimodal language models (MLLMs) can be improved during inference without modifying backbone model parameters. However, existing approaches primarily operate at the prompt or output level, leaving the latent reasoning process largely uncontrolled and insufficient to prevent error accumulation during autoregressive generation. To address this limitation, we propose LatentWeave, a test-time framework that treats multimodal reasoning as a controllable latent trajectory. LatentWeave performs structured Latent Weaving, progressively refining intermediate hidden states segment-wise to correct emerging deviations, and further introduces Reasoning Anchors via lightweight WEAVE tokens to stabilize reasoning structure and guide answer-oriented generation. These mechanisms are designed to enable fine-grained, step-aware intervention in the latent reasoning process while keeping the main MLLM backbone parameters fixed. Extensive experiments across diverse multimodal reasoning benchmarks report improvements over the evaluated baselines; the contribution of latent optimization, anchor training, and additional test-time computation is analyzed separately in the ablation and efficiency studies. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Explainable and Adaptive Machine Learning for Fraud Risk Detection in Bangladesh's Digital Banking Ecosystem](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39556)**  `arXiv:2610.39556`  `cs.LG`  
  _Md Najmus Swaqeeb_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The rapid expansion of digital banking and mobile financial services creates growing challenges for fraud detection, particularly under severe class imbalance and operational constraints. This study presents an explainable and adaptive machine-learning framework integrating chronological evaluation, leakage-conscious feature engineering, probability calibration, SHAP-based explainability, and adaptive risk prioritization. Experiments use the synthetic PaySim mobile-money benchmark and compare Logistic Regression, Random Forest, XGBoost, and LightGBM. XGBoost achieved the strongest fixed-threshold performance, with PR-AUC of 0.5658, ROC-AUC of 0.9068, and F1-score of 0.5369. Platt calibration reduced the test Brier score from 0.1141 to 0.0245, improving probability reliability. SHAP analysis identified temporal, destination-account, transaction-type, and amount features as important model drivers. An adaptive top-1% policy reduced false positives from 192 to 117, but recall declined from 0.4236 to 0.2122, demonstrating a clear alert-capacity trade-off. The results provide a reproducible methodological framework rather than evidence of deployment performance in Bangladesh, since PaySim is synthetic. Validation using governed real-world financial data is therefore an important direction for future research.
  </details>

- **[Interpretable by Design Tabular Prediction via In-Context Local Linear Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39506)**  `arXiv:2610.39506`  `cs.LG`  
  _Ran Eisenberg, Steven Berger, Ofir Lindenbaum_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In-context learning (ICL) lets tabular foundation models make predictions without dataset-specific training or fine-tuning. These models achieve strong predictive accuracy, but interpreting how individual features contribute to the prediction remains difficult. In biology, for example, feature contributions can help identify biomarkers or risk genes. Post-hoc interpretability methods assign feature importance scores, but these scores do not indicate what the model actually uses and can lead to explanation errors. We introduce TICL$^3$ (Tabular In-Context Local Linear Learning), an interpretable-by-design predictor that uses a tabular foundation model to guide a simple linear prediction rule. TICL$^3$ first learns a shared linear model to capture dataset-level feature effects. Then, for each query, it retrieves similar training samples using representations from the foundation model and fits a second linear model to the residuals in that neighborhood. The shared model captures broad effects, while the local model corrects errors among similar samples. Their combined coefficients determine each feature contribution to the final query-specific linear score, so the same model makes and explains the prediction. Across 386 classification benchmark-dataset evaluations, TICL$^3$ trails TabICLv2 by 1.1 percentage points and outperforms CatBoost in 266 evaluations. It improves average support F1 over MAPLE by 4.7 points and achieves the best performance on both real-data selected-feature utility metrics.
  </details>

- **[CBE: A Complexity-adaptive Boundary-aware Ensemble Method for Imbalanced Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39466)**  `arXiv:2610.39466`  `cs.LG`  
  _Xun Wei_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In many real-world classification tasks, datasets exhibit class imbalance, which can degrade the generalization performance of conventional learning algorithms. Data complexity has recently been recognized as a more objective indicator of learning difficulty than the imbalance ratio alone, because class overlap and complex decision boundaries often impair classifiers more severely than imbalance itself. Samples near the decision boundary are inherently difficult to classify, which has motivated many researchers to focus on such instances to improve classifier performance. However, most under-sampling approaches fail to differentiate their sampling of boundary samples across datasets of varying complexity. To overcome this limitation, we propose a complexity-adaptive boundary-aware ensemble (CBE) framework that links the sampling weight distribution to data complexity and dynamically adjusts the boundary preference of under-sampling accordingly. We also introduce an overall complexity measure that combines metrics at multiple levels and use it to divide imbalanced datasets into three categories. Extensive experiments on 24 benchmark datasets from UCI and KEEL show that the proposed method outperforms many existing methods in terms of F-measure and G-mean on Easy and Median datasets, without degrading performance on hard cases.
  </details>

- **[IMRouter: Imbalance-aware Temporal Pattern Routing for LLM-based Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39246)**  `arXiv:2610.39246`  `cs.LG`  
  _Xin Yang, Cai Xu, Haishun Chen, Ying Liu, Jie Zhao, Wei Zhao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Accurate time series forecasting (TSF) is essential for Web applications, such as traffic monitoring and online resource management. Recent advances in leveraging pre-trained large language models (LLMs) have shown promising performance in TSF. However, existing LLM-based TSF methods largely optimize the overall forecasting objective while overlooking temporal pattern imbalance (TPI), where different temporal patterns occur at highly unequal frequencies. Our empirical analysis reveals that TPI is prevalent across widely used TSF benchmarks, with substantially higher forecasting errors on less frequent patterns. To address this issue, we propose IMRouter (Imbalance-aware Temporal Pattern Routing), a novel framework that mitigates TPI-induced biases in both temporal representation learning and cross-modal alignment. Specifically, IMRouter introduces Pattern-Adaptive Temporal Representation Learning (PATR), which leverages gradient conflict and local gradient density to guide expert specialization. By penalizing incompatible expert assignments with density-aware weighting, PATR reduces the dominance of frequent optimization signals in router training. Furthermore, Routing-Guided Semantic Alignment (RGSA) transfers the pattern-specific knowledge learned by the router to cross-modal alignment through mutual information maximization, preserving distinctive temporal information across different patterns. Extensive experiments on benchmark datasets demonstrate the effectiveness of IMRouter, particularly in improving forecasting performance on less frequent temporal patterns.
  </details>

- **[BASILISC: Bayesian Alignment of Siamese-Integrated Latents for Inferring Single-cell Correspondence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39050)**  `arXiv:2610.39050`  `cs.LG`  
  _Sepideh Saran, Mahsa Ghanbari, Uwe Ohler_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Single-cell assays study the genome one cell at a time. Unimodal assays measure one molecular layer, the newer multimodal assays measure several. Most data are single-modality, so integrating them relies on models trained on multimodal assays, where the cell correspondence across modalities is known. These models return a matching, but rarely a confidence measure. We present BASILISC (Bayesian Alignment of Siamese-Integrated Latents for Inferring Single-cell Correspondence), a framework for matching single-cell representations with uncertainty on the correspondence itself. A BASILISC model is a probabilistic siamese network over the two modalities with a predictive distribution over the correspondence for a cross-modal pair of cells. We instantiate it at two levels: at the cell-type level the question is whether two cells share a cell type, at the cell level which cell is the partner in the other modality. To our knowledge, this is the first uncertainty score on a learned cross-modal correspondence evaluated against a known ground-truth pairing. BASILISC learns a similarity across modalities that reflects cell lineage: an unseen cell type is matched to its lineage relative, matching still works on such held-out types (balanced accuracy 0.86 on unseen against 0.93 on seen types), and the predictive uncertainty rises on these out-of-distribution cells. The uncertainty score also selects high-quality matches for downstream analysis: discarding the least certain fifth of the pairs raises accuracy from 0.93 to 0.98. At the cell level, a deep ensemble of the deterministic matcher reaches 0.062 matching accuracy on the benchmark test set, above the competition winner; on the Bayesian matcher's assignment, the posterior flags the wrong matches that cross a cell-type boundary better than a deterministic read of the same scores (AUROC 0.68 against 0.64).
  </details>

- **[FairSplit: Decomposing the Embedding Space for Fair Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39031)**  `arXiv:2610.39031`  `cs.LG`  
  _Andrii Shkabrii, Anna Beer, Ylli Sadikaj, Claudia Plant_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Fair classification faces a twofold challenge: maintaining high classification accuracy while limiting reliance on sensitive information. Our method FairSplit achieves this by learning a decomposition of the embedding space into a Fair Space used for prediction and a Sensitive Space that isolates sensitive information. During training, the model must simultaneously learn the classification task and separate fair from sensitive information. The Sensitive Space gives sensitive attributes a destination, so the Fair Space can be used for prediction without relying on them. This decomposition is guided via a learnable mask and the Hilbert-Schmidt Independence Criterion (HSIC) as a measure of statistical dependence. We provide theoretical guarantees showing that the demographic parity gap is upper-bounded by both (i) the learned dimensionality of the Fair Space and (ii) the HSIC between the Fair Space and sensitive attributes. FairSplit effectively handles multiclass tasks and non-binary sensitive attributes, overcoming key limitations of existing fair classification methods. Experiments on established fairness benchmarks show that FairSplit achieves competitive accuracy-fairness trade-offs. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Communication-Efficient Clustered Federated Learning in Non-IID Wireless Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38970)**  `arXiv:2610.38970`  `cs.LG`
