  _Martin Isaksson, Rickard Cöster, Šarūnas Girdzijauskas, Seif Haridi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Current and emerging mobile networks face increasing privacy, scalability, and performance challenges as distributed learning becomes essential for advanced 6G services. These challenges are amplified by the presence of non-IID data, naturally arising in heterogeneous wireless environments, that degrade the performance of standard Federated Learning (FL) approaches. To address non-IID data across clients, we propose a Clustered Federated Learning (CFL) framework that assigns clients to specialized models during training. Data-domain clustering initializes model-based clustering, enabling identification and exploitation of client similarities without sharing raw data while reducing communication cost.Motivated by mmWave beam selection, we evaluate our methods on a wireless simulation dataset (DeepMIMO) and complement it with two non-IID benchmarks (FEMNIST, Rotated CIFAR-10) to isolate different non-IID characteristics. Our best configurations improve accuracy over IFCA by 4.8 pp on DeepMIMO and 10.6 pp on FEMNIST while reducing downlink data volume by 83.1 % on FEMNIST compared to IFCA.We demonstrate that different clustering strategies suit different non-IID characteristics. These results show that our CFL methods enable resource-efficient, and high-performance distributed learning in future 6G networks.
  </details>

- **[Two-Stage Clipping: Linear-Time Approximate and Exact Mixed-Norm Projections for Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38598)**  `arXiv:2610.38598`  `cs.LG`  
  _Michel Barlaud, Jean-Paul Marmorat_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models (LLMs) based on Transformer architectures have achieved remarkable performance across artificial intelligence tasks, but their computational cost remains a major bottleneck. Structured sparsification provides a promising approach to reducing this cost. In particular, $\ell_{1,\infty}$ projections can induce column-wise sparsity in model matrices, but existing proximal methods (Prox) typically require $\mathcal{O}(nm\log(nm))$ operations for matrices in $\mathbb{R}^{n\times m}$, limiting their computational and energy efficiency.In this paper, we introduce Two-Stage Clipping (TSC), an efficient structured sparsification operator inspired by bilevel optimization. Rather than solving a bilevel optimization problem in the strict sense, TSC decomposes the computation into two successive stages. First, group-level quantities are projected to allocate the global sparsity budget and identify active groups. Second, the resulting values are used as group-wise clipping thresholds for the matrix coefficients. For square attention matrices ($n \times n$), the complexity of our TSC method is $O(n^2)$, which is linear in the number of matrix coefficients.We establish a theoretical bound on the discrepancy between TSC and the exact proximal projection and show on synthetic data that the additional Euclidean approximation cost remains small in practice. Extensive benchmarks demonstrate substantial runtime improvements over proximal baselines and validate the theoretical norm identities underlying the proposed construction.
  </details>

- **[Learning a Prior-Data Fitted Network for Graph Foundation Models via Structural Causal Model Priors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38335)**  `arXiv:2610.38335`  `cs.LG`  
  _Ziwei Zhang, Tianyin Liao, Chunyu Hu, Ge Lan, Nuo Xu, Yi Huang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Graph Foundation Models (GFMs) have recently emerged as a promising paradigm for learning transferable representations across different graphs and domains. However, existing GFMs face notable limitations, including limited pre-training scale or reliance on aligning with textual information in Large Language Models (LLMs). To overcome these limitations, recent studies have drawn inspiration from prior-data fitted networks (PFNs), which learn from large synthetic datasets and support multi-domain prediction backed by causal learning. However, existing graph PFNs encounter severe difficulties in modeling causal relationships within and across node and edge sets, generating diverse graph structures, and designing suitable architectures that can learn from complex synthetic datasets. To address these challenges, we propose **GSCM-PFN** (Graph SCM-based Prior-Data Fitted Network), a graph PFN pre-trained on synthetic data generated from an SCM-based prior that jointly models nodes and graph topology. Specifically, we (1) design a joint feature-edge-label structural causal model (SCM) to guide synthetic graph generation and capture causal relationships within and across node and edge sets; (2) introduce prior-guided node-edge dependency modeling to instantiate diverse graph generation strategies, and prove that our SCM-based generation prior is more expressive than existing priors; and (3) propose a feature-permutation-invariant dual-path graph network with theoretical guarantees for learning from diverse synthetic graphs. Experiments on 23 real-world benchmarks show that GSCM-PFN outperforms both conventional GFMs and PFN-based GFMs, without utilizing real-world graph data for pre-training. Code:this https URL
  </details>

- **[NAGO: Noise-Aware Generative Operator via Flow Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38273)**  `arXiv:2610.38273`  `cs.LG`  
  _Guanyu Chen, Shengze Xu, Pengwei Liu, Xingyu Ren, Pengkai Wang, Antonios Armaou, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce NAGO, a noise-aware generative operator for efficient sampling from invariant measures of stochastic partial differential equations (SPDEs) in function space. Our central idea is to treat the source measure as a design variable rather than a fixed Gaussian default. We establish admissibility conditions for well-defined infinite-dimensional flows and show that the finite-band source--target covariance ratio controls the spectral stiffness of the marginal velocity field. This analysis yields an admissibility-constrained spectral-matching rule: choose a source that is sufficiently rough for well-posedness, then minimize covariance mismatch over the resolved Fourier modes. On three representative stochastic-field benchmarks, this source design improves few-step spectral accuracy and several complementary distributional and physical statistics. In a controlled Matern-field ablation, it reduces the accurate RK4 budget from about 30 steps for Gaussian functional flow matching to 3--5 steps, a $6$--$10\times$ reduction in neural ODE evaluations. These gains are strongest for stationary multiscale fields and depend on the metric, resolution, and guidance protocol.
  </details>

- **[Variational Consequence-Driven Offline Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38238)**  `arXiv:2610.38238`  `cs.LG`  
  _Wen Jiang, Ke Jiang, Xiaoyang Tan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Offline reinforcement learning (RL) typically mitigates distribution shift by imposing divergence constraints on the action distributions of the learned and behavior policies. However, this paradigm cannot ensure the underlying dynamic consistency structure: even minor action deviations can lead to drastically different transitions, inadvertently driving the agent away from the offline dataset. To address this, we explore policy constraints through their induced state-consequence distributions rather than relying on pure action-distribution matching. Nevertheless, directly enforcing such consequence-aware constraints is computationally intractable without access to the true underlying dynamics model. To bypass this issue, we propose Structural Behavior Regularization (StBR), which replaces the intractable objective with a closed-form latent divergence. Structuring this latent space to reflect transition dynamics renders the latent divergence a theoretically bounded surrogate for the true consequence-distribution divergence. By regularizing the learned policy with this latent divergence, StBR adaptively tightens the behavior constraint in dynamics-sensitive regions, effectively preventing deviations outside the offline dataset. Empirically, we demonstrate that StBR achieves superior performance across diverse offline RL benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[MASS: Multi-mask Selective Self-Distillation for Leakage-Resilient Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38154)**  `arXiv:2610.38154`  `cs.LG`  
  _Xiuqi Liu, Oveys Delafrooz Noroozi, Upamanyu Madhow_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  On-policy self-distillation enables the same model to act as student and teacher, with dense token-level supervision obtained by the teacher being given access to privileged information. However, conditioning the teacher on a single reference can concentrate its predictions on one reasoning trajectory and limit transfer to the student. We propose Multi-Mask Selective Self-Distillation (MASS), which approximates the unavailable marginal over diverse reference realizations using multiple independently masked views of one verified solution. MASS aggregates these complementary teacher predictions, selectively transfers reliable token-level corrections, and adaptively focuses masking on influential reference spans. Across mathematical reasoning benchmarks and model families, MASS consistently outperforms strong self-distillation baselines.
  </details>

- **[A Separation Principle for Adversarial Attacks and Defense of Graph Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38127)**  `arXiv:2610.38127`  `cs.LG`  
  _Farid Mafi, Saber Jafarpour, Mohammad Pirani_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Adversarial attacks on graph neural networks (GNNs) have been studied as a model-centric optimization problem driven by gradients, learned policies, or meta-learning, with the graph treated as a passive substrate. This view overlooks a defining feature of GNNs: their predictions are produced by structured message passing along the graph, so their adversarial vulnerabilities are encoded as much in the geometry of the graph as in the learned weights. We develop a structure-driven framework for feature attacks in which the attack is derived from the spectral properties of the normalized adjacency operator. Its central result is a separation principle: under a natural relaxation of the attack objective, the optimal feature perturbation is rank one and factorizes into a graph-dependent component (degree-proportional spatial allocation, depending only on the graph) and a weight-dependent component (top singular vector of the composed weights, depending only on the weights). Node degree thus emerges as the fundamental graph quantity governing adversarial vulnerability to feature perturbations. The resulting attack, which we call SepAttack, requires no gradient computation, carries analytical guarantees, achieves competitive or superior performance against established baselines such as Nettack and IG-Attack with up to ${\sim}70\times$ speedup in the targeted setting, and transfers near-perfectly across architectures on continuous-feature graphs because its graph-dependent component is architecture-invariant by construction. We further derive a separation-principle adversarial training procedure (SepAT) directly from the closed-form worst-case attack and report robustness gains on binary-feature benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[Preserving Safety During Sequential Fine-Tuning: A Factorial and Geometric Analysis of Replay](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38001)**  `arXiv:2610.38001`  `cs.LG`  
  _George Ruellan, Diana Benavides-Prado_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Benign fine-tuning can erode safety alignment in large language models, and sequential adaptation substantially compounds this risk. Prior work mitigates this erosion by replaying harmful-prompt refusals and benign-compliance examples, while other work instead matches base-model logits via distillation. However, the individual contributions of supervised exposure and distillation to safety behavior and refusal geometry during sequential adaptation remain unclear. We factor these two strategies apart and study their behavioral and geometric effects during sequential LoRA fine-tuning of Mistral-7B and Llama-3-8B across diverse task sequences (code generation, German-language summarization, and science question-answering). Behaviorally, supervised replay exposure yields the largest reduction in HarmBench attack success rate (ASR) on Mistral, averaging 51.1 points below base, but at a substantial over-refusal cost, averaging 53.4 points above base on OR-Bench-hard. Logit distillation, without any supervised replay loss, largely prevents safety erosion at a fraction of this cost: an average 5.4-point ASR reduction against only a 5.1-point rise in over-refusal. Llama shows the same asymmetry between the two strategies. Combining exposure and distillation moderates exposure's over-refusal while leaving downstream task scores largely unaffected. Geometrically, the two models diverge: on Mistral, distillation preserves the base refusal direction more closely than exposure does, whereas on Llama, refusal behavior after exposure largely survives ablation of the extracted refusal direction. This dissociation between models indicates that preserving the base refusal direction is not a reliable proxy for behavioral safety.
  </details>

- **[Deep learning for non-transvenous ICD screening: tailored neural network architectures, extended experiments, and explainability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37993)**  `arXiv:2610.37993`  `cs.LG`  
  _Bartosz Morawski, Samuel J. Ward, Alex Touze, Lin-Thiri Toon, Paul R. Roberts, John Paisey, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This paper studies two non-transvenous implantable cardioverter-defibrillators (ICDs), namely the Subcutaneous ICD (S-ICD) and the Extravascular ICD (EV-ICD). Both avoid the complications of transvenous leads but are prone to oversensing, which can trigger inappropriate shocks associated with increased mortality. Oversensing risk is predicted by the ratio of T- or P-wave amplitude to R-wave amplitude (T:R, P:R), and these ratios vary over a 24-hour period. Yet S-ICD screening uses a single 10-second electrocardiogram (ECG) segment, and the EV-ICD has no formal screening protocol whatsoever. We present a 24-hour ambulatory screening framework for both devices which includes the first T:R analysis in an EV-ICD cohort and the first full evaluation of P:R prediction. We introduce AmpRatioNet, a family of 1D convolutional neural network architectures operating directly on the ECG signal, and benchmark it against the best existing approach - based on 2D phase space reconstruction images. Evaluation uses 4,605 clinician-annotated 10-second segments from 89 patients, with patient-level 10-fold cross-validation and bootstrap confidence intervals. AmpRatioNet-SE demonstrates superior performance on every metric in each setting with no device- or ratio-specific redesign. An explainability analysis against interpretable classical peak labelling algorithms shows that AmpRatioNet-SE's increase in error on oversensing-prone segments is up to 14 times smaller than theirs. Segment-level predictions are aggregated into a 24-hour timeline, extending S-ICD screening beyond 10 seconds and offering a plausible EV-ICD screening pathway pending device-level validation.
  </details>

- **[Separability Is Not Alignability: Representations for Spectrum-to-Molecule Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37979)**  `arXiv:2610.37979`  `cs.LG`  
  _Karol Musielinski, Fanurs C.E. Teh, Takahiko Koyama, Artur Dubrawski_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Identifying a small molecule from its tandem mass spectrum is limited by paired data. Foundation models offer a way around this scarcity: pretrain a spectrum encoder on unlabeled spectra and a molecule encoder on a large corpus of structures, then align the two frozen representations, as in vision-language and as the Platonic Representation Hypothesis suggests. We audit this route on MassSpecGym with spectrum encoders retrained free of test-fold contamination. Alignment needs a metric relating distinct molecules, but the objectives that produce spectrum encoders never see molecular structure: we prove that some predictive objectives fix molecular identity without fixing that metric. Moreover, we find that a candidate-based objective rewards a spectrum-independent prior over the benchmark's decoys, which we isolate. What ultimately addresses data scarcity is not architectural: simulated spectra surpass frozen geometry.
  </details>

- **[Application of PDE emulators for a multi-material, multi-physics simulation problem](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37972)**  `arXiv:2610.37972`  `cs.LG`  
  _Diane Oyen, Morgan Jones, Adithan Kathirgamaraju, Abu Bucker Siddik, Alexander Most, David Schodt, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Machine learning (ML) architectures are being developed for fast emulation of numerical simulators to accelerate both scientific discovery and engineering design. While these approaches are well-tested on popular PDE (partial differential equation) benchmarks, it is necessary to evaluate their performance on datasets that more accurately reflect industrial and engineering applications. We evaluate several computer vision-based architectures trained as surrogate models on a challenging computational physics task involving multiple materials, revealing significant implementation challenges for adapting certain pre-trained models, including transformer-based architectures. For such multi-physics multi-material datasets, our results underscore the importance of learning algorithms designed for sparse and imbalanced data, techniques for adapting spatial encodings and physical consistency metrics, in addition to methods for reducing the cost of generating sufficient training data.
  </details>

- **[Depth through Recurrence: Towards Ultra-Efficient On-Device ASR](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37833)**  `arXiv:2610.37833`  `cs.LG`  
  _Chen Feng, Tianyi Xu, Ramchalam Kinattinkara Ramakrishnan, Yicheng Lin, Shaojie Zhuo, Zhaocong Yuan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Modern automatic speech recognition (ASR) systems have achieved remarkable accuracy by scaling model depth and capacity, but at the cost of increased memory, energy, and computational demands. On edge devices where ASR is often most needed, such as watches and glasses, deploying such large models is infeasible due to extremely constrained resources. This raises a fundamental question: can we achieve high representational power in deep ASR models without scaling up parameterization? In this work, we revisit the role of depth and identify layer wise representational dynamics, in which most layers learn functionally similar transformations. Motivated by this insight, we propose a block-recurrent ASR architecture that replaces parameterized depth with a small set of recurrent blocks, each consisting of weight-shared layers, thereby preserving effective depth while drastically reducing model size. Through representation-guided grouping and knowledge distillation, block-recurrent models retain the accuracy of large reference models while using an order of magnitude fewer parameters. Across Open ASR Leaderboard benchmarks, a model with only two shared blocks recovers 97.4% of the accuracy of large models on average. The compact backbone further enables efficient on-device personalization through lightweight MoE-LoRA adaptation, allowing user-specific models to match or surpass the accuracy of general-purpose ASR systems with significantly lower inference cost. By reusing weights across recurrent steps, the proposed architecture reduces memory-access overhead and inference energy consumption. Together, these results show that high quality ASR can be achieved without large parameterization, and that structured recurrence provides a principled path towards ultra-compressed, energy-efficient, and personalized speech recognition on the edge.
  </details>

- **[CTLite: Lightweight Irregular Multivariate Time Series Forecasting with Reusable Continuous-Time Summaries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26379)**  `arXiv:2610.26379`  `cs.LG`  
  _Shusen Ma, Xudong Zuo, Yangchun Xie, Yun-Bo Zhao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Irregular multivariate time series forecasting (IMTSF) requires capturing temporal dynamics and cross-variable dependencies from sparse, asynchronous observations. Existing methods often rely on learned temporal encoders, coupling historical representation construction with model optimisation and repeatedly processing the same observations. We propose CTLite, a lightweight forecasting framework that decouples historical summarisation from learned prediction. Specifically, a parameter-free continuous-time moment encoder summarises local levels and trends at multiple temporal scales while suppressing poorly supported slope estimates. A compact readout combines damped local extrapolation with a low-rank cross-variable correction that accounts for observation freshness, target availability, and forecast horizon. Since the historical summaries are independent of trainable parameters and forecast queries, they can be reused across optimisation steps and repeated predictions for an unchanged history without altering predictions or parameter gradients. Experiments on four real-world benchmarks show that CTLite achieves the best or second-best mean MSE and MAE among the evaluated methods with only 88-2,537 trainable parameters. Under matched GPU workloads at batch size 32, CTLite reduces inference latency by 18.6-87.3% compared with the latest models, including the cost of historical encoding. Reusing summaries further reduces total runtime by approximately 52% over 16 forecast calls on the same history compared with recomputing the encoder for every call, including the initial encoding cost. The code is at:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[HiPPO Functional Memory: Coordinate-Queryable Attention for Irregular Time Series](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24501)**  `arXiv:2610.24501`  `cs.LG`  
  _Zhaodong Guo, Haoyun Feng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Irregular time series require models to answer temporal queries at arbitrary coordinates, yet existing approaches typically retain observation-dependent representations that must be repeatedly resolved for each query. We introduce HiPPO Functional Memory (HiPPO-FM), a functional-memory attention operator that compiles an irregular history into a reusable coordinate-addressable state. Instead of storing observation-indexed tokens or latent trajectories, HiPPO-FM writes observations into a fixed-rank functional space and performs direct coordinate evaluation through matched functional geometry. Kernel-smoothed HiPPO coordinates and Gram-based readout define a shared write/read mechanism, while observed-support self-attention enables content-dependent representations before compression. We characterize the induced finite-dimensional query space and derive an error decomposition separating functional geometry approximation from learned-memory mismatch. Across continuous-coordinate reconstruction, irregular representation learning, real-world extrapolation, and event-sequence prediction, HiPPO-FM consistently improves strong irregular time-series baselines, achieving strong reconstruction accuracy on Spiral, competitive performance across 20 UEA datasets, improved real-world extrapolation, and superior results on several irregular event benchmarks. After encoding, the compact functional state can be reused across different query sets without reprocessing the observation history.
  </details>

- **[ResidualQuant: KV Cache Quantization for Looped Transformers with 2-Bit Residuals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.10381)**  `arXiv:2610.10381`  `cs.LG`  
  _Heejun Kim, Junyoung Lee, SangLyul Cho, Dongsu Han, Insu Han, Sehoon Kim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Looped Transformers improve parameter efficiency by applying shared Transformer blocks over recurrent loops, increasing computational depth without increasing the parameter count. However, KV cache memory still scales with loop count, becoming a key memory bottleneck that limits batch size and inference throughput. KV cache quantization can alleviate this bottleneck, but existing methods often suffer substantial accuracy degradation at aggressive low-precision regimes. We observe that looped Transformers offer a unique opportunity: KV states across loops are highly similar. Based on this observation, we propose ResidualQuant, which uses the final-loop KV states as a reference and represents the remaining loops with low-precision residuals. Our method further combines least-square scaling and rotation-based quantization, as well as loop-wise mixed precision, to enable accurate quantization down to INT2 while retaining efficient reconstruction. Across multiple looped Transformer models and mathematical reasoning and code generation benchmarks, ResidualQuant consistently improves the accuracy-memory tradeoff over state-of-the-art rotation-based KV quantization. In particular, our method retains accuracy close to BF16 under mixed-precision settings while reducing theoretical KV storage by 80.7%, achieving up to 13.0% higher accuracy than the rotation-based baseline at nearly the same memory budget. On an RTX 5090, reduced KV memory traffic improves fixed-batch decode throughput by up to 3.66x, while the smaller memory footprint enables up to 8x larger batches, improving peak throughput by up to 5.38x.
  </details>

- **[Towards Better Training Signal: Advantage Clipped Policy Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36816)**  `arXiv:2609.36816`  `cs.LG`  
  _Ruichuan Huang, Jinghan Liu, Congliang Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning (RL) has become a cornerstone for improving the reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs), but the need for on-policy data substantially limits training efficiency. Reusing off-policy data through importance sampling (IS) can improve efficiency but introduce considerable instability. Hence, algorithms such as PPO and GRPO widely adopt IS-ratio clipping to stabilize training. However, training stability and gradient estimate are mainly determined by the product of IS ratio and advantage. To further stabilize training, we propose ACPO, which clips the product of the IS ratio and the advantage, leading to more stable gradient estimates. We also establish a connection between ACPO and gradient clipping in policy mirror descent (PMD), which is a standard technique to stabilize optimization process, and prove the convergence of clipped-PMD under the standard RL setting. Experiments on widely used mathematical reasoning benchmarks show that ACPO consistently outperforms PPO and GRPO in both accuracy and training efficiency, delivering 4-6 percentage points gains on standard math benchmarks, with Qwen3-8B+PPO. Hence, ACPO is a practical and effective alternative to conventional IS-ratio clipping for RL post-training of LLMs.
  </details>

- **[ScAn-Bench: Evaluating Scaling Analysis Methodology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35707)**  `arXiv:2609.35707`  `cs.LG`  
  _Artin Sermaxhaj, Nastaran Alipour, Donat Sinani, Johannes Hog, Neeratyoy Mallik, Steven Adriaensen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent progress in machine learning is driven by large-scale foundation models, where scaling laws and finding optimal scaling prescriptions for architecture, data, and hyperparameters are key in advancing the state-of-the-art. Therefore, it is surprising that no systematic study evaluates the methodology to obtain scaling laws and prescriptions across different model types. To shed light on this crucial blind spot and facilitate future research, we introduce the surrogate benchmarks ScAn-Bench-LLM and ScAn-Bench-VLM based on 4524 and 8024 checkpoints of language and vision-language model pipelines. On our benchmarks, we perform the first systematic evaluation of both data acquisition and extrapolation methodology for scaling analysis across different data modalities.
  </details>

- **[The Model Organism Lottery: Model Organism Interpretability Strongly Depends on Training Methodology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01033)**  `arXiv:2607.01033`  `cs.LG`  
  _Andrzej Szablewski, Gabriel Konar-Steenberg, Raffaello Fornasiere, Nikita Menon, Stefan Heimersheim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Model organisms (MOs) - language models trained to exhibit undesired or unnatural behaviours - are frequently used as testbeds for evaluating white-box interpretability techniques. Current MOs are typically constructed via post-hoc supervised fine-tuning (SFT). Prior research has shown that interpretability methods can easily identify hidden behaviours in these MOs. However, recent work suggests that such post-hoc training methods may make interpretability unrealistically easy. We investigate this claim by constructing a suite of 61 $\verb|OLMo2-1B|$- and $\verb|gemma-3-1b-it|$-based MOs trained with seven different techniques, including standard post-hoc SFT, post-hoc DPO, and more realistic integration of MO data into the OLMo post-training DPO phase. We also use these to train 96 further variants via knowledge distillation. We use these MO variants to benchmark activation oracles, activation steering, the logit lens, and the Jacobian lens. Our findings show that (i) MO interpretability depends strongly on training objective, target behaviour, model architecture, and training data generation pipeline; (ii) substantial variance remains even after controlling for differences in the strength of target behaviour expression; and (iii) both our more realistic $\textit{integrated training}$ and knowledge distillation often yield less interpretable MOs than standard post-hoc methods. Our results cast substantial doubt on the validity of current MOs as interpretability proxies and suggest actionable steps towards more robust benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[MassSpecGym in the Wild: Uncovering and Correcting Evaluation Pitfalls in AI-Driven Molecule Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.19624)**  `arXiv:2606.19624`  `cs.LG`  
  _Hongxuan Liu, Roman Bushuiev, Ivy Lightheart, Mrunali Manjrekar, Anton Bushuiev, Magdalena Lederbauer, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reliable benchmarking is critical for developing machine learning models for tandem mass spectrometry (MS/MS) based molecule discovery. Subtle issues in experimental design and model evaluation procedures can degrade the trustworthiness of such benchmarks and lead to erroneous conclusions. We conduct a thorough review of model evaluation issues in the recent MS/MS machine learning literature, using the standard MassSpecGym benchmark suite as a case study to illustrate the impact of these issues. We find evaluation issues in at least 17 of 26 papers reporting MassSpecGym benchmark results in the first year of its adoption. We isolate three classes of failures: (i) data leakage, (ii) shortcut learning, and (iii) implementation bugs and metric divergence. Through extensive experimentation and code replication, we quantify the impact of these issues and show how they corrupt the evaluation standards MassSpecGym was designed to enforce. We distill our findings into recommendations generalizable to MS/MS challenges, benchmarks, and custom evaluation setups. We also release MassSpecGym v1.5, an implementation of our recommendations in the MassSpecGym benchmarking suite which addresses the failure modes identified in this audit. MassSpecGym v1.5 is publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[SkillHone: A Harness for Continual Agent Skill Evolution Through Persistent Decision History](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.08671)**  `arXiv:2606.08671`  `cs.LG`  
  _Zhiwei Li, Yong Hu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agent skills extend language-model agents with task-specific procedures, scripts, and references, but the tasks and environments they target continually change. Existing methods improve skills in bounded runs and retain only the final artifact, discarding the decision history that later agents need to interpret prior revisions, evaluations, and rejected alternatives. We introduce SkillHone, a harness for continual agent skill evolution grounded in persistent decision history. SkillHone pairs skill revisions with evaluation-side evidence that supplies practice feedback, recording structured histories of diagnoses, revisions, evidence, and outcomes. Role-separated subagents run candidate skills on practice probes with redacted reporting and propose revisions informed by prior decisions, enabling cross-session refinement without rediscovering past rationale. On deep-research benchmarks, SkillHone runs without a pre-integrated search stack and outperforms the commercially backed deep-research agent by 15.8 points on GAIA and 3.2 points on WebWalkerQA-EN, while also exceeding prior skill-evolution methods. We further deploy SkillHone on internal tool-mediated analysis scenarios, where it improves accuracy by an average of 18.8 points across seven settings.
  </details>

- **[Dialectics of Alignment: Harnessing Unsafe Knowledge for Dynamic Safety Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.00686)**  `arXiv:2606.00686`  `cs.LG`  
  _Maryam Hashemzadeh, Jerry Huang, Minseon Kim, Marc-Alexandre Côté, Sarath Chandar_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The prevailing paradigm in large language model (LLM) alignment operates via erasure, filtering unsafe data or training models to strictly refuse harmful prompts. While effective at reducing immediate toxicity, this approach fundamentally constricts the model's epistemological scope, resulting in over-cautious systems that output uninformative blanket refusals to sensitive yet benign queries. In this work, we challenge the orthodoxy that unsafe data must be discarded. We propose a dialectical approach to alignment, positing that unsafe data encodes rich, domain-specific knowledge critical for nuanced, safe, and informative generation. To operationalize this, we introduce SafeMoE, a Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) framework that isolates unsafe knowledge into domain-specific Low-Rank Adapters (LoRA experts) trained exclusively on harmful corpora. To synthesize safety from these unsafe primitives, we train a lightweight gating network using a minimal, highly curated set of safe-informative responses (as few as 100 samples per domain). During inference, this router dynamically orchestrates the unsafe experts, effectively steering the generation trajectory toward safe outcomes while harnessing the experts' deep domain knowledge. Extensive empirical evaluations across stringent safety benchmarks demonstrate that SafeMoE is not only safer, achieving over a 20% relative improvement in safe response rate over the strongest baseline (RealSafe-R1 on PKU-SafeRLHF; more than a 15% absolute gain), but also produces more informative responses when safety and harmfulness are of paramount concern. Furthermore, the routing mechanism exhibits strong zero-shot generalization to unseen domains and broader safety tasks without domain-specific supervision. Our findings suggest a paradigm shift in alignment: true safety requires not the masking of unsafe knowledge, but its controlled integration.
  </details>

- **[Bastion: Budget-Aware Speculative Decoding with Tree-structured Block Diffusion Drafting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29727)**  `arXiv:2605.29727`  `cs.LG`  
  _Soowon Oh, Nam Cao, Yujin Kim, Hojung Jung, Huzama Ahmad, Sangmin Bae, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Block-diffusion drafters have recently emerged as a powerful alternative for speculative decoding by predicting multiple future-token distributions in a single parallel step. However, since these parallel predictions are sampled from position-wise marginals rather than fully conditioned sequences, committing to a single greedy path often fails to capture the target model's preferred trajectory. To address this, we propose BASTION, a budget-aware speculative decoding framework with tree-based diffusion drafting. Unlike existing methods that rely on static tree topologies, BASTION dynamically constructs query-dependent trees by balancing draft quality against hardware constraints. Our framework integrates three synergistic components: (1) an acceptance surrogate that estimates expected accepted length via path confidence, (2) an online latency estimator that calibrates a hardware-aware roofline model, and (3) an adaptive best-first expansion that grows the tree until marginal gains no longer justify incremental verification costs. BASTION is training-free, preserves the target model's distribution, and requires no per-setting tuning. Across diverse benchmarks and GPU architectures, BASTION achieves up to a $6.61\times$ speedup over standard autoregressive decoding, outperforming state-of-the-art block-diffusion baselines by 39%.
  </details>

- **[Where to Spend Rollouts: Hit-Utility Optimal Rollout Allocation for Group-Based RLVR](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.07114)**  `arXiv:2605.07114`  `cs.LG`  
  _Tao Wang, Shuo Li, Yan Sun, Dongsheng Ding, Edgar Dobriban_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) has emerged as a central paradigm for improving the reasoning capabilities of large language models. Group-based policy optimization methods, such as GRPO, typically allocate a fixed number of rollouts to every prompt. This uniform allocation can be inefficient: it over-allocates compute to prompts whose sampled groups are already saturated while under-exploring prompts for which additional samples may reveal useful correct trajectories. To address this limitation, we introduce hit utility, the posterior probability that at least one rollout in a proposed additional allocation for a prompt will be correct. Building on this notion, we propose Hit-Utility Optimal Rollout Allocation (HORA), a learning-free rollout allocation policy that maximizes total posterior hit utility within each allocation batch. HORA adaptively reallocates rollout budgets while leaving the downstream reward evaluation and group-based advantage estimator unchanged. Across two training sets, four mathematical reasoning benchmarks, and three model families at multiple scales, HORA preserves comparable Pass@1 and improves Pass@K over compute-matched GRPO, and it outperforms related allocation baselines at large K. It is also drop-in compatible with other group-based estimators such as RLOO. Ablation studies indicate that the uniform prior used by HORA is competitive with five prompt-conditioned learned-prior alternatives.
  </details>

- **[Self-Organizing Maps with Optimized Latent Positions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.13622)**  `arXiv:2604.13622`  `cs.LG`  
  _Seiki Ubukata, Akira Notsu, Katsuhiro Honda_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Self-Organizing Maps (SOM) are a classical method for unsupervised learning, vector quantization, and topographic mapping of high-dimensional data. However, existing SOM formulations often involve a trade-off between computational efficiency and a clearly defined optimization objective. Objective-based variants such as Soft Topographic Vector Quantization (STVQ) provide a principled formulation, but their neighborhood-coupled computations become expensive as the number of latent nodes increases. In this paper, we propose Self-Organizing Maps with Optimized Latent Positions (SOM-OLP), an objective-based topographic mapping method that introduces a continuous latent position for each data point. Starting from the neighborhood distortion of STVQ, we construct a separable surrogate local cost based on its local quadratic structure and formulate an entropy-regularized objective based on it. This yields a simple block coordinate descent scheme with closed-form updates for assignment probabilities, latent positions, and reference vectors, while guaranteeing monotonic non-increase of the objective and retaining linear per-iteration complexity in the numbers of data points and latent nodes. Experiments on a synthetic saddle manifold, scalability studies on the Digits and MNIST datasets, and 16 benchmark datasets show that SOM-OLP achieves competitive neighborhood preservation and quantization performance, favorable scalability for large numbers of latent nodes and large datasets, and the best average rank among the compared methods on the benchmark datasets.
  </details>

- **[Improved Sampling Schedules for Discrete Diffusion Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.06849)**  `arXiv:2602.06849`  `cs.LG`  
  _Alberto Foresti, Mustapha Bounoua, Giulio Franzese, Luca Ambrogioni, Pietro Michiardi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Uniform Discrete Diffusion Models have emerged as a powerful paradigm for generative modeling on sequence data; however, the information-theoretic principles governing their reverse processes remain underexplored. In this work, we bridge this gap by analyzing the reverse process dynamics through the lens of thermodynamic entropy production and dynamical activity. We use a variational expression of the discrete Wasserstein distance to characterize the dynamics of the generation process. Leveraging these insights, we first introduce the Entropic Discrete Schedule (EDS), which aligns with entropy production, and later construct the Wasserstein Discrete Schedule (WDS), which combines entropy production and dynamical activity to act as a geometric cost. Our experimental results on language modeling show that WDS provide the same results as uniform while using up to half of the computations on the MBPP benchmark. The code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Learning Where It Matters: Geometric Anchoring for Robust Preference Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.04909)**  `arXiv:2602.04909`  `cs.LG`  
  _Youngjae Cho, Jongsuk Kim, Ji-Hoon Kim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) and related methods align large language models from pairwise preferences by regularizing updates against a fixed reference policy. As the policy drifts, a static reference, however, can become increasingly miscalibrated, leading to distributional mismatch and amplifying spurious preference signals under noisy supervision. Conversely, reference-free variants avoid mismatch but often suffer from unconstrained reward drift. We propose Geometric Anchor Preference Optimization (GAPO), which replaces the fixed reference with a dynamic, geometry-aware anchor: an adversarial local perturbation of the current policy within a small radius that serves as a pessimistic baseline. This anchor enables an adaptive reweighting mechanism, modulating the importance of each preference pair based on its local sensitivity. We further introduce the Anchor Gap, the reward discrepancy between the policy and its anchor, and show under smoothness conditions that it approximates worst-case local margin degradation. Optimizing a logistic objective weighted by this gap downweights geometrically brittle instances while emphasizing robust preference signals. Across diverse noise settings, GAPO consistently improves robustness while matching or improving performance on standard LLM alignment and reasoning benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[Riemannian Lyapunov Framework: Optimization as Closed Loop Control on Riemannian Manifolds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.22284)**  `arXiv:2601.22284`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yixuan Wang, Omkar Sudhir Patil, Warren E. Dixon_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce the Riemannian Lyapunov Framework (RLF), a unified framework that represents first order optimization as a closed loop controlled dynamical system on a Riemannian parameter manifold. Within this framework, optimizers are specified by a quadruple $(M, g, \Phi, \eta)$ consisting of a Riemannian metric, an internal state transition map, a direction field generator, and a lifting parameter. Classical and modern methods including SGD, Adam, AdamW, Lion and Shampoo are recovered as specific instantiations of this quadruple. The central structural object of the framework is a time indexed family of target submanifolds in the extended state space of parameters, velocities, and internal memory, which we call the Normally Attracting Invariant Manifold (NAIM) family. These submanifolds organize training dynamics into two timescales: a fast contraction of the velocity state onto the current target submanifold, followed by a slow descent along it. Using Riemannian backstepping, we construct a strict Lyapunov function on the extended state space and prove Uniform Ultimate Boundedness (UUB) of the optimization trajectory under a descent alignment assumption and bounded stochastic disturbances. Under the additional Polyak-Lojasiewicz (PL) condition, the Lyapunov function converges linearly to a bounded neighborhood of the optimum, defined as the drift of the target direction field between consecutive iterates after parallel transport. To validate the framework's generativity, we derive three concrete instantiations of the quadruple, each exercising a distinct design axis, and show on geometric diagnostics, image classification benchmarks, and language modeling that their empirical behavior matches the theoretical predictions. The framework casts optimizer design as controller synthesis, complementing heuristic perspectives by providing a constructive template with provable guarantees.
  </details>

- **[A Scalable Multi-Task Model for Virtual Sensors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.20634)**  `arXiv:2601.20634`  `cs.LG`  
  _Leon Götz, Lars Frederik Peiss, Erik Sauer, Andreas Udo Sass, Thorsten Bagdonat, Stephan Günnemann, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Virtual sensors replace expensive physical sensors in critical applications through machine learning by predicting target signals from available measurements. Existing virtual sensor approaches require application-specific models with hand-selected inputs for each sensor, cannot leverage task synergies, and lack consistent benchmarks. While emerging time series foundation models offer general-purpose, pretrained solutions in other domains, they are computationally expensive and limited to predicting their input signals, making them incompatible with virtual sensors. We introduce the first multi-task model for virtual sensors addressing both limitations. Our unified model can simultaneously predict diverse virtual sensors exploiting synergies while maintaining computational efficiency. It learns relevant input signals for each virtual sensor, eliminating expert knowledge requirements while adding explainability. In our large-scale evaluation on three standard benchmarks and an application-specific dataset with over 18 billion samples, our architecture reduces computation time by up to 415x and memory requirements by 951x, while maintaining or even improving predictive quality compared to unified baselines. Compared to existing isolated models for a single virtual sensor, our unified approach generates superior predictions at similar inference speed while scaling gracefully to hundreds of virtual sensors with nearly constant parameter count, enabling practical deployment in large-scale sensor networks.
  </details>

- **[SelfGRL: Self-Organized Graph Representation Learning Inspired by Helmholtz Free Energy Minimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.24062)**  `arXiv:2512.24062`  `cs.LG`  
  _Rui Chen, Junjun Guo, Hongbin Wang, Yan Xiang, Yantuan Xian, Zhengtao Yu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Graph Representation Learning (GRL) requires node embeddings to preserve local structural coherence while remaining globally discriminative. Existing graph self-supervised methods generally coordinate these competing objectives through method-specific designs, lacking a unified and adaptive mechanism that responds to the evolving representation state. To address this limitation, we propose SelfGRL, a self-organized graph representation learning framework that reformulates representation learning as Helmholtz free-energy minimization on the unit hypersphere. SelfGRL models local neighborhood attraction as a Structural Binding Energy through neighbor-mean alignment, while introducing a Sampling-Free Repulsive Entropy objective to preserve global dispersion and prevent representation collapse without pairwise negative sampling. To dynamically coordinate these competing effects, we develop an endogenous Bayesian Thermostat that adaptively regulates their relative strengths in response to the evolving representation state, allowing the system to approach a metastable semantic equilibrium without requiring manually tuned loss weights. Extensive experiments on node classification across both homophilic and heterophilic benchmark datasets, together with link prediction evaluations, demonstrate that SelfGRL consistently achieves strong performance across diverse graph structures.
  </details>

- **[Understanding the Staged Dynamics of Transformers in Learning Latent Structure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.19328)**  `arXiv:2511.19328`  `cs.LG`  
  _Rohan Saha, Farzane Aminmansour, Alona Fyshe_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Language models have proven to be capable of learning the intricate latent structures in language. However, the dynamics of how such models acquire the components of that structure remain poorly understood, leaving them open to the criticism that models just remix training data. In this work, we use the Alchemy benchmark as a controlled setting in which we investigate how models acquire the different latent structure components of the data. We train a small decoder-only transformer on three controlled task variants: 1) inferring missing steps from partial contextual information, 2) composing simple rules to solve multi-step sequences, and 3) decomposing complex multi-step examples to infer intermediate states. By factorizing each task into interpretable components, we show that the model learns the different latent structure components in discrete $\textit{stages}$ and in a $\textit{coarse-to-fine}$ manner. Using inference-time ablations, we demonstrate that when certain latent structures are masked or replaced, the model can use an alternative intermediate latent structure to solve the task. We also observe an asymmetry: the model composes single step transitions robustly, but struggles to decompose complex examples to discover the atomic steps. Our findings provide insight into $\textit{how}$ a transformer model acquires latent structure, offering a detailed view of how capabilities evolve during training.
  </details>

- **[Solver-Integrated Adversarial Attacking and Training of Neural Operators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.18989)**  `arXiv:2510.18989`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yifei Sun_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Neural operators are widely used as fast surrogates for numerical PDE solvers, mapping input functions to solution functions. However, their generalizability and robustness are not yet clearly defined in the operator-learning setting, which differs from traditional adversarial robustness definitions. This paper studies the generalizability and robustness of a learned neural operator from a solver-integrated perspective, addressing the challenge that the output of a learned operator and a numerical solver tends to change in tandem under input perturbation. First, we formalize the definition of generalization and robustness through a model-solver error operator, identifying fixed-input model-solver loss as generalization metric, and norm-bounded adversarial attack loss increase and Jacobian-error function norm as robustness metric. Second, we identify the solver-integrated adversarial attack as appropriate for PDE operator learning and show why model-only or fixed-ground-truth attacks can be insufficient when the solver output also changes with the input. Third, we develop solver-integrated adversarial training methods for neural operators. Experiments on representative PDE benchmarks show that this solver-integrated adversarial training clearly improves both generalizability and robustness. Deeper solver integration yields more effective attacks, more informative samples, and more efficient training than less integrated alternatives. These results provide a general framework for robust operator training and automatic sample selection without heavy manual intervention. More broadly, the formulation applies to adversarial regression whenever a ground-truth oracle can evaluate, and ideally differentiate, the true input-output map; PDE operator learning is one such case.
  </details>

- **[In-Place Feedback: Reliable Refinement for Multi-Turn Expert-LLM Collaboration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.00777)**  `arXiv:2510.00777`  `cs.LG`  
  _Youngbin Choi, Minjong Lee, Saemi Moon, Seunghyuk Cho, Chaehyeon Chung, MoonJeong Park, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM-generated drafts often contain subtle factual or logical errors, yet prior work shows that models struggle to reliably integrate multi-turn feedback aimed at fixing them. We propose in-place feedback, an interaction paradigm in which the user directly edits the model's previous response and the model continues generation from the edited context. In-place feedback consistently outperforms standard multi-turn feedback across five reasoning-intensive benchmarks while requiring fewer tokens, and our fine-grained analysis shows that it applies corrections more reliably and propagates them to subsequent reasoning. A user study with domain experts refining LLM-generated summaries corroborates these findings: participants report higher final-output satisfaction and substantially lower fatigue with in-place feedback, and a mixed strategy combining in-place and multi-turn feedback improves every dimension we measure. These results suggest that editing errors in place is a more reliable way to incorporate expert corrections than multi-turn feedback, while combining the two acts complementarily to form a more effective collaboration than either mechanism alone.
  </details>

- **[HiFOR: Hierarchical Foresight with Action-Guided World Grounding for Vision-Language-Action Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40373)**  `arXiv:2610.40373`  `cs.CV` `cs.RO`  
  _Rongxing Ding, Hongyu Qu, Kehong Wu, Xudong Wang, Fan Zhang, Xinguang Xiang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models have demonstrated promising capabilities in robotic manipulation by integrating visual perception, language understanding, and action generation. Recent advances have further incorporated future prediction to enhance the understanding of environmental dynamics and task progression. However, existing methods often model short-term dynamics and long-term task objectives separately, while directly incorporating predicted futures into action policies without sufficiently considering their relevance to current action requirements. To address these limitations, we propose HiFOR, a hierarchical foresight framework with action-guided world retrieval. Specifically, Hierarchical Foresight Prediction jointly predicts short-term state evolution, semantic milestones, and final goal states through a Hierarchical Dependency Mask (HDM), establishing dependencies across temporal horizons. Building upon these predictions, Structure-Aware Latent World Modeling organizes future latent representations along the horizon, view, and spatial dimensions to facilitate fine-grained retrieval. Furthermore, Action-Guided Factorized World Retrieval adaptively retrieves relevant future information from the structured latent world based on evolving action representations during denoising, providing targeted guidance for action generation. Extensive experiments on two simulation benchmarks and six real-world robotic manipulation tasks demonstrate the effectiveness of our approach.
  </details>

- **[NovaSplat: Model-Free 6D Object Pose Estimation via 3D Gaussian Splatting Onboarding and Refinement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13309)**  `arXiv:2610.13309`  `cs.CV` `cs.RO`  
  _Georgios Floros, Evangelos Sartinas, Orestis Vaggelis, Maria Pateraki_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  6D pose estimation of novel objects without CAD models remains an open challenge: model-free methods must build the object representation from scratch during onboarding and have long trailed their model-based counterparts. We present NovaSplat, a model-free pipeline that closes this gap. From a short smartphone video captured at onboarding, we train a 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) model that serves both as a template source for detection and coarse pose estimation and as a differentiable backbone for pose refinement. Our refiner optimizes the object-to-camera pose with combined photometric and geometric losses under a continuous rotation parametrization free of gradient singularities. On a household-object benchmark, NovaSplat surpasses all model-based methods. On a challenging industrial benchmark with textureless and specular surfaces, it outperforms all model-free competitors while substantially narrowing the gap to model-based ones. We further introduce NovaSplat-Lite, an inference variant that is $3.1\times$ faster at negligible accuracy cost. Code will be made available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[ActionUNet: Improving Robustness of VLA Models with Efficient Multi-scale Fine-tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34982)**  `arXiv:2609.34982`  `cs.CV` `cs.RO`  
  _Di Zhu, Ziheng Yan, Fang Wan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models have shown great promise for robotic manipulation by mapping multi-modal semantics to physical actions. However, this mapping inherently struggles to align these coarse-grained semantics with fine-grained temporal execution. It leaves VLA models with limited generalization and insufficient robustness in cluttered environments. To overcome this issue, we propose ActionUNet, an efficient multi-scale fine-tuning framework that enhances pre-trained VLA models with minimal computational cost. ActionUNet first constructs a lightweight temporal U-Net within the temporal-aligned action feature space to fuse hierarchical structural priors, effectively bridging the scale gap between semantics and temporal executions. Recognizing that multi-scale modeling can disrupt microscopic temporal continuity and cause mechanical oscillations, ActionUNet then employs a conditional SIREN as a continuous action decoder. Equipped with explicit second-order smoothness constraints, this decoder guarantees temporal continuity and reduces high-frequency motion jitter. By smoothing temporal discontinuities from multi-scale fusion, this continuous formulation reduces mechanical execution failures while preserving the base VLA model's generalization and manipulation robustness. Extensive experiments on RoboTwin 2.0 and LIBERO-Plus benchmarks, together with real-world hard evaluations, demonstrate that ActionUNet significantly improves {\pi}0.5 success rates by absolute 9.8%, 6.1%, and 11.4%, respectively, while also generalizing to the regression-based OpenVLA-OFT backbone, highlighting its effectiveness and efficiency as a fine-tuning strategy. Code and implementation details are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[NTR: Neural Token Reconstruction for Scene Token Bottleneck in End-to-End Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.31116)**  `arXiv:2605.31116`  `cs.CV` `cs.RO`  
  _Jiahui Li, Jiawei Sun, Zixiang Ren, Ming Liu, Jiamin Shi, Ruiteng Zhao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent perception-free end-to-end (E2E) autonomous driving methods bypass explicit perception outputs by compressing dense image patch tokens into compact scene tokens for downstream trajectory generation and scoring. While these scene tokens form a compact visual bottleneck for the planner, they receive supervision solely from the planning objective, providing limited constraints on the encoded visual information. To address this limitation, we introduce Neural Token Reconstruction (NTR), a representation learning framework to directly constrain the compact scene-token bottleneck in perception-free driving. NTR introduces a self-distillation masked latent reconstruction objective that reconstructs masked patch-level latent features using only compact scene tokens as reconstruction memory. This forces reconstruction gradients to pass exclusively through the scene-token bottleneck, encouraging scene tokens to preserve richer and less redundant visual representations for planning. We further introduce semantic priors derived from foundation-model annotations as a weak semantic interface biasing reconstruction targets toward driving-related structures without introducing explicit perception heads. All auxiliary reconstruction components are removed at inference time, leaving the deployed planner unchanged. NTR achieves state-of-the-art performance on three public autonomous driving benchmarks, including 8.0461 RFS on Waymo E2E and 94.1 PDMS / 90.9 EPDMS on NavSim1&2. The learned scene tokens exhibit lower pairwise redundancy and higher effective rank, indicating that effective bottleneck supervision improves both compact visual representation learning and planning performance.
  </details>

- **[sDMD: Sinkhorn Distribution Matching Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39889)**  `arXiv:2610.39889`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Jaihoon Kim, Lingxiao Li, Soshi Shimada, Roshni Cooper, Minhyuk Sung, Yiwei Zhao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Distribution Matching Distillation (DMD) distills multi-step diffusion models into few-step generators by minimizing reverse KL divergence. Despite its strong performance, DMD guides samples by local density alone, which introduces two limitations: teacher scores can be unreliable in regions of low teacher density, and the mode-seeking behavior of reverse KL can collapse the student onto a subset of the teacher modes. We first connect DMD to Wasserstein gradient flow, showing that its gradient follows from a fixed-point formulation with integral KL (IKL) as the energy functional. This perspective motivates augmenting IKL with an optimal transport term that addresses these limitations by guiding outlier samples toward the target and promoting mode coverage. We realize this term using Sinkhorn divergence estimated from finite batches. In practice, however, errors in the estimated Sinkhorn velocity can perturb in-domain samples for which IKL guidance is already accurate. To this end, we introduce sDMD, which adaptively activates the Sinkhorn correction based on discriminator outputs, focusing on outlier samples where IKL may be unreliable. With 2--4 sampling steps on FLUX.1-dev, Qwen-Image-20B, and Wan2.1-14B, sDMD outperforms distillation baselines on GenEval, DPG, WISE, and VBench while improving sample diversity over DMD2.
  </details>

- **[MedCore: Boundary-Preserving Medical Core Pruning for MedSAM](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.13688)**  `arXiv:2605.13688`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Cenwei Zhang, Suncheng Xiang, Lei You_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Medical segmentation foundation models such as SAM and MedSAM provide strong prompt-driven segmentation, but their image encoders are still too large for many clinical settings. Compression is also risky in medicine because a model can keep high Dice while losing boundary fidelity. We propose MedCore, a structured pruning framework for MedSAM. The main idea is to preserve two kinds of structures: structures that became important during SAM-to-MedSAM adaptation, and structures that have high boundary leverage. We identify the first type by a dual-intervention score comparing zeroing a group with resetting it to its original SAM weights. We identify the second type by boundary-aware Fisher estimation. We also introduce a boundary leverage principle, which relates first-order boundary displacement to boundary logit perturbation divided by the spatial gradient norm. This principle explains why boundary metrics can degrade even when Dice remains high. On polyp segmentation benchmarks, MedCore reduces parameters by 60.0% and FLOPs by 58.4% while achieving Dice 0.9549, Boundary F1 0.6388, and HD95 5.14 after post-pruning fine-tuning. It also reaches 86.6% parameter reduction and 90.4G FLOPs, retaining Dice and HD95 near the fine-tuned MedSAM reference, with lower BF1. Our analysis shows that MedSAM lies in a head-fragile boundary regime: the median 95th-percentile boundary leverage is 2.887 times as high for head-pruning as for MLP-pruning steps, consistent with BF1 and HD95 degradation. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[KamonBench: A Grammar-Based Dataset for Evaluating Compositional Factor Recovery in Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.13322)**  `arXiv:2605.13322`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Richard Sproat, Stefano Peluchetti_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Kamon (family crests) are an important part of Japanese culture and a natural test case for compositional visual recognition: each crest combines a small number of symbolic choices, but the space of possible descriptions is sparse. We introduce KamonBench, a grammar-based image-to-structure benchmark with 20,000 synthetic composite crests and auxiliary component examples. Each composite crest is paired with a formal kamon description language (kamon yogo) description, a segmented Japanese analysis, an English translation, and a non-linguistic program code. Because each synthetic crest is generated from known factors, namely container, modifier, and motif, KamonBench supports evaluation beyond caption-level accuracy: direct program-code factor metrics, controlled factor recombination splits, counterfactual motif-sensitivity groups under fixed container-modifier contexts, and linear probes of factor accessibility. We include baseline results for a ViT encoder with a Transformer decoder, two VGG n-gram decoders with and without learned positional masks, and fine-tuned Qwen3-VL-4B and Qwen3-VL-8B models. KamonBench therefore provides a controlled testbed for sparse compositional visual recognition and factor recovery in vision-language models.
  </details>

- **[RAID: A Dataset for Testing the Adversarial Robustness of AI-Generated Image Detectors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.03988)**  `arXiv:2506.03988`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Hicham Eddoubi, Jonas Ricker, Federico Cocchi, Angelo Sotgiu, Maura Pintor, Marcella Cornia, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI-generated images (AIGIs) have reached a level of quality at which humans are unable to reliably distinguish them from real images. To counter the inherent risks of fraud and disinformation, their detection is a pressing challenge and an active research topic. While many of the presented methods claim high detection accuracy, they are often evaluated without considering their adversarial robustness. In real-world scenarios, malicious actors can modify AIGIs, e.g., through imperceptible perturbations, to evade detection systems. In this work, we provide a dataset to assess the robustness of AI-generated image detectors. We present RAID (Robust evaluation of AI-generated image Detectors), a benchmark dataset of 216k diverse and transferable adversarial images. The proposed dataset is created starting from images generated by four different text-to-image models (to promote diversity) and optimizing attacks against an ensemble of seven state-of-the-art detectors (to promote transferability). Extensive experiments show that our methodology generates adversarial images that transfer to unseen detectors, degrading the performance of every detector outside the attack ensemble that we evaluate, and provides a practical, worst-case robustness screen: failure on RAID is a strong indicator that a detector is not adversarially robust. We release our dataset atthis https URLand evaluation code atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[It Takes Two to Probe, Thousands to Retrieve: Putting Vision--Language Probes to the Retrieval Test](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40124)**  `arXiv:2610.40124`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL`  
  _Ivana Beňová, Michal Gregor, Marián Šimko_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision--language models (VLMs) are tested for fine-grained understanding with image--text matching probes, a model must prefer the correct image or caption over handcrafted negative. In retrieval, the correct item must beat thousands of alternatives. We measure how these two evaluation methods relate by using two probing benchmarks, \svo{} and SugarCrepe, for retrieval tasks. For 100 image and caption queries we judge the top-10 retrievals from full dataset of six VLMs, with human annotators and an LLM assessor. We than compare how well models rank pool of items on the same queries, while changing only where the negatives comes from: the benchmark, a random draw, or the judged-incorrect retrievals of other models. On \svo{} the accuracy of retrieving a positive over handcrafted negatives is significantly higher than for retrieved ones (the correct item is preferred in 85\% against 77\% of the time). Using pool of ten retrieved negatives leads to same accuracy as when one uses pool of 200 to 430 random negatives. On SugarCrepe the order is reversed: its handcrafted negatives are harder than the retrieved ones. Incomplete relevance labels explain most of the low retrieval scores on \svo{}, where the top-ranked item is correct for 54--90\% of the queries under human labels against 6--21\% under the benchmark's labels. However, we detect no association between passing the probe for a query and retrieving a correct item first. The LLM assessor o3 agrees with individual annotators ($\kappa$ 0.57--0.61) slightly less than they agree with each other (0.64--0.68).
  </details>

- **[Evaluating Cultural Knowledge Editing in Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39602)**  `arXiv:2610.39602`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL`  
  _Malvina Nikandrou, Georgios Pantazopoulos, Ioannis Konstas, Alessandro Suglia_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Current Vision-Language Models (VLMs) frequently fail to recognize culture-specific concepts, especially from underrepresented regions. Effectively injecting this long-tail knowledge into existing models remains a challenge. In this work, we investigate knowledge editing as a targeted alternative by introducing CulturalBind, a benchmark comprising 3.2K images of 1K concepts from 6 domains, designed to evaluate culture-aware cross-modal knowledge as well as concept-level knowledge editing in VLMs. Pre-edit evaluation reveals that textual and visual knowledge is decoupled: models often demonstrate text-based knowledge of cultural concepts but fail to identify them from images. We then evaluate editing methods in single and sequential scenarios. Although current methods successfully correct individual visual recognition errors, they exhibit low cross-modal portability, failing to integrate edits into the model's broader parametric knowledge. Furthermore, performance degrades rapidly under sequential editing, highlighting the need for comprehensive adaptation approaches that better consolidate multimodal knowledge over time.
  </details>

- **[Diagnosing the Reference Gap: What Visual Scaffolds Can and Cannot Fix in Multimodal Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39419)**  `arXiv:2610.39419`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL`  
  _Guizhen Chen, Weiwen Xu, Wotao Yin, Anh Tuan Luu, Hou Pong Chan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal large language models can recognize visual content yet struggle to reason reliably over it across multiple steps. In counting, a model may see all objects but lose track of which ones it has counted. We argue that such failures, commonly attributed to perception, reveal a reference gap: the failure to bind language to the relevant visual entities, regions, or locations. Motivated by the theory of visual routines, we test whether external references such as numbering can narrow this gap. We introduce ReGap-Bench, a paired benchmark of five visual reasoning tasks in which the scene, question, and answer are fixed and only the reference varies. Results show that task-matched references improve accuracy by up to 25\%, while mismatched references provide little benefit or even hurt performance. These findings identify the reference gap as a bottleneck distinct from visual perception and show that constructing task-appropriate visual references can serve as a training-free visual skill for improving multimodal reasoning.
  </details>

- **[AskStream: When and What to Ask Proactively under Uncertainty in Streaming Video Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38678)**  `arXiv:2610.38678`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL`  
  _Lu Wang, Zhuoran Jin, Longxiang Wang, Junhong Chen, Yupu Hao, Yao Xu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in multimodal large language models have increased interest in interaction paradigms for video understanding. The evolution from offline to streaming and proactive interaction has gradually shifted models from passive responders toward more proactive agents that can decide when to answer. However, existing proactive paradigms typically assume that the information required to answer a query is complete, leaving models to only wait or answer. In the real world, however, information is often incomplete due to uncertainty from either the video or the user. In proactive paradigms, such uncertainty can cause the model to wait forever or generate highly hallucinatory responses. We therefore argue that proactive interaction should be redefined with a third action: ask, allowing models to actively ask for missing information. To study when and what models should ask before answering, we introduce AskStream, the first multi-turn benchmark spanning video and user uncertainty for streaming video understanding, with four task categories and eight subtasks. It contains 438 videos with 2,714 manually annotated frame-level time windows across three types: wait, ask, and answer. Across both multiple-choice and open-ended settings, even the strongest model attains only 52.18% on our proposed Ask-F1 metric, highlighting substantial limitations in proactive information seeking under uncertainty.
  </details>

- **[Cross-Primitive Consistency in Jev](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38623)**  `arXiv:2610.38623`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL`  
  _Xu Liu, Guikun Chen, Kanzhi Wu, Wenguan Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Jev enables efficient decision-making with large language models (LLMs) through typed querying primitives, allowing the same semantic state to be expressed in different formats, such as binary and multiple-choice queries. However, the consistency of decisions across primitives has not been systematically evaluated. This work introduces PrimaCons, a four-axis benchmark for assessing cross-primitive consistency while preserving the semantics of the query. Evaluations across three Jev sizes reveal consistency violations across all four axes. Our analysis shows that these violations follow systematic patterns and that cross-primitive discrepancies predict decision failure with a ROC-AUC of 0.91. To mitigate these inconsistencies, we propose the Cross-Primitive Residual Calibrator (CPRC), which attaches a lightweight residual network to a frozen backbone and jointly optimizes predictive accuracy and cross-primitive consistency. Under a leave-one-domain-out protocol, CPRC reduces cross-primitive inconsistency by 58.57% and improves accuracy by +2.97%., demonstrating its effectiveness in both in-domain and out-of-domain settings. Our findings establish cross-primitive consistency as a key dimension for evaluating Jev and provide a practical approach to improving the reliability of its decisions. Code and benchmark is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[PRISM-BENCH: Diagnosing Grounded and Faithful Multimodal Reasoning Beyond Final-Answer Accuracy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36554)**  `arXiv:2610.36554`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL`  
  _Vansh Bhatia, Mehdi Rezagholizadeh, Parsa Ashrafi Fashi, Igor Gilitschenski, Vikram Appia, Emad Barsoum_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Final-answer accuracy reports whether a vision-language model was right, but not whether its answer was grounded in the image, whether its stated reasoning reflects that evidence, or what the answer cost to produce. Those are the properties that decide whether a system can be deployed. We present PRISM-BENCH, a benchmark of 1,000 expert-curated questions spanning five under-represented reasoning categories, in which every item carries an idealized reasoning trace written by one human annotator and validated by another. The traces support two diagnostics that accuracy cannot provide. A perception probe, in which the model answers from a description it wrote itself, attributes roughly 85% of frontier-model failures to misperception and places the bottleneck at grounding. Trace alignment predicts answer correctness for all 25 systems tested (macro AUROC 0.66) and survives length control, so it measures reasoning content and not verbosity. We further introduce CORE, an efficiency-aware metric that reorders 24.2% of model pairs relative to accuracy while remaining uncorrelated with response length. Across 25 open and closed systems, including GPT-5 and Claude Opus 4.6, the best accuracy is 66.0%. On the four categories with a human baseline the best system trails humans by 18.4 points, and recent frontier gains fall on recognition and not on spatial grounding.
  </details>

- **[Learning to Read Braille: Benchmarking and Improving Braille Recognition Capabilities of VLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36195)**  `arXiv:2610.36195`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL`  
  _Kartik Jangra, Atharva Gawane, Siddharth Pulipaka, Hemachandran Karah, Raj Dabre_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Despite rapid progress in visual understanding and document transcription, modern vision-language models (VLMs) struggle with a fundamental accessibility task: reading Braille from images. We introduce BrailleBench, a manually curated benchmark of 259 real-world Grade-1 and Grade-2 Braille images. We evaluate proprietary, open-weight, OCR-specialized, and tool-assisted multimodal systems and find that standalone general-purpose models perform poorly at directly transcribing Braille images, whereas tool-assisted systems perform much better. We design a synthetic Braille generation pipeline with diverse layouts and image perturbations. A 2B VLM model trained on our synthetic data reaches 87% exact match on Grade-1 and 61% on Grade-2 BrailleBench, outperforming standalone VLMs and achieving the highest Grade-2 exact match score among the evaluated systems. We further show that high accuracy on synthetic validation data does not guarantee high accuracy on real photographs in our experiments: models trained on prior synthetic data reach up to 96% on their synthetic validation set but at most 3.26% on Grade-1 BrailleBench. Our results identify an accessibility-relevant capability gap in multimodal systems and show that targeted synthetic supervision can improve real-world Braille transcription.
  </details>
