- **[MER-Bench: Benchmarking Multimodal Emotional Reappraisal for Image-Text Memes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.15020)**  `arXiv:2603.15020`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL`  
  _Yiqi Nie, Fei Wang, Junjie Chen, Kun Li, Yudi Cai, Dan Guo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal Emotional Reappraisal (MER) aims to shift affective interpretation by rewriting and editing a situation without altering its underlying semantic context. Despite advances in controllable editing and sentiment transfer, MER remains challenging under coupled multimodal constraints, especially in internet memes where images, embedded text, and layout jointly encode affective meaning. To bridge this gap, we present MER-Bench, a systematic benchmark for MER on image-text memes, containing over 3,000 real-world memes with fine-grained annotations for emotion transformation and structure-preserving multimodal editing. MER-Bench spans diverse visual, textual, and structural settings and is paired with a structured MLLM-as-a-Judge evaluation suite, comprising eight fine-grained metrics and three aggregated objectives for emotional alignment, content fidelity, and transformation strength. Extensive evaluation of 14 advanced multimodal models reveals that: (1) current models struggle to jointly balance affect, semantics, structure, and text; (2) strong visual editing does not imply effective MER, with poor embedded text rendering and affect-aware rewriting remaining key bottlenecks. Our results identify MER as a critical capability gap in current multimodal generation systems and highlight affective image-text reasoning as a key challenge for future work. Project Page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[WorldFlow: Joint Video and 3D Motion Prediction via Large-Scale Pretraining](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39278)**  `arXiv:2610.39278`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.RO`  
  _Yuhao Lu, Jiongxu Chen, Hexiang Wei, Jiajin Wu, Juelin Li, Mingjian Liang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Predicting future physical states stands as a central challenge in embodied intelligence. Existing video world models leave 3D dynamics implicit, action-based formulations remain tied to specific robot configurations, and 3D motion prediction models use flow-only supervision, providing a limited learning signal for physical dynamics. To circumvent these limitations, we introduce \method{}, a generative world model that jointly predicts future video and query-based 3D trajectories. By parameterizing 3D motion as continuous displacement tokens aligned with the video grid, a unified Mixture-of-Transformers (MoT) backbone jointly denoises both modalities under a rectified-flow objective. This formulation couples explicit geometric dynamics with visual evolution, effectively unlocking the physical commonsense embedded within pretrained video models. To empirically scale and validate this unified architecture, we train \method{} on a heterogeneous mixture of 1.44 million object-level samples spanning simulated and real-world domains. We further introduce WorldFlowBench, comprising 1,678 evaluation samples from 11 data sources across simulated and real-world interactions, including spatial reasoning tasks. Across all three evaluation sets, WorldFlow achieves the lowest average displacement error (ADE) and final displacement error (FDE) among the evaluated learning-based methods. Notably, on the challenging SpatialCog set, WorldFlow achieves an ADE of 0.060 m, a 54.2\% reduction compared with the strongest external baseline. It also reduces ADE and FDE by 55.9\% and 68.5\%, respectively, compared with its flow-only variant. Project page:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[GlanceWAM: Sparse Test-Time Imagination for World-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23927)**  `arXiv:2608.23927`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.RO`  
  _Linhan Wang, Zijian An, Mingyuan Zhang, Chen Dai, Yi Xu, Can Cui, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Video generative models provide rich physical priors for robot learning, yet existing world-action models (WAMs) face a fundamental trade-off: synchronous video generation at control rate is latency-prohibitive, while abandoning test-time visual imagination sacrifices task success. We show that visual imagination achieves both real-time inference and superior success rates when generated asynchronously off the critical path and consumed directly in latent space. We introduce GlanceWAM, which decouples imagination from control on a single shared video DiT backbone: an asynchronous proposer glances ahead on a slow clock to imagine a single lookahead frame seconds into the future in the background, while an action head decodes action chunks at control rate (48 ms) purely in latent space without blocking. Enabled by a non-interfering attention mask that isolates video representations and staleness-robust horizon training that accommodates asynchronous lookahead aging, GlanceWAM breaks the speed-success dilemma. Trained purely on demonstrations, it attains 72.2% on the 24-task RoboCasa kitchen benchmark (vs. 67.1% for synchronous Cosmos Policy) and 99.0% on LIBERO while cutting per-chunk control latency 24x relative to synchronous world-action models (48 ms on one A100). In single-arm and bimanual real-robot manipulation, it achieves higher average success than $\pi_{0.5}$ without any robot-data pretraining. Code is available atthis https URLand real-robot videos atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Luggage re-identification: A study of strategies towards a real-world application](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40643)**  `arXiv:2610.40643`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Quentin Dhôte, Romaric Audigier_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Luggage re-identification (ReID) is a critical task in video surveillance for forensics, security or border management (suspicious or abandoned luggage recognition), as well as luggage management (lost luggage recovery). It enables the association of observations of the same luggage across disjoint camera views in complex environments such as airports, train stations and border crossing. While existing approaches primarily focus on appearance-based cropped image matching under relatively clean conditions, real-world scenarios are characterized by frequent occlusions, cluttered background and crowded scenes which significantly degrade performance. Yet, strategies leveraging the complementarity of multiple shots and views from different video streams is overlooked. In this paper, we study strategies for an end-to-end pipeline that operates from multiple video streams to detect and re-identify luggage. We evaluate different variants on a benchmark dataset acquired in real-world environments to address the questions of selecting and merging different views and shots of the same luggage as well as training on different environment datasets for an overall ReID performance improvement in practical applications.
  </details>

- **[MetaRefine: Score Refinement with Multi-Level Features for Image Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40487)**  `arXiv:2610.40487`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Chuanzhi Xu, Chengkun Yue, Haolan Guo, Xuanhua Yin, Boyu Wei, Zihan Deng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  An image's original dimensions, aspect ratio, and composition provide important cues that can affect its technical and aesthetic quality. Image assessment methods use resizing, cropping, padding, or patch sampling to accommodate different input sizes. However, these operations can alter or obscure original geometric and compositional cues. Existing assessment models also encode rich multi-level visual evidence that can be further extracted and exploited to improve existing models. This motivates us to combine visual evidence with original-image geometry for score refinement without changing the original scoring pipelines. In this paper, we introduce MetaRefine, a metadata-aware score refinement architecture whose lightweight residual heads predict score corrections from geometry, multi-level visual features, and the frozen model's original score. Across 100 configurations on 21 datasets, MetaRefine improves PLCC in 88 configurations and SRCC in 85, with average relative gains of 7.55% and 7.10%, respectively. It reduces MAE in 90 configurations and RMSE in 89, with average relative reductions of 23.27% and 21.93%, respectively. The median parameter overhead is only 1.11%. MetaRefine can further improve state-of-the-art (SOTA) assessment models on multiple benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[Localizing Concepts in Visual Autoregressive Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40061)**  `arXiv:2610.40061`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Nanxiang Jiang, Yang Liu, Yuanhao Wang, Min Xu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Understanding the internal routing of visual concepts is crucial for the safe and controllable adaptation of generative models. While concept localization has been widely studied in diffusion models, the emerging paradigm of next-scale visual autoregressive models remains largely unexplored. In this paper, we introduce LoCo, the first model-agnostic framework to localize where and when specific concepts emerge within autoregressive models. Driven by the native coarse-to-fine nature of next-scale generation, our method precisely maps conceptual knowledge across three distinct dimensions: Layer, Scale, and Position. To systematically localize and evaluate concept routing without context bias, we propose LoCoBench, a comprehensive dataset spanning 10 diverse categories. Extensive probing on image and video autoregressive models like Infinity, HunyuanImage-3.0, and InfinityStar shows that the localized positions are both interpretable and causally related to concept emergence. Building on these insights, we apply our localization method to three key applications: concept erasure, model personalization, and adversarial concept injection. Experiments demonstrate that our targeted intervention achieves state-of-the-art performance, substantially reducing computational overhead while preserving benign utility. Overall, our findings offer insights into how conceptual knowledge is routed during autoregressive generation, introducing a practical pathway for more interpretable, efficient, and secure adaptation.
  </details>

- **[Spectral Invariant Fusion and Refinement for Domain Generalized Semantic Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39926)**  `arXiv:2610.39926`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Yansong Bai, Yunzhi Zhuge, Xinzhuo Yu, Lu Zhang, Jin Zhan, Pingping Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Domain generalized semantic segmentation is challenging because it requires accurate dense prediction on unseen domains without any access to target-domain data during training. Existing methods improve transferability through data diversification, invariant learning, or direct use of foundation models, but they often struggle to simultaneously suppress domain-sensitive interference and preserve fine-grained spatial cues. In this work, we propose SIFR, a frequency-aware dual-stream framework that tackles domain shift at both the fusion and decoding stages. SIFR uses spectral modeling as a unified principle to regulate both heterogeneous prior interaction and dense decoder refinement. Instead of directly fusing frozen CLIP and DINOv2 features, SIFR performs spectral-guided invariant fusion to filter out domain-sensitive responses while preserving complementary semantic and structural information. In addition, SIFR incorporates a spectral decoder refinement module that stabilizes multi-scale decoder features through low-frequency statistics-based normalization and adaptive frequency selection, thereby reducing the propagation of residual domain bias in dense prediction. This design enables SIFR to jointly improve domain robustness and segmentation precision under severe distribution shifts. Extensive experiments on multiple DGSS benchmarks, including synthetic-to-real, real-to-real, and adverse-weather settings, demonstrate consistent improvements over previous state-of-the-art methods.
  </details>

- **[Aligning Medical Vision Language Models at the Capability Boundary with Entropy Adaptive Rubric Grounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39835)**  `arXiv:2610.39835`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Hongyu Bai, Jiaqi Zeng, Chunzheng Zhu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Medical diagnostic reasoning requires verifiable links between visual evidence and clinical knowledge, yet current medical vision language models are aligned with objectives that reward the wrong behavior. Uniform supervised fine tuning lets easy cases dominate updates while clinically critical boundary cases saturate prematurely. Sparse rewards based only on final-answer correctness cannot assess anatomical grounding or clinical logic, inviting reward hacking through answer shortcuts. We propose MedCBR, a two-stage alignment framework coupling boundary-aware preference optimization with rubric-grounded reinforcement learning. First, we construct counterfactual boundary preferences at the model's capability frontier using empirical pass rates and preference pairs that share reasoning prefixes but diverge at decisive diagnostic branches. Difficulty Weighted Boundary Preference Optimization (DBPO) concentrates learning on this calibrated boundary region. Second, we distill reusable clinical rubrics from boundary trajectories and activate them according to prediction entropy. Entropy Gated Grounded Policy Optimization (EGPO) rewards answer correctness, anatomical grounding, and reasoning fidelity while suppressing rubric noise on reliable samples. A reference policy anchored to the boundary-aligned model couples population-level boundary priors with instance-level uncertainty, making diagnostic reasoning more accurate and clinically grounded. Experiments on six medical benchmarks show consistent improvements over strong general, specialized, and reinforcement learning baselines, reaching 61.2% average accuracy with the largest gains on challenging differential diagnosis tasks. Ablations show super-additive gains: combining the two stages exceeds the sum of their individual improvements.
  </details>

- **[Where Do Vision Language Models Fail at 3D Reasoning?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39639)**  `arXiv:2610.39639`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Khai Le-Duc, Nguyen Kim Hai Bui, An Ngo, Nam D. Hoang, Yueqi Song, Jiarui Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language models can get individual judgments right while struggling to recover and consistently use the exact properties of a 3D object. To characterize this gap, we introduce BedBench, a controlled benchmark of thirteen procedurally generated tasks spanning individual judgments and composite answers. Across twelve open-weight and four frontier models, per-color counting exposes a pronounced weakness: humans achieve 93.3% accuracy, compared with 7.2% for the best model. Targeted fine-tuning raises one model's accuracy from 3.0% to 68.6%, but reveals a further difficulty: its answers about the most common colors remain inconsistent with its own per-color counts. Supplying those counts as text improves its judgments, showing that recovering a property does not mean using it consistently. Reasoning, scaling, and added visual cues each improve individual judgments, but none makes exact answers reliable. To locate the difficulty, we probe Qwen3.5-27B: exact per-color counts remain recoverable at the language model's input, yet the model's answers rarely recover them (65.0% for the probe vs. 3.0% for the model). Image-removal controls further show that some apparent successes require no visual evidence. These findings motivate evaluating 3D reasoning by exact correctness, consistent use, and image dependence, rather than accuracy alone. Code and dataset are publicly available:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Intrinsic Distribution Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39633)**  `arXiv:2610.39633`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Deyuan Liu, Yansen Han, Kaiwen Zheng, Jun Xie, Jiacheng Liu, Zhenglin Cheng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  One-step and few-step text-to-image generation must accurately render the details specified in the prompt despite limited opportunities for refinement. Distribution matching distillation (DMD) uses score differences to bring the generated distribution closer to a reference distribution. Following prior work, we estimate this difference with internal velocity branches, avoiding a separate fake-score network. We ask whether an explicit moment constraint improves this learned score supervision. A decomposition of the reverse-KL field into a Gaussian moment component and a non-Gaussian residual identifies statistics that can be constrained explicitly. We introduce Intrinsic Distribution Matching (IDM), which retains the full DMD correction and matches feature means and per-coordinate standard deviations using the generator's exponential-moving-average (EMA) copy, requiring no external feature encoder. Experiments use full-parameter and low-rank adaptation (LoRA) tuning on Z-Image-Turbo 6B and Qwen-Image 20B. At two network evaluations, IDM reaches 94.25% and 90.20% on Chinese and English LongText-Bench, the highest scores among the compared methods at this budget. Controlled ablations show gains from the added moment loss, while generator EMA features perform competitively with an external DINOv2 encoder across the five reported metrics. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[SymKL: A Unified Representation-Space Measure of Signal Degradation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39403)**  `arXiv:2610.39403`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Dhananjaya Jayasundara, Sudarshan Rajagopalan, Vishal M. Patel_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Quantifying degradation between a pristine signal and its degraded observation is fundamental to signal processing, yet existing full-reference measures are inherently modality-specific. We introduce SymKL, a unified full-reference degradation measure based on implicit neural representations (INRs). An INR is fitted once to the pristine reference and characterized by its diagonal Fisher information, which captures the local information geometry of the learned representation. Evaluating the Fisher information with the pristine reference and a degraded observation yields two parameter-sensitivity profiles whose symmetric KL divergence defines the degradation score. The proposed formulation requires only the reference and degraded observation, without knowledge of the corruption process or degradation severity, making it applicable across images, audio, and 3D data. We further derive an exact closed-form expression for the diagonal Fisher of SIREN, enabling efficient computation. Experiments on image, speech, and volumetric 3D benchmarks demonstrate that SymKL accurately tracks degradation severity, remains monotonic where conventional metrics saturate or fail, and provides a unified modality-agnostic measure of signal degradation. Code will be made publicly available after the review process.
  </details>

- **[DeCoRL: Decomposed Consistency Reinforcement Learning for Multi-Image Composition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39265)**  `arXiv:2610.39265`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Zhiqiang Wu, Shuang Sun, Jiale Zhang, Jing Li, Yibo Chen, Xu Tang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-image composition (MICo) is a critical yet challenging capability for large-scale image editing models, where consistency degrades rapidly as the number of reference images increases. While supervised fine-tuning (SFT) can equip models with basic MICo ability, it provides only implicit supervision on the composed image and often fails to learn reliable object-level correspondence across multiple references. We propose DeCoRL, a reinforcement-learning (RL) framework that improves MICo consistency by decomposing holistic MICo consistency into object-level consistency. DeCoRL first establishes instruction-relevant object correspondences across reference images and localizes the corresponding regions in the generated image. It then uses a VLM-based reward model trained on single-object consistency annotations to produce fine-grained, object-wise feedback, which is aggregated into a multi-object consistency reward to guide RL post-training. We further introduce DeCo-Bench, a benchmark covering compositions from 2 to 6 reference images. Experiments on DeCo-Bench and the public MICo-Bench demonstrate consistent improvements in MICo consistency.
  </details>

- **[inkGround: Benchmarking Fine-grained Visual Grounding Capabilities of Vision-Language Models for Cursive Handwriting in the Wild](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38720)**  `arXiv:2610.38720`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Sidra Hanif_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Human handwriting is produced as an ordered sequence of pen movements, but an offline image keeps only the final ink. Handwriting trajectory recovery aims to restore the ordered stroke points and their pen-up/pen-down transitions. Existing image-trajectory pair datasets are recorded with digitizing tablets under controlled conditions, which limits writer coverage and stylistic diversity. Moreover, there is no benchmark that measures the visual grounding capabilities of general-purpose Vision-Language Models (VLMs) for the stroke trajectory recovery task. In this work, we introduce inkGround, a benchmark for stroke trajectory recovery from full-page, camera-captured handwriting. inkGround pairs 537 page images (~18.7k words, ~163k strokes) with reference trajectories produced by a pretrained offline-to-online model, filtered by OCR agreement, and verified by human raters. Because these references are pseudo-labels that represent one plausible pen trajectory, we also evaluate VLMs on the IAM-OnDB dataset, which provides recorded pen trajectories. We cast the task as text-conditioned point grounding: given a page image and a target word, the model must locate the word and output its ordered stroke points. Outputs are scored by trajectory fidelity (DTW, Chamfer distance), readability of the rendered strokes (Accurate Rate, Correct Rate), and style similarity. We benchmark four open-source and three frontier closed-source VLMs with a shared in-context prompt. Closed-source models, notably Gemini-3.1-Pro, produce legible trajectories close to the reference, and this ranking also holds on IAM-OnDB.
  </details>

- **[WeVisCut: The Whole Editing Room in One Agent From Narration to Long-Form Video](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38352)**  `arXiv:2610.38352`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Chenyu Mu, Wenming Weng, Qinrui Wang, Xingsong Ye, Hao Yu, Jun He, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Producing long-form videos from narration requires interdependent decisions that remain coherent across shots. Existing agentic editors often use multi-stage workflows driven by general models, leaving the quality-cost trade-off of compact, task-adapted policies unclear. We address this gap with WeVisCut, a long-form script-to-video system that connects tool execution with policy learning. Its agent harness maintains shared production state so that tool feedback informs subsequent decisions and repairs. Because finished videos do not expose these decisions, our four-stage data pipeline combines editing inversion with verified replay to turn recovered editing intent into executable supervision. Applied to 3,750 finished videos, this pipeline yields over 130K training examples. We use this supervision to train a single 9B policy that coordinates production and recovers from failures within the same harness. Moreover, we introduce a 100-task benchmark and five metrics assessing editing quality and delivery reliability. Under a shared tool environment, WeVisCut substantially outperforms its base model and achieves comparable performance to Claude Opus 5 while using nearly 33x fewer generated tokens per successful output minute. It also outperforms task-matched native editors on our proposed metrics.
  </details>

- **[Aligning Misaligned Benchmarks for Single-View Novel View Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38349)**  `arXiv:2610.38349`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Yang-Che Tseng, Erqun Dong, Jeong-gi Kwak, Gopal Sharma, Shakiba Kheradmand, Stephan Garbin, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While single-view novel view synthesis methods have been dramatically enhanced recently, how to quantify their performance has yet to be standardized. In this work, we demonstrate that an important factor lies in how each method's own reference frame is aligned with the so-called ground-truth pose. Existing methods often rely on test-time optimization of target poses for this alignment, which inherently is ill-posed due to the complexity of the optimization landscape of camera poses vs render accuracy, leading to methods being evaluated at an arbitrary local optimum. Some recognize this limitation and thus rely on 3D reconstructions of entire scenes using training and test views to align poses, which may be infeasible. In this work, instead, we propose to use traditional 2D-to-2D and 2D-to-3D image matching techniques to align 3D reconstructions. We also evaluate only within co-visible regions, between different views, which is the only place where a `correct' ground truth can be defined. Our evaluations show that with proper coordinate alignment, novel-view synthesis methods that use explicit 3D Gaussians achieve the state of the art, despite being previously reported in the literature to be outperformed by pure image-based 2D methods. We further provide a simple baseline that directly utilizes an off-the-shelf video generator and a feed-forward geometry estimator. Our simple baseline is already comparable to state-of-the-art methods specifically trained for novel-view synthesis, without any task-specific training.
  </details>

- **[The Prestige: Benchmarking Cognitive Visual Reasoning using Magic Tricks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38103)**  `arXiv:2610.38103`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Shuo Wen, Beixi Du, Edwin Meriaux, Junming Shi, Chloe Si, David Meger, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce The Prestige, a video understanding benchmark that probes cognitive visual reasoning through professional magic performances, where deliberate misdirection challenges models to track events and explain unexpected outcomes. The benchmark comprises open-ended questions ranging from temporal and causal understanding to robustness against adversarial questions. Evaluation of leading open-source and proprietary VLMs reveals a substantial gap between even the strongest model and human performance. We also find systematic discrepancies between human and automated grading and identify recurring model failure modes. Ablations further show that models can select correct multiple-choice answers even when they fail to generate correct explanations independently, motivating further investigations into question formats. Together, these findings highlight limitations in both VLM reasoning and its evaluation, underscoring the need for more reliable video understanding models and more rigorous benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[Can Event Detectors Leave the Broadcast Camera? Evidence from Tennis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38042)**  `arXiv:2610.38042`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _David Soeiro-Vuong, Andre-Louis Rochet_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Ball bounces and racket hits are the events from which tennis statistics are derived, and on monocular video the two are easy to confuse. We release a benchmark of 345 bounce and hit annotations at frame level on eight clips that mix broadcast, fixed elevated, phone and court-level viewpoints, with a frozen development and test split, a confusion-aware matcher, clip-level bootstrap intervals and a blind second annotation of three clips. On it we score supervised precise spotters with published broadcast weights, two zero-shot vision-language models, and RallyArb, an arbitration layer over a ball track and player poses that fits no parameter on event labels. The supervised spotters score near-perfectly on broadcast footage and almost nothing on phone and court-level footage, where the pooled bounce F1 of E2E-Spot is 0.130. RallyArb keeps a bounce F1 above 0.8 on the three phone and court-level clips, two of which were used to tune it, and finds 8 of 11 bounces on the held-out one, but it places hits too far from the contact frame to match human timing. The vision-language models detect events on every viewpoint and miss the contact frame by about 0.1 s. Fine-tuned leave-one-clip-out from its broadcast weights, the spotter reaches about 0.8 bounce F1 on two phone and court-level clips and stays at 0.2 on a grazing phone angle. The annotations, the evaluator and the frozen predictions of every scored row are released.
  </details>

- **[PairCLIP-SWA: Similarity-Guided Balancing and Stochastic Weight Averaging for Generalized Deepfake Image Detection with OpenCLIP](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37874)**  `arXiv:2610.37874`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Md. Nakib, Sudipto Sarkar, A. J. A. Jubayer Talukder, Mohammad Ariful Haque_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deepfake detectors are almost always deployed as the single checkpoint that scored best on a validation split, yet the reliability of that choice is rarely measured. We measure it with PairCLIP-SWA, a single-backbone detector that adapts an OpenCLIP ViT-L/14 visual tower by progressive block unfreezing on a class-balanced corpus built by CLIP-similarity-guided one-to-one subsampling, and averages the fine-tuning trajectory in weight space. Trained in-domain on the IEEE SP Cup 2025 (DF-Wild-Cup) benchmark under the same protocol as the strongest published three-backbone ensemble, the weight-averaged detector reaches 97.04% accuracy, 98.64% AU-ROC and a 4.07% equal error rate, against 94.46%, 97.60% and 5.00%. Its training log shows why averaging matters: over the averaged epochs the non-averaged checkpoint swings across 5.34 points of validation accuracy while its validation loss more than doubles, whereas the average is never worse and is up to 5.99 points better. On an OpenForensics-derived benchmark the same instability becomes a misranking: the checkpoint that validation accuracy selects (98.43%) scores 88.85% on a disjoint 10,905-image test partition, rejecting 21.1% of genuine photographs, while the terminal average, ranked last on validation, scores 94.76%. A study of twenty-two in-house configurations on the identical DF-Wild-Cup split, including preprocessing ablations, shows that CLIP pre-training rather than ensembling drives accuracy, that secondary face cropping costs about 34 points, and that frequency-domain inputs transfer poorly. We recommend per-class selection criteria measured off the early-stopping split.
  </details>

- **[CrowdCC: Jointly Benchmarking Corruption Robustness and Deployment Quantization in Crowd Counting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36340)**  `arXiv:2610.36340`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _S Mahmudul Hasan, Lu Peng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Crowd-counting models deployed on edge devices face two challenges: inputs are degraded along the capture and transmission pipeline, while models are often quantized to satisfy resource constraints of the edge. Yet corruption robustness and deployment quantization are typically evaluated separately. We introduce CrowdCC, a controlled benchmark that jointly evaluates these effects across 23 deployment-motivated corruptions at three severity levels, four crowd-counting datasets, and 26 models. We evaluate TensorRT engines at FP32, FP16, and post-training INT8 precision on an A100 GPU, alongside throughput and energy measurements on corresponding Jetson Orin~NX configurations. The corruptions span six families, with head annotations adjusted when geometry or visibility changes. Our results show that capture-chain corruptions consistently cause the largest degradation across evaluated models, while crowd density affects absolute and relative errors differently. Accuracy on clean images does not reliably predict performance on the corresponding corrupted images. INT8 changes corruption robustness more than FP16, without a consistent direction, and does not consistently improve latency or energy efficiency. INT8 inference efficiency is strongly associated with reformat overhead in the compiled engine, with slowdowns also observed under limited INT8 coverage. Precision changes corrupted-error rankings and robustness--cost frontiers, highlighting the need to consider corruption robustness and deployment cost jointly when selecting models and precision. The code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Joint Latent Reasoning and Observation for Agentic Video Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35229)**  `arXiv:2610.35229`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Junzhang Liu, Hao Yuan, Yiming Wu, Qi Zhang, Long Chen, Zhecan Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Effective video agents must learn not only how to answer, but also where to look next. Transferring this capability to smaller models is challenging because each observation serves two purposes: supporting an answer and guiding the search for further evidence. Textual descriptions may capture what is currently visible while omitting cues needed for subsequent acquisition. We introduce Latent Agentic Reasoning for Video Analysis (LARVA), a framework that distills multi-round video agents through jointly learned latent reasoning and visual observations. Rather than communicating through captions, an observer passes compact continuous representations to a reasoner that selects the next acquisition action or produces the final answer. Crucially, action and answer supervision propagate through this interface, training the observer to communicate information useful for downstream decisions. Observation reconstruction and reasoning-distribution alignment provide auxiliary supervision from textual teacher trajectories. Across five video-understanding benchmarks, LARVA achieves strong performance with 4B and 7B backbones. On VideoHolmes, Video-MME, and LVBench, the 7B model outperforms a textual CoT student trained on the same teacher trajectories by 5.1, 2.5, and 3.7 percentage points, respectively. These results highlight the value of jointly learning how agents represent evidence and act on it when distilling video-understanding capabilities.
  </details>

- **[MarineLife-16K: An Attribute-Rich Video-Text Dataset for Motion-Aware Marine Video Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33478)**  `arXiv:2610.33478`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Quang-Trung Truong, Thanh-Nghia Nguyen, Phu-Phong Vo, Jaeyeon Kim, Cheng Chen, Jie Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Marine video understanding is crucial for a wide range of real-world applications. However, existing marine video datasets suffer from limitations, including the limited number of data samples, fine-grained text descriptions, and detailed visual attributes. To address these limitations, we introduce a large-scale, video-text dataset featuring rich, in-the-wild visual attributes tailored for complex marine environments. Our dataset comprises 2,000 video-text pairs annotated with 13 attributes covering up to four objects per scene, object visual/geometric properties, behaviors, environmental context, and camera motion, spanning diverse lighting conditions, times of day, and 7 countries. From these annotations, we generate 16,080 video-question pairs (12,080 multiple-choice, 4,000 open-ended). Furthermore, we benchmark both proprietary and open-source video-LLM models on video question answering and video captioning tasks. We find that (i) proprietary models often achieves superior performances; (ii) VLMs handle object motion well but struggle with complex camera motion; (iii) closed-source models struggle to perceive camera motion; and (iv) VLMs struggle to reason in static cameras.
  </details>

- **[PACE: Pareto-Adaptive Compression for Efficient Native MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24005)**  `arXiv:2610.24005`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Lei Tian, Chunzheng Zhu, Kai Wu, Yifan Zhang, Haihua Yang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Native multimodal large language models (native MLLMs) jointly encode visual and linguistic sequences within a unified autoregressive Transformer, emerging as a promising paradigm for multimodal understanding. Unlike conventional ViT-based MLLMs relying on external vision encoders, ViT-free native MLLMs tokenize pixel patches directly, potentially suffering severe visual token redundancy. A pilot study confirms this: unlike ViT-based models that degrade under compression, ViT-free MLLMs tolerate aggressive compression with near-lossless or even improved performance. Yet existing compression methods target ViT-based settings, leaving native MLLMs, which stand to gain the most, unexplored. To close this gap, we introduce an analysis pipeline centered on token discriminability and cross-dimensional heterogeneous redundancy, revealing that (i) redundancy is distributed independently and heterogeneously along height and width axes, and (ii) early-layer visual tokens are indiscriminable and uniformly redundant. Guided by these findings, we propose PACE (Pareto-Adaptive Compression for Efficient Native MLLMs), the first visual token compression framework tailored for native MLLMs with Multimodal-RoPE. Leveraging dimensional decoupling of positional encoding, PACE decomposes attention into two orthogonal spatial saliency estimates along height and width, gated by a positional attention signal, yielding direction-aware importance with negligible overhead. It further casts token budget allocation as Pareto optimization over performance preservation, inference efficiency, and spatial coverage, jointly identifying optimal compression depths and retention ratios. Experiments show PACE maintains near-lossless performance at only 25%-50% visual token retention, even improving several benchmarks, while delivering over 2x FLOPs reduction.
  </details>

- **[SituHOI: Scalable Capture of Scene-Situated Full-Body Human-Object Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23980)**  `arXiv:2610.23980`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Qifan Wang, Qihang Fang, Boao Shi, Yushi Du, Pei Zhou, Siyan Dong, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Modeling human-object interaction (HOI) in real environments requires jointly representing whole-body motion, manipulated objects, and scene geometry, yet existing datasets trade off scalable capture against structured, scene-aligned supervision. We introduce SituHOI, a large-scale RGB-D dataset containing 18,429 annotated clips across 16 indoor rooms and approximately 86 hours of quality-screened activity. It pairs recordings from a single moving RGB-D camera with static scene scans, object geometry, language descriptions, and estimated human and object trajectories. Using the scans as persistent references, our pipeline combines camera localization and depth-based metric alignment with human mesh recovery, object tracking, and temporal refinement to register all geometric annotations in a shared floor-aligned frame. These data support FlexHOI-Bench, a goal-conditioned joint motion prediction benchmark with variable observation and prediction durations. Given observed histories, scene and object geometry, and a terminal object pose, models predict future human and object motion. We provide SharedHOI as a reference predictor and evaluate world-space accuracy and human-object coordination. On a MoCap subset, our pipeline reduces human G-MPJPE from 195.4 to 68.3 mm and object translation error from 43.0 to 28.7 mm. SharedHOI leads four adapted baselines on all three aggregate FlexHOI-Bench metrics. Data will be released upon acceptance. An anonymized code repository is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[CRAFT: Causal Responsibility and Failure Tracing in Medical Vision Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38810)**  `arXiv:2609.38810`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Chunzheng Zhu, Jiaqi Zeng, Hongbo Zhao, Yihang Chen, Yijun Wang, Jianxin Lin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As vision language models are increasingly deployed in clinical diagnosis, understanding how they internally resolve competing visual and textual signals becomes a safety imperative. Existing mechanistic analyses remain confined to unimodal text and offer no explanation for why a single misleading sentence can override a correct image based diagnosis, or why a model commits to a confident answer despite insufficient visual evidence. We find that these two safety risks, arbitration failure where textual context overrides visual grounding and brake failure where the model commits without adequate evidence, are mediated by spatially disjoint attention head populations: arbitration heads form a mid-to-deep wideband reflecting cross-layer evidence competition, while brake heads concentrate in a narrow middle-to-late layer band that regulates evidence sufficiency and abstention behavior. To ground these observations in causal circuitry, we introduce CRAFT, which localizes each failure mode to a minimal causal head set via dual criteria and verifies necessity and sufficiency through temporal probes and Tuned Lens trajectory analysis. Excising arbitration heads sharply reduces conflict following with negligible degradation on clean inputs, while excising brake heads restores appropriate abstention under degraded visual evidence. The two interventions target spatially disjoint head sets and produce distinct corrective effects, underscoring the mechanistic separability of the failure modes. Experiments across multiple medical VQA benchmarks and VLM architectures validate both the localization and interventions, demonstrating that the identified heads causally drive each failure mode and that targeted modulation generalises without retraining. The code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[FLORO: A Multimodal Geospatial Foundation Model for Ecological Remote Sensing Across Sensors and Scales](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28174)**  `arXiv:2605.28174`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Jorge L. Rodriguez, Victor Angulo Morales, Areej Alwahas, Mariana Elias Lara, Fida Mohammad Thoker, Kasper Johansen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Foundation models offer a promising route to transferable remote sensing representations, but many current approaches depend on very large pretraining datasets and fixed sensor configurations, limiting their suitability for ecological and environmental applications, where observations often vary across platforms, spatial and spectral resolutions, and available modalities. We introduce FLORO, a multimodal geospatial foundation model designed to learn transferable representations from a small but highly diverse remote sensing corpus. FLORO is pretrained using masked autoencoding on a heterogeneous combination of Sentinel-1, Sentinel-2, SkySAT imagery, elevation, and UAV-derived data. To accommodate sensor variability, FLORO incorporates availability-aware inputs that indicate which spectral bands and auxiliary modalities are present in each sample, enabling a unified input space across heterogeneous sensor configurations. We evaluated FLORO on the PANGAEA benchmark under a frozen-encoder protocol across scene classification, segmentation, and regression tasks. Despite being pretrained on a smaller corpus than competing foundation models, FLORO achieved strong and stable transfer across optical, optical-SAR, and optical-elevation benchmarks spanning medium-resolution satellite, airborne, and ultra-high-resolution UAV imagery. FLORO obtained the second-best average segmentation performance across six PANGAEA benchmarks, the third-best average rank across three scene-classification benchmarks, and excelled in regression tasks, while qualitative results showed improved preservation of spatial structure in flood, urban, biomass, and canopy-height prediction settings. Overall, these results position FLORO as a practical framework for ecological and environmental monitoring under heterogeneous sensing conditions.
  </details>

- **[CaC: Advancing Video Reward Models via Hierarchical Spatiotemporal Concentrating](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.11723)**  `arXiv:2605.11723`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Jiyuan Wang, Huan Ouyang, Jiuzhou Lin, Chunyu Lin, Dewen Fan, Boheng Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In this paper, we propose Concentrate and Concentrate (CaC), a coarse-to-fine anomaly reward model based on Vision-Language Models. During inference, it first conducts a global temporal scan to anchor anomalous time windows, then performs fine-grained spatial grounding within the localized interval, and finally derives robust judgments via structured spatiotemporal Chain-of-Thought reasoning. To equip the model with these capabilities, we construct the first large-scale generated video anomaly dataset with per-frame bounding-box annotations, temporal anomaly windows, and fine-grained attribution labels. Building on this dataset, we design a three-stage progressive training paradigm. The model initially learns spatial and temporal anchoring through single- and multi-frame supervised fine-tuning, and then is optimized by a reinforcement learning strategy based on two-turn Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO). Beyond conventional accuracy rewards, we introduce Temporal and Spatial IoU rewards to supervise the intermediate localization process, effectively guiding the model toward more grounded and interpretable spatiotemporal reasoning. Extensive experiments demonstrate that CaC can stably concentrate on subtle anomalies, achieving a 25.7% accuracy improvement on fine-grained anomaly benchmarks and, when used as a reward signal, CaC reduces generated-video anomalies by 11.7% while improving overall video quality.
  </details>

- **[ClueTracer: Question-to-Vision Clue Tracing for Training-Free Hallucination Suppression in Multimodal Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.02004)**  `arXiv:2602.02004`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Gongli Xi, Kun Wang, Zeming Gao, Huahui Yi, Haolang Lu, Qianshan Wei, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large multimodal reasoning models solve challenging visual problems via explicit long-chain inference: they gather visual clues from images and decode clues into textual tokens. Yet this capability also increases hallucinations, where the model generates content that is not supported by the input image or the question. To understand this failure mode, we identify \emph{reasoning drift}: during clue gathering, the model over-focuses on question-irrelevant entities, diluting focus on task-relevant cues and gradually decoupling the reasoning trace from visual grounding. As a consequence, many inference-time localization or intervention methods developed for non-reasoning models fail to pinpoint the true clues in reasoning settings. Motivated by these insights, we introduce ClueRecall, a metric for assessing visual clue retrieval, and present ClueTracer, a training-free, parameter-free, and architecture-agnostic plugin for hallucination suppression. ClueTracer starts from the question and traces how key clues propagate along the model's reasoning pathway (question $\rightarrow$ outputs $\rightarrow$ visual tokens), thereby localizing task-relevant patches while suppressing spurious attention to irrelevant regions. Remarkably, \textbf{without any additional training}, ClueTracer improves all \textbf{reasoning} architectures (including \texttt{R1-OneVision}, \texttt{Ocean-R1}, \texttt{MM-Eureka}, \emph{etc}.) by $\mathbf{1.21\times}$ on reasoning benchmarks
  </details>

- **[PhysCount: Do MLLMs Count Visual Appearances or Physical Entities?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40530)**  `arXiv:2610.40530`  `cs.CV`  
  _Lidong Lu, Jiaxin Lv, Liangrui Dong, Xinyi Mao, Lidong Zou, Zhenquan Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Images reveal appearances, but counting often asks about physical objects. A single entity may be visible directly, in several reflections, or through glass, so detecting every visible occurrence does not determine how many entities are present. Existing visual counting benchmarks rarely separate these two operations. We introduce PhysCount, a benchmark for evaluating whether multimodal large language models (MLLMs) can distinguish what appears in an image from what physically exists in the scene. PhysCount contains 546 real-world images and 4,595 human-written questions organized into 651 target-specific groups. For each group, annotators locate the relevant appearances, label how each is observed, and identify appearances of the same entity. Four complementary question families test the enumeration of appearances, the enumeration of physical entities, image-formation-specific counting, and appearance--entity correspondence. We measure both accuracy on each family and the proportion of groups answered entirely correctly. Across 40 MLLMs, counting visible appearances is substantially easier than recovering the identities that connect them. Even the strongest model answers every question correctly in only 13.36\% of groups. Performance declines when one entity produces many appearances or when its appearances cross observation modes. Higher image resolution improves access to visual evidence but brings limited gains in complete-group accuracy, whereas answering related questions jointly produces a marked improvement. These results expose a gap between recognizing what is visible and correctly recovering the physical entities behind those observations.
  </details>

- **[LucidLyra: Feed-Forward Generative 3D Scene Reconstruction and Joint Understanding via Multi-Task Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40495)**  `arXiv:2610.40495`  `cs.CV`  
  _Oliver Hahn, Rodrigo Marcuzzi, Sangeetha Grama Srinivasan, Alessandro Burzio, Sherwin Bahmani, Jiahui Huang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present LucidLyra, a framework that makes generatively reconstructed 3D scenes directly queryable for semantics and instances in a feed-forward manner from video latents. While generative 3D reconstruction can synthesize virtual environments from a single image, downstream applications in robotics and physical AI require not only visual realism but also access to scene semantics and object identities. Existing 3D understanding approaches, however, require captured multi-view scenes, have limited generalization capabilities, or rely on expensive per-scene processing and optimization. To bridge this gap, our method distills complementary knowledge from a camera-controlled video diffusion model and frozen 2D vision foundation models into a unified model to jointly infer 3D Gaussians encoding geometry, appearance, open-vocabulary semantics, and class-agnostic instance cues. The resulting scenes support novel-view rendering, open-vocabulary segmentation, and object grouping without per-scene optimization, while training requires neither manual 3D annotations nor captured multi-view data. Our method outperforms baselines combining state-of-the-art 3DGS segmentation approaches with generative 3D scene reconstruction on curated synthetic and real-image-conditioned benchmarks, while preserving reconstruction quality. Furthermore, our latent formulation naturally extends to monocular video, reconstructing structured dynamic 3D scenes across time. By turning renderable but unstructured outputs into queryable 3D worlds, LucidLyra enables downstream applications including 3D scene editing, 3D asset mining, and robotic simulation.
  </details>

- **[AVPS-360: A Spatial Audio-Visual Benchmark for Instance Segmentation and Tracking of Sound-Producing Persons](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40480)**  `arXiv:2610.40480`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yifu Sun, Parthasaarathy Sudarsanam, Tuomas Virtanen, Archontis Politis_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Audio-visual instance segmentation (AVIS) segments and tracks individual sound-producing objects in videos. Existing benchmarks predominantly use monaural audio, which lacks inter-channel cues for spatially distinguishing concurrent sound sources. We introduce AVPS-360 (Audio-Visual Person Segmentation 360), a spatial audio-visual benchmark bridging AVIS and sound event localisation and detection (SELD). Built upon STARSS23, it pairs first-order Ambisonics audio with synchronised 360° video across 230 recordings totalling 10.5 hours. Recordings average 164.0 seconds. The dataset captures intermittent sound production in real-world interactions dominated by speech, alongside laughter, clapping, and footsteps. It provides 31,678 manually reviewed instance masks linked to source identities and sound-event labels. We also present a baseline that integrates spatial audio with visual features for frame-level segmentation and identity association across frames. Experiments on AVPS-360 show that existing AVIS methods struggle with detection and identity association in long recordings with intermittent sound production, while our baseline substantially improves performance in both aspects.
  </details>

- **[InfantNet: A Large Scale Dataset for Infant Body Pose and Shape Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40467)**  `arXiv:2610.40467`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zhipeng Hui, Jiahao Wang, Xun Dong, Guofeng Zhang, Xingrui Wang, Jiawei Peng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Infant pose and shape estimation is essential for applications in childcare, developmental monitoring, and medical diagnosis. However, existing methods and datasets are largely designed for adults, and direct transfer to infants fails due to substantial differences in body proportions, articulation limits, and frequent self-occlusion. To address this gap, we introduce InfantNet, the largest real-image infant dataset to date, comprising 108,902 RGB images of infants aged 6-18 months. Each image is annotated with 2D keypoints, and a curated subset of 11,642 images additionally includes 3D pose and shape annotations with full SMIL parameters, verified through multi-view rendering and reprojection-error filtering. We use an iterative annotation pipeline to ensure high fidelity across both 2D and 3D labels. InfantNet establishes a large-scale, comprehensive benchmark for infant 2D keypoint detection and 3D pose-and-shape recovery. Baseline experiments demonstrate that state-of-the-art adult pose estimators do not generalize well to infants, whereas fine-tuning on InfantNet yields a consistent improvement. The gains are even more pronounced for 3D pose and shape estimation, where SMIL-based estimation demonstrates robust performance with accuracy comparable to results reported for SMPL-based models. By releasing the InfantNet dataset and benchmark, we provide a vital resource for advancing infant pose analysis and related healthcare applications.
  </details>

- **[A Unified Benchmark for Aerial-Enhanced Autonomous Driving: The KITScenes Aerial Dataset](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40455)**  `arXiv:2610.40455`  `cs.CV`  
  _Daniel Lengerer, Richard Schwarzkopf, Jonas Merkert, Fabian Immel, Rupert Polley, Frank Bieder, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Aerial imagery provides complementary spatial context for autonomous driving by exposing road structure beyond the immediate perception range of onboard sensors. Yet map construction from aerial imagery, aerial-assisted online HD mapping, and cross-view localization are typically studied on separate data and benchmarks. We introduce KITScenes Aerial, an extension of the KITScenes Multimodal dataset that augments synchronized onboard sensor data, vehicle poses, and rich Lanelet2 map annotations with spatially associated aerial imagery and higher-resolution neural representations, providing a common basis for a variety of research tasks. Our mapping evaluation focuses on all 53 KITScenes map classes across different model designs, spatial ranges, and aerial resolutions, while retaining compatibility with the widely used three-class setting. Representative baselines show that aerial context is particularly beneficial for fine-grained and extended-range mapping, more than doubling 53-class mAP at the larger mapping extent. Higher-resolution aerial imagery yields a further 4.3 mAP (+24 %) improvement, with gains extending to challenging map elements such as traffic lights. We additionally evaluate cross-view localization under geographically disjoint conditions, where an adapted fine-grained feature-matching baseline achieves a mean translation error of 5.13 m. KITScenes Aerial thus enables unified study of aerial and ground-level information across complementary mapping and localization tasks. Project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[PhysInsert: Physically Plausible Object Insertion with Geometric Priors and Bidirectional Object-Scene Interactions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40453)**  `arXiv:2610.40453`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yue Gong, Le Zhang, Rui Chen, Song Chang, Xiaochao Qu, Luoqi Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present PhysInsert, a framework for physically plausible reference-based object insertion that jointly adapts objects and their surrounding scenes. Existing methods often rely on masked inputs, generation-based pairs, or training pairs with limited viewpoint variation, leaving pose and illumination adaptation and bidirectional object-scene interactions insufficiently constrained. We construct the Insert World dataset with strictly aligned Blender-rendered counterfactual pairs and object references across viewpoints and lighting conditions. Building on these data, Geometry-Guided Adaptation couples a VGGT geometry stream with image and text streams under target-scene feature supervision to guide spatially consistent insertion. Complementary 2D Object-Scene Spatial Supervision jointly supervises object occupancy and environmental changes while grounding spatial instructions in object regions through image-text attention. We introduce PhysInsert-Bench with dedicated cross-environment and cross-view cases requiring illumination and pose adaptation beyond appearance copying. Experiments on PhysInsert-Bench and DreamBooth demonstrate improved image fidelity; a user study further shows more accurate placement and improved lighting integration, perspective, and pose plausibility.
  </details>

- **[Benchmarking color judgment in multimodal LLMs with CIE-computable ground truth](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40212)**  `arXiv:2610.40212`  `cs.CV`  
  _Shuwei Yue, Kaida Xiao, Zhihua Wang, Qiao Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) are now used as zero-shot judges of image quality, but their color judgments are rarely tested with colorimetric ground truth. We present a five-task benchmark based on the CIE colorimetric framework: AWB pair judgment, skin-tone bias direction, CCT estimation, CIE Delta E 2000 pair ordering, and color naming under changing illuminants. We test eight MLLM endpoints, including four Qwen3-VL variants, GLM-4.5V, GPT-5.4, Claude-Opus-4.7, and Gemini-3.1-Pro. The models often solve coarse binary AWB judgments, but they remain weak on numeric CCT estimation and on some fine color-difference judgments. On ColorChecker-24 stimuli, the three closed-weight frontier models reach CCT MAE of 715-1056 K, compared with 1358-2133 K for the five open-weight models. A non-ColorChecker control shows that this advantage is partly template-dependent. The results suggest that current MLLMs have useful color words and coarse visual priors, but their color judgments are not reliably calibrated to CIE-computable color differences.
  </details>

- **[Beyond CCT: Multi-channel SPD recovery for per-shot camera color correction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40175)**  `arXiv:2610.40175`  `cs.CV`  
  _Shuwei Yue, Hongke Wei, Qiao Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Modern cameras correct color via two or three pre-calibrated 3x3 matrices interpolated by Correlated Color Temperature (CCT). This collapses the illuminant SPD to a scalar. LEDs, fluorescents, and mixed sources sit off the Planckian locus, and metameric illuminants with the same chromaticity demand different optimal CCMs that a CCT pipeline cannot distinguish. We recover the illuminant SPD instead using non-negative least squares against a basis dictionary, conditioned on the camera white point and a multi-channel ambient-light sensor (ALS). A closed-form 3x3 CCM is then fit per shot. On a synthetic benchmark of 300 illuminants and 3,100 reflectances, mean angular error drops from 1.89 to 1.20 degrees, with a 1.7x tail reduction on the LED-like off-Planckian group (n=45) where metameric ambiguity is tangible. The gain holds under realistic ALS imperfections and observation noise, and the same off-Planckian advantage carries over to 1,684 real measured SPDs.
  </details>

- **[VideoMemBench: How Well Do MLLMs Remember in Video Conversations?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40131)**  `arXiv:2610.40131`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zhengdong Hu, Chao Wang, Kaituo Feng, Huanjin Yao, Mingfei Lu, Hehe Fan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) can now process hour-long videos, making extended multi-turn dialogue over a single video a natural mode of use. Sustaining such a dialogue requires more than answering each question in isolation: the model must retrieve and reuse information established earlier in the conversation, both what it has seen and what it has said, an ability we term in-context video memory. Existing video QA benchmarks leave this ability unmeasured: their questions are mutually independent, never reference one another, and each is answerable from the video alone, so nothing ever requires carrying information across turns. We introduce VideoMemBench, in which each sample pairs a single video with a long multi-turn dialogue, whose turns are connected by typed cross-turn dependencies: an Episodic Dependency refers back to a visual episode described in an earlier turn, probing memory of what the model has seen, while an Answer Dependency builds on the model's own earlier answer, probing memory of what the model has said. Every dependency is verified considering two properties: 1) necessity, meaning the question cannot be answered without its earlier turns, and 2) non-leakage, meaning the question does not restate the information from its earlier turns. To enforce these properties, we design a construction pipeline with three LLM roles: a planner, a generator, and a judger, with each dialogue reviewed by human experts. VideoMemBench comprises 326 videos with 2,476 turns, of which 1,387 depend on earlier turns, including multi-parent dependencies and long-range call-back. Among over 20 evaluated open- and closed-source MLLMs, Claude Opus 5 performs the best with an overall score of only 45.7\%, highlighting the challenge of in-context video memory. Our dataset and evaluation are released.
  </details>

- **[SelfThinker: Self-Evolving Reasoning via Reliability-Verified Intrinsic Visual Thoughts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40115)**  `arXiv:2610.40115`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zhangquan Chen, Ruqi Huang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Unified multimodal models can both generate and understand images within a single set of weights, offering an opportunity to use image synthesis as training supervision for visual comprehension. However, existing visual chain-of-thought methods rely on human-annotated helper images during training and require slow image generation at test time. We present SelfThinker, the first self-evolving framework that turns a unified model's self-generated visual thoughts into stronger understanding without human image annotations. Given a visual problem, SelfThinker samples candidate helper images from its generation pathway and uses its frozen understanding pathway to score each candidate via conditional pointwise mutual information. High-utility visual thoughts then refine the generator via flow matching and serve as privileged teacher context for on-policy self-distillation. At inference, SelfThinker reasons directly from the original image in text without generating intermediate images. Although trained only on small synthetic visual-search and navigation datasets, SelfThinker transfers broadly to natural-image and spatial benchmarks, raising the seven-benchmark average of ThinkMorph-7B from 52.7 to 64.0 (+11.3 points). Controlled image-perturbation probes further show that SelfThinker surpasses ThinkMorph-7B by up to 40.4 points and its pretrained BAGEL-7B backbone by up to 23.2 points across fine-grained and spatial perception tasks. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[BRACHIO: Broad Representations Across Centres for Head-and-neck Imaging Oncology (HECKTOR 2026)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40068)**  `arXiv:2610.40068`  `cs.CV`  
  _Roxanne De Maeyer, Ine Dirks, Yannick Brackenier, Simon DeKeyser, Jef Vandemeulebroucke, Vicky De Ridder_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The HECKTOR 2026 challenge benchmarks machine learning for head and neck cancer, where teams delineate primary and nodal tumour volumes, perform TN staging, and predict recurrence-free survival (RFS). This report describes the submissions for the HECKTOR challenge of team nucli. As the three tasks span both dense and global information from multicentric PET/CT scans, we utilise self-supervised pre-trained encoders, training a 2D DINOv2 encoder (PET-DINOv2-MIPs) on HECKTOR 2026's PET scans. Our final test submission ensembles a DynUNet trained from scratch with a SwinUNETR whose Swin trunk was initialised from a SimMIM-pre-trained checkpoint for segmentation, reaching a mean Dice of 0.5409. For TN staging, GTVp/GTVn geometric features from the segmentation masks are fit to stage probabilities by logistic regression (LR) and fused with PET-DINOv2-MIPs deep features (PCA + LR) via a second-level LR, reaching balanced accuracies of 0.3962 (T-stage) and 0.6432 (N-stage). For the RFS prognosis task, we fine-tune the last two blocks of the PET-DINOv2-MIPs ViT-S encoder jointly with an MLP head under a CoxPH loss, concatenating clinical covariates with the imaging features, reaching a test Harrell's C-index of 0.6577 and a weighted challenge score of 0.5802. The prognosis result is comparable to the best entry of the previous edition and consistent with our internal cross-validation, while our segmentation result is well below it and limits the staging results that depend on it.
  </details>

- **[DINOv2 for PET: A Pre-Training Domain Benchmark Across Classification, Segmentation, and Prognosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40018)**  `arXiv:2610.40018`  `cs.CV`  
  _Roxanne De Maeyer, Yannick Brackenier, Vicky De Ridder, Simon DeKeyser, Maarten Larmuseau, Jef Vandemeulebroucke_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  DINO (self-DIstillation with NO labels) has become a stan- dard for self-supervised pre-training, producing features that generalise across datasets and downstream tasks. In medical imaging it has been adopted mainly for radiology (X-ray, CT and MRI), where even natural- image DINO features transfer well. In the literature, these features have been reported to transfer worse to functional imaging such as PET. Yet PET-specific DINO models have barely been introduced, and even less validated across multiple tasks and datasets. We present PET-DINOv2-2D, the first DINOv2 model pre-trained on 2D PET, and benchmark it against open-source encoders: the origi- nal DINOv2 (pre-trained on the LVD-142M natural-image dataset) and OmniRad (pre-trained on CT, MRI and ultrasound). Using the same lightweight heads for every backbone, and despite far less pre-training data, PET-DINOv2-2D matches or outperforms both on three down- stream PET tasks: normal/abnormal classification, lesion segmentation and recurrence-free survival prognosis. Its advantage is largest when few labels are available, suggesting that PET pre-training gives more transferable, label-efficient features. Code is made available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[ElasticFormer: Detecting Objects in HRW Shots via Elastic Computing Vision Transformer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39946)**  `arXiv:2610.39946`  `cs.CV`  
  _Wenxi Li, Jingchen Huang, Chenyang Lyu, Moran Liu, Haozhe Lin, Guiguang Ding, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in gigapixel-level imaging have brought High-Resolution Wide shots to the forefront of research. However, these images present significant challenges: extreme sparsity of foreground, gigapixel-level resolutions and diverse target counts. This makes traditional close-up detectors inaccurate and slow as they are overwhelmed by the background. Although previous research has explored sparse backbones, their fixed sparsity patterns lack the adaptability required to handle diverse target numbers. To address this, we introduce ElasticFormer, a sparse backbone that dynamically allocates computational resources based on foreground proportion. After scoring windows based on variance, proposed ElasticSelector module will predict the foreground proportion for top-k selection. The mechanism guides the model to select target-containing windows, scaling resources in areas where objects are clustered. We introduce a novel loss function combined with the 3-phase training strategy for ElasticSelector, allowing it to function properly when bounding box annotations are missing. A WSOD study is carried on PASCAL VOC 2007 to evaluate its extensibility. Further, ElasticNet is created to verify its backbone-agnostic nature. In experiments on the PANDA gigapixel benchmark, ElasticFormer reduces backbone FLOPs by 80% while achieving a significant improvement in AP50 when compared to fixed-ratio sparse methods.
  </details>

- **[BiHashFormer: Hash-Driven Dual-Branch Transformer for Efficient Object Detection in HRW Shots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39937)**  `arXiv:2610.39937`  `cs.CV`  
  _Jingchen Huang, Wenxi Li, Chenyang Lyu, Moran Liu, Haozhe Lin, Yuchen Guo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in gigapixel-level imaging have brought High-Resolution Wide shots to the forefront of research. However, these images present significant challenges: extreme sparsity of foreground, gigapixel-level resolutions and interleaving of foreground and background. This causes traditional detectors and attention mechanisms to be hindered by background, resulting in inefficiency and inaccuracy. To tackle this problem, we propose BiHashFormer, a dual sparsified branch transformer built on hashing. RoI Selector will predict the foreground proportion for top-k selecting target-containing windows. The compression branch processes windows selected and refines attention into HashAttention by discarding half of the key-value pairs, reducing cost and improving efficiency. The compensation branch uses HashMiner to perform low-cost hash searches in the remaining regions, recovering features of missed objects. Experiments confirm that the selection relationship in HashAttention is one-way and reveal the preference of different queries for keys. In experiments on the gigapixel benchmark PANDA, BiHashFormer reduces 37.5% backbone FLOPs while improving AP50 to 81.0%. Moreover, this parallel dual-branch structure allows the model to avoid inference delays caused by inter-branch dependencies, while achieving much greater stability than single-branch models, specially with low rates of RoI selection.
  </details>

- **[PhysAg3D: Bridging 3D Generative and Agentic Models for High-Fidelity and Physically-Interactive Assets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39924)**  `arXiv:2610.39924`  `cs.CV`  
  _Bi'an Du, Fanpeng Meng, Jiemin Fang, Chen Yang, Pufan Li, Daizong Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Generating interactive 3D assets from a single image requires realistic appearance, fidelity to the depicted object, and plausible physical behavior. Yet detailed geometry alone does not determine how an object should move or deform. We introduce PhysAg3D, which couples 3D generative models with agentic reasoning and tool use. A vision-language agent first infers semantic parts, candidate interactions, and physical priors from the image; a 3D generator then produces a textured whole-object mesh from the same image. A hierarchical interaction representation links these revisable priors to mechanisms and the generated surfaces they control, organizing interaction construction and refinement. Guided by this representation and observations of the mesh, the agent uses geometric and physical tools to construct parts, joints, deformable regions, and surface bindings while retaining useful generated geometry and materials. Rendered interactions and execution measurements guide targeted revisions within a fixed budget. The framework accommodates both rigid articulation and compliant deformation. Quantitative and qualitative evaluations on real photographs and external benchmarks demonstrate detailed, image-aligned assets with plausible interactions. Ablations support bounded refinement and source-surface preservation. Project Page:this http URL.
  </details>

- **[WorldProbe: When Video World Models Fall Apart under Long-Horizon Spatial Exploration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39721)**  `arXiv:2610.39721`  `cs.CV`  
  _Minqi Wang, Jie Peng, Hejun Dong, Ruchang Yao, Puyi Wang, Siyuan Huang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Video world models generate future visual observations from camera or action inputs, with the goal of synthesizing persistent 3D worlds that can be explored across time and space. Existing benchmarks typically evaluate short interactions or rely on camera trajectories fixed before generation. The performance of video world models under long-horizon exploration with extensive camera motion remains under-explored. We introduce WorldProbe, a diagnostic benchmark that uses a goal-conditioned multimodal agent to adapt camera actions online during generation. WorldProbe comprises 164 test cases across four probing families. Across 10 state-of-the-art models, we generate more than 1,600 rollouts totaling approximately 800 minutes, with horizons up to 180 seconds. Our evaluation across four dimensions reveals both the degradation of local measures and the emergence of long-range inconsistencies. We find that recent models sustain visual and content quality for longer, while long-range memory and evolution remain weak. These results expose a gap between locally stable generation and persistent world consistency that is largely missed by existing benchmarks. Code and benchmark resources are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[EditAgent: Rethinking Agentic Image Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39664)**  `arXiv:2610.39664`  `cs.CV`  
  _Haiyang Xu, Tianyu Wang, Jialu Li, Jiteng Mu, Yuanjun Xiong, Jianming Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agentic image editors decompose complex requests into steps, but when a step fails, they mostly retry with the same narrow set of tools and a judge that scores each output by eye directly. We argue that such agents are bounded by two spaces: the action space of what the agent can do, and the observation space of what its judge can see. On our expert-annotated benchmark, a general-purpose editor yields no usable result in over 40% of the cases, and no judge is reliable across edit types. We present EditAgent, which expands both spaces. It routes each step to general editors and specialized methods, and attaches to each candidate a verification record. With these records, off-the-shelf VLM judges outperform finetuned reward models. Candidates are generated in parallel and judged in one listwise call: experts prefer this to sequential refinement in 78.5% of decided comparisons, at no more than half the judge calls. EditAgent improves two open-weight editors on three benchmarks and serves as a data engine for training stronger editors.
  </details>

- **[Look at Where You Think: Localization-Head Alignment for Fine-Grained Perception in Thinking VLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39649)**  `arXiv:2610.39649`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yuan Wang, Wei Wu, Hongning Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Thinking vision-language models reason for thousands of tokens, yet still miss small details in high-resolution images. Zooming methods pay for extra inference passes, and outcome rewards never tell the model where to look at. In Qwen3.5-4B, we find that the bottleneck is selection, not seeing: a few attention heads, which we call localization heads, pick the evidence out of thousands of visual tokens; ablating four of them costs 13.2 points more than ablating four random heads, and their focus fades as the reasoning grows. We therefore propose Localization-Head Alignment (LHA), which makes attention a training signal: whenever the reasoning states a visual fact, these heads learn to look at the focal region rather than the background, as an auxiliary loss in both SFT and RL training. On benchmarks of V*, HR-Bench 4K and TreeBench, LHA lifts Qwen3.5-4B from 74.1% to 76.6% accuracy, the best single-pass 4B result we evaluate, ahead of multi-pass zooming and released 7B models. Alignment alone adds 1.8 points over otherwise identical training while shortening reasoning by 20%, and the gains carry over to Qwen2.5-VL-3B.
  </details>

- **[FlexTry-On: A Time-Efficient Framework for Multi-Scenario Virtual Try-on](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39646)**  `arXiv:2610.39646`  `cs.CV`  
  _Phuong Dam, Quyen Nguyen Duc, Son Hoang Minh, Daeyoung Kim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Virtual try-on has achieved remarkable progress in recent years, yet most existing methods are designed for a specific scenario, such as cloth-to-person or person-to-person try-on, and often require separate conditioning strategies and processing pipelines for different settings. We present FlexTry-On, a unified and time-efficient diffusion-based framework for diverse virtual try-on scenarios, including cloth-to-person, person-to-person, cross-domain, and in-the-wild settings. FlexTry-On adopts a common conditioning formulation, where a text-aware condition extraction strategy converts heterogeneous garment sources into a unified representation, and a dual-conditioning diffusion transformer jointly models garment appearance and person identity. Extensive experiments demonstrate competitive performance on both cloth-to-person and person-to-person benchmarks while maintaining fast inference. Moreover, the same framework can be directly applied to unseen cross-domain scenarios without requiring separate architectures or scenario-specific processing pipelines. These results highlight the potential of unified and efficient virtual try-on frameworks as a practical step toward more flexible and widely applicable virtual try-on systems.
  </details>

- **[FutureWorld: A Plug-and-Play Causal Next-State Predictor for Interactive Video World Exploration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39619)**  `arXiv:2610.39619`  `cs.CV`  
  _Guangyuan Li, Kelu Yao, Shuolin Xu, Xinlei Yu, Wei Xing, Lei Zhao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Interactive video world models aim to follow user actions while maintaining scene consistency over extended interactions.
