History-based methods reuse generated latents to preserve scene content, but three limitations of this reuse contribute to error recirculation. 1) The latent generated at each interaction is retained as history, potentially containing blurred details, geometric distortions, or semantic drift. 2) Although retrieved historical content matches the current scene, it can still contain errors that are carried into the next chunk. 3) New outputs then become history for later interactions, potentially reinforcing the same errors through repeated reuse. To limit this cycle, we aim to predict the future scene state from the current generated latent and the next action before generating the next interaction's video chunk. This future-state prior provides guidance for the next chunk, with the aim of reducing the errors carried forward from history. To this end, we propose a plug-and-play Causal Next-Chunk Predictor (C-NCP) with cross-model adaptability and prediction-guided modulation. We integrate C-NCP into four base generators and evaluate the augmented models across three benchmarks for interactive long-video generation and action-conditioned world modeling. Extensive experiments demonstrate reduced error accumulation and improved long-horizon consistency across diverse generation settings. The project page is atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[UniTele: Learning Device-Conditioned Degradations for Unified Smartphone Telephoto Super-Resolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39617)**  `arXiv:2610.39617`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yuchen Zhang, Aojie Li, Wei Cheng, Xianfang Zeng, Runxing Liu, haixin hao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Super-telephoto imaging on mobile phones suffers from complex, device-dependent degradations that are poorly captured by conventional handcrafted pipelines. Collecting large-scale aligned training pairs across different phones and diverse scenes, however, is both costly and difficult to scale. We therefore study whether device-specific degradations can be learned from only a small number of physical captures and transferred for training a unified super-resolution (SR) model. Our key idea is to factor degradation learning into two learnable components: one captures the device-specific imaging characteristics under well-resolved conditions, while the other captures the additional information loss and artifacts that arise in super-telephoto imaging. We build a paired benchmark across three phone brands (Huawei, Apple, and vivo) by photographing the same content at two display sizes and zoom settings. The larger displayed image is captured at the phone's maximum optical zoom setting, while the smaller image is photographed directly at the phone's highest native zoom setting through the phone's native imaging pipeline. The benchmark contains 519 training and 60 held-out pairs. We use the benchmark to learn device-specific degradation, then apply it to external images to synthesize thousands of additional pairs to train our UniTele SR model. Relative to the pretrained Qwen and FLUX backbones before adaptation, the full UniTele training recipe substantially improves paired restoration fidelity and perceptual similarity on the held-out benchmark and yields markedly more stable and visually faithful restorations.
  </details>

- **[DiFuse: Fusing Native Diffusion Representations for Spatial Reasoning in Unified Multimodal Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39609)**  `arXiv:2610.39609`  `cs.CV`  
  _Ruixin Fan, Weining Wang, Jiahui Sun, Tailai Chen, Jing Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Unified multimodal models (UMMs) jointly support visual understanding and image generation, yet information flow between these two capabilities remains largely asymmetric: understanding representations routinely condition generation, whereas generative representations are rarely reused for multimodal reasoning. In this work, we investigate whether intermediate representations from a UMM's native diffusion branch can enhance spatial reasoning without compromising general multimodal understanding. We propose DiFuse, which selectively incorporates intermediate diffusion representations into the understanding pathway of UMMs. Through systematic layer-wise analysis, we identify which diffusion representations are most informative and where they can be integrated with minimal interference to semantic processing. Guided by these findings, DiFuse spatially aligns diffusion features with visual tokens and injects them into selected multimodal decoder layers via residual fusion. Extensive experiments on UniWorld-V1 show that, DiFuse consistently improves spatial reasoning over a matched SFT baseline while maintaining broadly comparable performance on general multimodal benchmarks. Further analyses show that these gains depend critically on the choice of diffusion representations, injection positions, and the preservation of spatial correspondence between diffusion features and visual tokens. Results on OmniGen2 further demonstrate the transferability of DiFuse across unified multimodal architectures. Overall, our findings show that native diffusion representations can provide complementary spatial cues for multimodal reasoning when selectively integrated in a structure-preserving manner.
  </details>

- **[TactoHand: Learning Geometry-Based Dense Hand-Surface Interaction Fields from Video](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39585)**  `arXiv:2610.39585`  `cs.CV`  
  _Ziheng Xi, Weibo Li, Jiayi Yu, Sipeng Zheng, Wanpeng Zhang, Haiweng Xu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Understanding how humans interact with the world requires a representation of physical interactions at the hand surface. Tactile sensing captures these interactions, but collecting tactile data at scale requires specialized hardware. We introduce TactoHand, a model that predicts three dense hand-surface interaction fields---contact, proximity, and 3D force---from monocular video. We derive geometry-based contact and proximity labels and motion-derived force labels from eligible training data within a curated collection spanning approximately 50 million source frames. TactoHand uses a temporal transformer to aggregate visual evidence across frames, while three learned queries decode the corresponding hand-surface fields. A differentiable physics layer further constrains force predictions using observed object motion. Across in-domain and out-of-domain benchmarks, TactoHand improves contact and proximity estimation over prior methods. In addition, physics supervision reduces position and rotation errors in motion rollouts, and the predicted contact and proximity fields improve downstream hand-object reconstruction. Together, these results show a scalable, low-cost path to learning hand-surface interaction fields without collecting tactile measurements. Code will be released atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[MorphSIG: Subject-Driven Image Generation via Decoupled Anchoring and Feature Transport](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39530)**  `arXiv:2610.39530`  `cs.CV`  
  _Hailong Yan, Yongrui Zhang, Xiangtao Zhang, Le Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We propose MorphSIG, a training-free framework that reformulates subject-driven image generation (SIG) as pseudo-video feature transport. MorphSIG contains two core modules: Progressive Decoupled Anchoring, which uses frequency-domain decoupling and structural scrambling during early denoising to break structural locking and enable a gradual transition from free composition to identity alignment; and Pseudo-flow Guided Feature Transport, which constructs a latent-space pseudo-flow field to mimic Image-to-Video temporal coherence and transport subject semantics precisely. To address stylistic homogeneity and the lack of ground truth in existing benchmarks, we further introduce a benchmark with diverse stylized subjects. Extensive experiments show that MorphSIG significantly improves pose diversity and subject fidelity over baselines without additional training. Overall, MorphSIG bridges static generation and dynamic propagation, providing a cost-effective paradigm for high-fidelity subject consistency.
  </details>

- **[FFAI: Flow Matching with Feedback Training and Adaptive Initialization for CT Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39517)**  `arXiv:2610.39517`  `cs.CV`  
  _Jigang Duan, Heran Wang, Genwei Ma, Xing Zhao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Although low-dose, sparse-view, and limited-angle CT involve distinct degradation mechanisms, all three pose reconstruction challenges due to limited measurement information and can therefore be addressed within a unified framework. In iterative image restoration with flow matching, prediction errors may cause inference states to deviate from the prescribed training path, while a fixed initialization may be unsuitable for inputs with varying degradation severity. We propose a unified conditional flow matching method that conditions on the initial reconstruction and combines feedback-aware training with degradation-adaptive initialization. During training, a teacher network simulates inference updates to generate shifted states, from which a student network learns to recover high-quality reference images, improving its ability to handle prediction errors. During inference, the starting time is selected based on the noise level or extent of missing projection data, and the noise added to the initial state is adjusted accordingly to adapt the starting point to the input. Experiments on public benchmark datasets show that the proposed method achieves better quantitative results than the compared methods across all three reconstruction tasks, effectively suppressing noise and artifacts while preserving anatomical structures and details.
  </details>

- **[Calibrating Attention-Based Token Pruning for Efficient Multi-Image Synthesis in UMMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39467)**  `arXiv:2610.39467`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zimeng Wu, Qijian Tian, Penghui Du, Changqian Yu, Jiaxin Chen, Yunhong Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Unified Multimodal Models (UMMs) enable powerful multi-image synthesis but incur substantial computation from long visual contexts. While token pruning offers a direct route to accelerating inference, existing methods are primarily adapted from understanding tasks, suffering from insufficient reference image exploration, confused conditional guidance, and pooling-induced ambiguity that degrades generation quality. To address these issues, we propose CATP, the first training-free context-compression method tailored to UMM generation, which \textbf{C}alibrates the discrepancies of general \textbf{A}ttention-based \textbf{T}oken \textbf{P}runing for better quality-efficiency trade-off. Concretely, building on independent layer-wise pruning during denoising, we introduce Guidance-Consistent Pruning (GCP) to synchronize pruning decisions across conditioning branches, and a Taylor-Calibrated Attention Metric (TCAM) that derives a theoretically grounded second-order correction for pooling-induced bias. In addition, a Fast Scoring Operator (FSO) fuses the calibration into tiled online Softmax to reduce pruning cost. Extensive experiments across representative UMMs and benchmarks demonstrate that CATP consistently outperforms state-of-the-art token-reduction methods, achieving up to 2.09$\times$ attention-core speedup.
  </details>

- **[PathCowork: Learning from real-world pathology practice to build AI copilots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39441)**  `arXiv:2610.39441`  `cs.CV`  
  _Sheng Wang, Jialu Yao, Maria V. Rossi, Shunsuke Koga, Esther Baranov, MacLean P. Nasrallah, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Medical AI learns from the answers doctors record, but rarely from how they interpret the evidence to reach them. Here we introduce PathCowork, a framework that turns pathologists' narrated examinations into supervision for visual grounding and procedures that guide diagnostic AI agents. Synchronized gaze, narration and navigation link visual findings to the process of interpreting them. Across 48 sessions from 16 pathologists, the platform captured 1,028 minutes of slide review. Using these data, we trained PathCowork-1T for server deployment and PathCowork-27B for local deployment, with improved visual grounding. Across five organ-specific evaluations, agents combining this grounding with recorded diagnostic procedures achieved primary task scores averaging 17.9 and 24.1 percentage points higher than Claude Code and Codex, respectively, with shorter processing times and lower inference costs. Compared to the state-of-the-art multiple-instance learning AIs, the PathCowork agent-proposed regions achieved higher prostate-grading scores with limited slide labels. PathCowork can also help construct pathology AI benchmarks where questions derived from the recordings identified difficulties in recognizing viewing context and rejecting inapplicable premises. In summary, PathCowork proposes a new paradigm to scale up the data collection and train pathology AI through transparent reasoning from diagnostic sessions, opening a path towards systems that learn directly from routine clinical practice.
  </details>

- **[CASE: Concept-Aware Segmentation from Positive and Negative Visual Exemplars](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39396)**  `arXiv:2610.39396`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yi-Jen Tsai, Yen-Yu Lin, Chien-Yao Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Few-shot semantic segmentation typically identifies target objects based on positive support examples of a single semantic class. In practice, however, positive cues alone are often underspecified: for instance, "red cars" may be confused with cars of all colors, "vehicles" may include cars that should be excluded, and "material-defined objects" may span multiple categories. Fulfilling precise requests requires positive exemplars to provide inclusion evidence and negative exemplars to act as intent-defining constraints for exclusion and disambiguation. We formulate this setting as Specified Visual Prompt Segmentation (SVPS), where the target concept is specified by both positive and negative visual prompts. In this work, visual exemplars refer to support-image regions specified by bounding-box prompts. To study SVPS, we introduce CASE (Concept-Aware Segmentation from Positive and Negative Visual Exemplars), a benchmark built on COCO and PACO. CASE encompasses six category- and attribute-level task families: exclusion, isolation, union, attribute exclusion, attribute isolation, and attribute union. We evaluate SAM 3 under this setting and find that, despite its powerful positive-only few-shot segmentation capability, frozen SAM 3 does not consistently treat negative visual prompts as exclusion evidence. To address this, we present Concept Prompt Adaptation (CPA), a conservative LoRA-based adaptation baseline that improves positive-negative prompt interpretation on CASE while remaining competitive under positive-only few-shot segmentation. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Can Image Agents Complete Visual Commissions? A Benchmark and Modular Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39330)**  `arXiv:2610.39330`  `cs.CV`  
  _Tong Li, Lishuai Gao, Qi Zeng, Yifan Li, Cong Wei, Zhuoliang Kang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Visual commissions require image agents to integrate heterogeneous source materials, preserve requirements across dependent stages, and produce coordinated deliverables. Existing evaluations examine specific capabilities and execution settings, leaving end-to-end commission completion less understood. We introduce Visual Commission Bench (VCB), comprising 600 tasks across 12 categories and three complementary tiers, from capability-focused tasks to complete commissions. VCB uses 5,039 requirement checks and separate visual quality ratings to assess content correctness, visual realization, constraint preservation, and delivery completeness without prescribing an internal workflow. We present V-Protocol, a reference agent that dynamically composes reusable task, cognitive, and expression modules at inference time. Task modules organize delivery, cognitive modules derive content, and expression modules guide visual realization. The modules exchange structured intermediate artifacts, while a shared execution harness tracks artifact revisions and dependencies, manages tool execution, and checks completion. With the same controller and image generator as the agent baselines, V-Protocol achieves 69.0\% strict task success on VCB, compared with 42.0\% for SCOPE. On Mind-Bench, strict accuracy increases from 39.2\% to 45.4\%.
  </details>

- **[MRC-MMAgent: Reinforcement Learning for Multi-Role Collaboration in Multimodal Tool-Using Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39324)**  `arXiv:2610.39324`  `cs.CV`  
  _Shengqin Wang, Jie Jin, Xiaofan Li, Wentao Yan, Yu Cheng, Qingsong HuYihang Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal tool-using agents augment vision-language models with access to external information and capabilities, enabling them to solve knowledge-intensive and visually complex tasks that cannot be addressed using visual context alone. However, existing reinforcement learning pipelines largely train single-role agents to search and answer using terminal rewards, overlooking how humans inspect retrieved evidence, reformulate queries, and revise conclusions during search. Consequently, they fail to optimize this self-corrective process over complete interaction trajectories. We propose MRC-MMAgent, a multi-role collaborative reinforcement learning framework that incorporates trajectory assessment and continuation directly into the executable agent loop. First, the framework establishes a multi-role collaborative loop that integrates solving, inspection, and continuation, enabling the agent to acquire evidence through tools, evaluate the preceding trajectory, and resume problem solving when the current answer is insufficiently supported. Second, we introduce a role-coupled learning objective that combines task success, interaction validity, and successful correction with full-trajectory policy optimization across the decisions generated by all roles. Extensive experiments show that, using only the original baseline training set without constructing additional datasets, our method consistently outperforms the corresponding base models and achieves leading or highly competitive performance across benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[Youtu-Parsing-Omni: One Encoder, One Schema, All Modalities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39301)**  `arXiv:2610.39301`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yunhang Shen, Zhangquan Chen, Xiaotian Li, Zhongpeng Cai, Qing Zhou, Zekai Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present Youtu-Parsing-Omni, an omni-modal parser that converts documents, images, audio, and video into structured records grounded in space and time, providing a perception layer for agents that read, locate, and cite evidence across modalities. Its design rests on one encoder for every modality and one schema for every task. Youtu-Omni-Encoder, initialized from a pre-trained language model to strengthen text reading, encodes all modalities with shared weights in a unified space-time coordinate system, and a few fusion layers let video frames attend to concurrent audio, bringing cross-modal fusion and temporal alignment into perception itself. OmniSchema unifies the outputs of all ten parsing tasks, allowing a single self-evolving post-training procedure, OmniSchema-Aware On-Policy Distillation (OSAD), to provide a shared token-role-based objective across tasks. In each round, the model is trained on its own rollouts against token-level targets from a reference-conditioned copy of itself, which is then refreshed with the trained weights, so that the parser and its supervision co-evolve. With a single 5 B-parameter checkpoint, Youtu-Parsing-Omni achieves state-of-the-art performance on OmniDocBench v1.6 (96.96 Overall) and leads all open-weight models on OmniParsingBench (75.08 Avg.), with its advantage concentrated on audio and video. It also achieves the best overall chart-parsing score (95.02) and the highest exact chemical-fingerprint agreement among compared systems, and matches a dedicated optical-music model in character-level transcription of typeset sheet music while staying far ahead of general-purpose systems. Code and model weights are publicly available.
  </details>

- **[COGS: A Diagnostic Benchmark for Attribute-Aware Object-Level 3D Gaussian Splatting Perception](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39201)**  `arXiv:2610.39201`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zheng Xiao, Yifan Liu, Weijian Deng, Jiashu Li, Ziqin Liu, Zhenjun Han, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) is increasingly explored as an input representation for 3D perception. Yet it remains unclear how current 3D architectures exploit heterogeneous Gaussian attributes. This ambiguity makes accuracy hard to interpret and limits principled architecture design for 3DGS perception. However, existing object-level 3DGS datasets lack the appearance variation or artifact control needed for systematic attribute-level analysis. We introduce COGS, a large-scale 3DGS dataset with 15,675 artifact-filtered and appearance-rich objects across 40 categories. Built on COGS, we establish COGS-Bench for object classification and attribute-usage diagnosis, complemented by external validation. Our benchmark shows that Gaussian attributes are not uniformly beneficial: selected subsets can outperform the full attribute set, and the effects of appearance, opacity, and covariance-related attribute combinations vary substantially across architectures. We further study this finding with HALO, a reference model that preserves attribute-specific evidence before hierarchical aggregation. Experiments on COGS-Bench and external 3DGS datasets demonstrate that COGS supports systematic diagnosis of 3DGS attribute usage, while HALO highlights the value of role-preserving attribute organization. Our dataset is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Multi-level Constrained Learning for Underwater Image Enhancement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39183)**  `arXiv:2610.39183`  `cs.CV`  
  _Wangtao Bao, Liping Xie, Kanjian Zhang, Huimin Lu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Underwater image enhancement aims to correct color and recover detail degraded by wavelength-dependent attenuation, scattering, and noise. Paired-reference supervision constrains output similarity, but does not by itself specify the color mapping, the strength of local detail corrections, or consistency with the degraded observation. We propose MCL-UIE, a paired-supervised framework that addresses these requirements by constraining restoration operations and the training objective. Monotonic Chromatic Transform (MCT) restricts global color mapping in log-LMS space through positive diagonal entries and bounded cross-channel coefficients. Uncertainty-Gated Wavelet Reconstruction (UGWR) modulates high-frequency residuals with a learned confidence gate calibrated against normalized wavelet reconstruction errors. During training, Reference-Anchored Degradation Replay (RADR) renders the enhanced image and its paired reference using shared latent degradation proxies, encouraging both to reproduce the input. The three components share a restoration network that combines structured color correction, locally controlled detail recovery, and reference-anchored replay. MCL-UIE ranks first in thirteen of sixteen full-reference dataset-metric comparisons and produces competitive visual results on no-reference benchmarks. Objective ablations indicate that confidence calibration is most effective when combined with replay supervision. RADR is used only during training and adds no inference-time computation. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Edited Endpoints as Partial Goals for Image-to-Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39179)**  `arXiv:2610.39179`  `cs.CV`  
  _Sihyun Kim, Sanghyun Jo, Kyungsu Kim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Edited endpoint images make the target of an image-to-video transition explicit and can improve transition completion, but also preserve context in the source image's layout. Full-frame endpoint conditioning turns this preserved context into requirements on the video's final state, potentially inducing temporal artifacts. We measure discontinuity with Hard-Cut (HC), late background reversal with Background Motion Reversal (BMR), and motion suppression with Dynamic Degree (DD). These artifacts appear across five video models spanning training-free and learned endpoint conditioning, in model-specific forms. We study Partial, a selective conditioning scheme that constrains only an edit-relevant region estimated from image-editor attention and leaves the remaining terminal content to the video model. Across three backbones on TC-Bench-I2V, Partial reduces temporal artifacts relative to full-frame conditioning, with no significant difference in Transition Completion Ratio (TCR); on Wan2.2-Turbo, uniformly weakening conditioning does not reproduce these reductions. A donor-context intervention keeps full-frame conditioning and replaces only the context outside the edit-relevant region with the final frame of a Partial video. It lowers HC and BMR on HunyuanVideo-1.5 and lowers BMR while restoring DD on Wan2.2-Turbo, with no significant difference in TCR, so constraint extent alone does not account for these artifacts. Together, these findings suggest that edited endpoints should serve as partial future goals, although edit relevance does not guarantee future validity. Code and data will be released atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[LightGenBench: A Benchmark for 3D Emission Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39038)**  `arXiv:2610.39038`  `cs.CV`  
  _Dongchen Yang, Xingguang Yan, Manolis Savva_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent generation methods produce 3D assets with detailed geometry and rich textures. However, these assets are non-emissive: they reflect light but emit none. We study a new task, emission generation: predicting spatially varying RGB emission over a 3D asset's surface from its geometry, non-emissive materials, and a rendered view.To support this task, we introduce LightGenBench. Our benchmark makes three contributions. First, we curate a dataset of 37k artist-authored emissive assets in UV, voxel, and multi-view form. Second, we establish baselines across four method families: UV-domain, sparse-voxel, multi-view, and segmentation-based generation. Third, we design an evaluation protocol that measures where an asset emits and how, complemented by user-study comparisons on renderings with varying emission strength.Across the four families, the sparse-voxel baseline TRELLIS.2-Emission performs best because generating emission natively in 3D avoids the failure modes of the other representations, such as the fracturing artifacts of UV space and the occluded emitters that multi-view generation cannot see. However, TRELLIS.2-Emission only reaches an IoU of 0.271, leaving the task far from solved. We hope LightGenBench establishes emission generation as a benchmarkable problem for 3D generation.
  </details>

- **[What do MLLMs know about 'Waldo'? Evaluating Visual Reasoning under Multi-Instance Multi-Object Challenges](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38957)**  `arXiv:2610.38957`  `cs.CV`  
  _Saumya Mundra, Ajoy Mondal, Vineeth N Balasubramanian, C. V. Jawahar_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have led to significant progress in visual reasoning capabilities. However, existing benchmarks curate image-question pairs pertaining to object categories with few instances, enabling trivial object referencing and limited visual ambiguity. In this work, we introduce MIMO-Bench, a benchmark for evaluating MLLMs in a Multi-Instance per Multi-Object categories (MIMO) setting. Each image-question pair in our benchmark pertains to instances belonging to object categories with multiple instances. The presence of such similar feature distractors causes hindrance in basic visual reasoning tasks, necessitating the referring expression to be composed of multiple visual subtasks to uniquely and unambiguously identify the target object. MIMO-Bench comprises 3,802 image-question pairs across attribute binding and numerical counting tasks. Despite impressive performances on existing benchmarks, both SOTA open-source (InternVL, QwenVL) and proprietary (Gemini, GPT-5) models exhibit substantially lower performance on MIMO-Bench, highlighting their limitations in the MIMO setting. The benchmark will be made publicly available.
  </details>

- **[OneScene-Bench: Do Vision-Language Models Integrate Evidence Across Views?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38926)**  `arXiv:2610.38926`  `cs.CV`  
  _Swathi Suhas, Anurag Das, Bernt Schiele, Jonas Fischer_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language models (VLMs) increasingly process multiple views of the same scene, but it remains unclear whether they integrate these observations into a coherent scene-level representation. Existing multi-image evaluations do not isolate this capability, as tasks may admit correct solutions through independent per-view processing without requiring cross-view correspondence. We introduce OneScene-Bench, a controlled benchmark designed to explicitly distinguish when cross-view integration is necessary. Our benchmark evaluates three complementary capabilities on real-world multi-view scenes: unique object counting, cross-view consistency, and spatial reasoning. For counting, we control object overlap across views to separate cases solvable by simple per-view aggregation from those requiring instance-level correspondence. For consistency, we introduce localized edits in shared and view-specific regions, testing whether models distinguish genuine scene contradictions from differences caused by viewpoint and visibility. For spatial reasoning, we construct object relations that require combining spatial evidence across viewpoints to compare physical proximity. We evaluate twelve state-of-the-art open-weight and proprietary VLMs and find that counting accuracy drops substantially on complementary views, ranging from 4.5-50.0%, compared with 45.5-81.5% on complete-overlap examples. Moreover, cross-view consistency remains around 50% across models, including recent proprietary VLMs, despite substantially stronger performance on spatial reasoning. Our results reveal a substantial gap between processing multiple images and integrating evidence across them, highlighting the need for evaluations that explicitly test scene-level multi-view understanding. Code and data are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[RuleForge: Inferring and Instantiating Latent Visual Rules as Executable Programs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38737)**  `arXiv:2610.38737`  `cs.CV`  
  _Weifeng Xu, Yaxiong Wang, Zhedong Zheng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Visual rule reasoning asks whether multimodal models can recover an abstract concept from a few examples. Existing studies either infer rules from visual examples but evaluate only classification or verbalization, or supervise executable generation with rules provided as input. Consequently, the relationship between visual rule understanding and reliable rule instantiation remains insufficiently explored. We present Bongard-Forge100k, a controlled benchmark, together with RuleForge, a backbone-agnostic framework that jointly learns visual rule understanding and rule-conditioned executable generation. The benchmark contains 108,797 problems: 86,000 for training, 19,297 for testing familiar rules, and 3,500 for testing held-out rule compositions. The framework progresses through rule-understanding training, joint understanding and scene-program supervision, and refinement via execution-grounded reinforcement learning with Understanding Replay. Executing generated programs under multiple random seeds provides verifiable feedback on executability, rule satisfaction, diversity, and robustness. With UniLIP as the primary backbone, RuleForge improves multiple-choice accuracy over the base model by 20.93 percentage points on familiar rules and 28.42 percentage points on held-out rule compositions, while increasing scene-level rule satisfaction on familiar rules from 2.91% to 70.99%. Additional experiments on InternVL3.5, Qwen3-VL, and Show-o2 demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed training data and framework across diverse multimodal architectures.
  </details>

- **[V-CAPE: Visual Consistency Assessment for Product Image Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38611)**  `arXiv:2610.38611`  `cs.CV`  
  _Iaroslav Melekhov, Roberto Alcover Couso, Francesco Taioli, Daniel Coelho, Artur Bekasov, Virginia Fernandez Arguedas, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in generative image editing enable complex transformations such as background replacement, object manipulation, and pose adjustment from natural language instructions. However, verifying that these transformations preserve visual consistency, defined as the preservation of an object's intrinsic visual attributes, including geometry, texture, color, and fine-grained details, remains a largely unsolved problem. This challenge is particularly pronounced at scale, where manual inspection is infeasible and automated quality assessment (AutoQA) could become essential. In this work, we introduce Visual Consistency Assessment for Product Image Editing (V-CAPE), a large-scale dataset for evaluating visual consistency in generative image editing. The dataset consists of 40K unique source-target product image pairs, each annotated with binary accept/reject labels and structured failure modes capturing fine-grained inconsistencies such as geometric distortions, pose errors, and identity mismatches. V-CAPE combines real-world edited images with a synthetic subset constructed from 3D assets, enabling both realistic evaluation and controlled analysis. Vision-language models (VLMs) across all evaluated size categories on AutoQA achieve accuracy below 65%, indicating that the dataset poses a substantial challenge regardless of model scale. Our results highlight fundamental limitations of current models in verifying visual consistency under transformation, positioning V-CAPE as a benchmark for advancing fine-grained image editing evaluation.
  </details>

- **[Data Scaling Laws in Stereo Matching: From In-domain Fitting to Cross-domain Generalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38600)**  `arXiv:2610.38600`  `cs.CV`  
  _Tong Zhao, Di Liang, Yiming Zeng, Bo Xu, Yingzhi Tang, Chen Bai_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In the era of large models, stereo matching is developing towards generalizable foundation models trained on million-scale synthetic images. However, how model performance scales up with synthetic data is still underexplored. In this work, we preliminarily investigate the in-domain and cross-domain data scaling laws in stereo matching. A realistic synthetic dataset, named XStereo, containing 200 thousand samples is first built to serve as a high-quality benchmark for data scaling research. In-domain training on five large-scale datasets demonstrate a two-regime scaling behavior with data, transitioning from the standard power law to saturation, where the critical transition point is approximately invariant under a fixed model. Cross-domain performance exhibits a substantially weaker and less predictable dependence on the source-domain data scale. The distribution and coverage diversity are more effective for diminishing domain gaps. More instructive suggestions are provided for high-efficiency data construction and training. The insightful findings are significant verification and extension of the existing scaling laws in both language and vision, paving the way to efficient and stronger stereo matching. The XStereo dataset is public atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Detecting Fine-Grained Differences between Image Datasets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38547)**  `arXiv:2610.38547`  `cs.CV`  
  _Leander Girrbach, Alexander von Recum, Kirill Bykov, Zeynep Akata_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Detecting whether and in particular how two data distributions differ is a fundamental statistical problem. For example, we might be interested in which concepts (expressed in natural language) differ between two classes, the outputs of different generative models, or the same model with different prompt specifications. When working with large and complex image datasets, we intuitively think that first selecting concepts from a broad list using fine-grained patch embeddings, and then optimizing the concepts to better fit the data is an effective workflow. However, prior work instead uses MLLMs to propose concepts and image-level embeddings to select them. Therefore, we systematically compare these techniques and find that selecting concepts from broad lists can replace MLLM-proposals at obviously lower cost. MLLM inference can then be spent on optimizing selected concepts instead to achieve substantial improvements. Additionally, by designing a new benchmark that focuses on low-prevalence and multi-concept distinctions, we find that selecting nonredundant concepts instead of only the most distinguishing concepts matters, and we introduce techniques for how to effectively do this. Finally, we show how our methods address important problems like detecting spurious correlations and biases. Overall, our work presents a fundamental perspective shift on how we analyze interpretable distributional differences between datasets.
  </details>

- **[Residual Motion Splatting for Continuous Space-Time Video Super-Resolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38499)**  `arXiv:2610.38499`  `cs.CV`  
  _Vincent Herfeld, Eva Coupet{é}, Julien Mairal, Jean Ponce_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Continuous space-time video super-resolution produces a high-resolution, highframe-rate video from a low-resolution, low-frame-rate one at arbitrary spatial scales and temporal instants. Classical spatial multi-frame super-resolution has shown that detail beyond a single frame's sampling limit can be recovered by aligning complementary observations with sub-pixel precision and accumulating them on a finer grid. Existing learning-based methods largely depart from this principle: some align and fuse frames only at low resolution before regressing a continuous high-resolution representation, while others interpolate each frame to the output resolution before splatting. In both cases, the per-frame samples are resampled before fusion, so their sub-pixel positions are lost before frames are combined. Restoring this principle in a learned continuous model raises two challenges: estimating sub-pixel motion at unobserved times, and handling holes and occlusions when splatting a coarse grid onto a finer one. We address the first by predicting per-pixel continuous trajectories that refine linear optical-flow paths with smooth residual motion, using features integrated over the full video sequence. We address the second by directly splatting consecutive low-resolution features in a learned latent space onto a high-resolution grid at their predicted sub-pixel positions with predicted visibility weights, before a decoder resolves remaining ambiguities. This makes temporal interpolation and multi-frame superresolution a single operation. Our method improves both reconstruction fidelity and perceptual quality on multiple standard benchmarks over the state of the art, while running up to an order of magnitude faster.
  </details>

- **[Geometry-Aware Trajectory State Modeling for Few-Shot Action Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38482)**  `arXiv:2610.38482`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zhiqi Miao, Marko Haralović, Stanislav Levendeev, Sarvesh Angadi, Jannatul Ferdous, Dan-Cristian Ploesteanu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Few-shot action recognition (FSAR) requires representations that expose discriminative motion dynamics from only a few labeled videos. Recent trajectory-based methods leverage long-term point tracks for explicit temporal correspondence, but often treat trajectories as carriers of sampled visual features or summarize their motion with compact 2D displacement-based descriptors. We instead view an action as the ordered evolution of 2.5D trajectory states and their local geometric interactions. Based on this view, we introduce GeoTRAM, a geometry-aware trajectory state modeling framework for FSAR. GeoTRAM lifts sparse tracks into 2.5D trajectories, where StateMotion models ordered intra-trajectory state evolution and RelMotion captures state-conditioned local interactions through time-varying relative geometry and motion. Quality-filtered spectral sampling provides more reliable trajectory initialization; complementary semantic, motion, and depth cues are separately refined and preserved for joint spatio-temporal modeling; action-guided reweighting and top-$\rho$ trajectory selection support complementary frame- and trajectory-level matching. Experiments on five FSAR benchmarks demonstrate strong gains on the motion-centric Something-Something benchmarks while maintaining competitive performance on more appearance-driven benchmarks and a favorable accuracy--efficiency trade-off.
  </details>

- **[IntReal: A Real-World Dataset and Benchmark for Multimodal Interleaved Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38425)**  `arXiv:2610.38425`  `cs.CV`  
  _Shaohan Yu, Jie Qin, Wende Tan, Haiwen Diao, Limeng Qiao, Yueming Lyu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Humans routinely consume multimodal content, where textual and visual information is naturally interleaved. Although Unified Multimodal Models (UMMs) can jointly generate text and images, multimodal interleaved generation remains largely underexplored. Progress in this area is hindered by two key limitations: (1) the lack of well-defined interleaved generation tasks and corresponding training data, and (2) the absence of fine-grained evaluation protocols that can reliably diagnose model performance across different capability dimensions. To this end, we present IntReal, comprising two complementary components: IntReal-100K, a real-world dataset of multimodal interleaved samples spanning four major categories, and the IntReal Evaluation Suite, which evaluates model performance across seven complementary dimensions using specialized expert models, enabling coarse-to-fine diagnosis. Extensive experiments validate our evaluation suite through low inter-metric redundancy (max pairwise absolute Spearman's $\rho \leq 0.42$ ), high agreement with human judgments (83.3% accuracy), and strong consistency across different expert evaluators (Spearman's $\rho > 0.9$ ), supporting the complementarity and reliability of our metrics. By benchmarking four families of UMMs, we show that IntReal Evaluation Suite reveals distinct strengths and weaknesses across models. Post-training experiments demonstrate the utility of IntReal-100K for improving interleaved generation and further show that training unimodal generation can also benefit interleaved generation. These findings suggest that interleaved generation can benefit from modality-specific optimization, rather than requiring joint text-image training at every stage.
  </details>

- **[Visually Irrelevant Hallucinations in Medical Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38389)**  `arXiv:2610.38389`  `cs.CV`  
  _Ke Zou, Nan Zhou, Tao Yang, Meng Liu, Linchao He, Zehui Liao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Medical vision-language models (VLMs) show strong performance in radiology report generation (RRG) and medical visual question answering (Med-VQA), producing free-form clinical descriptions and answers from medical images. However, they may generate clinically meaningful statements that lack visual evidence or contradict image content. We refer to these errors as visually irrelevant hallucinations and investigate them at the phrase level. We propose CLEAR (Counterfactual Localized Evaluation of Alignment and Response), a model-agnostic post-hoc framework for phrase-level hallucination risk estimation. CLEAR decomposes generated responses into visually assessable clinical phrases. For each phrase, phrase-conditioned grounding identifies a candidate visual region, which is selectively perturbed by a localized counterfactual intervention. CLEAR characterizes the intervention using two complementary auditing cues: response cues, capturing semantic and clinically meaningful changes in the generated response, and alignment cues, measuring changes in image-phrase compatibility. These cues are normalized and jointly modeled to estimate phrase-level hallucination risk. Across four datasets spanning RRG and Med-VQA, CLEAR outperforms state-of-the-art hallucination detection baselines across multiple medical and general-purpose VLMs. On MIMIC-CXR, CLEAR improves AUPRC from 85.10% to 90.23%. Its effectiveness is further validated on a GT-derived controlled VIH benchmark. These results establish visually irrelevant hallucination as an important fine-grained reliability failure mode in medical VLMs and demonstrate the value of model-agnostic visual-support auditing for more trustworthy multimodal clinical generation.
  </details>

- **[Volley3D: Unified 3D Full-Sequence Reconstruction from Monocular Broadcasts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38358)**  `arXiv:2610.38358`  `cs.CV`  
  _Noâm Boussouf, Adrien Maglo, Mohamed Chaouch, Hideo Saito_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  3D reconstruction from single-view sports broadcast is a pivotal capability for automated analytics and immersive AR/VR replays, yet notoriously ill-posed due to difficult-to-calibrate moving cameras and depth ambiguity. Unlike widely studied racket sports, team sports such as volleyball feature unpredictable multi-contact sequences and high altitude jumps. These dynamics categorically invalidate the standard planar projection assumptions relied upon by existing methods. To resolve these limitations without 3D training supervision, we propose a complete, physics-grounded pipeline tailored to monocular broadcast volleyball. First, we include a correction module to the perspective drift induced by ground-plane projection by coupling temporal jump spotting with Newtonian interpolation for 3D player placement. Second, for 3D ball trajectory estimation, we design a dedicated sub-trajectory segmentation architecture alongside a collision-matching algorithm. By anchoring physical equations to reconstructed 3D hand vertices, we lift noisy ball 2D detections into a spatiotemporally coherent 3D scene. Evaluations on multi-camera benchmarks demonstrate our approach significantly reduces 3D spatial drift compared to 2D-reprojection baselines.
  </details>

- **[SSQA: Sibling-Selective Quadtree Attention for Hierarchical Modeling in Perception Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38320)**  `arXiv:2610.38320`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yufan Chen, Arnav Bali, Angela Liu, Laura Zheng, Ming C. Lin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Perception tasks for navigation in robotics, including aerial platforms such as drones and autonomous driving systems, are inherently structured. Drone-mounted cameras typically capture sky above, terrain below, and obstacles or man-made structures in between, while driving data often contains organized road layouts, lane markings, and surrounding agents. Motivated by these axis-aligned structural priors, we note that such information is typically more structured than in generic image tasks. We hypothesize that processing information in a quadtree-esque manner can not only model features effectively in a hierarchical manner, but also offers an efficient linear-time alternative to vanilla attention mechanisms, which run in quadratic time. In this paper, we propose Sibling-Selective Quadtree Attention (SSQA), which models image tokens hierarchically as a structured, full quadtree. We show analytical complexity analysis that guarantees linear-time feature modeling, in addition to empirical experiments comparing inference speeds with other popular modeling approaches, such as Mamba 2 and Quadtree Attention. Our results, benchmarked across several tasks, show that we achieve results at least as good, if not notably better, as others at a fraction of the computational costs.
  </details>

- **[Neighbourhood shortcuts in building damage assessment from very-high-resolution satellite imagery: diagnosis and a building-evidence plug-in](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38162)**  `arXiv:2610.38162`  `cs.CV`  
  _JianChong Guo, YuTing Wan, Kefan Li, AiLong Ma, YanFei Zhong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Building damage assessment (BDA) from very-high-resolution imagery supports disaster response, and deep models now achieve high overall accuracy on public benchmarks. Because damage is spatially clustered, most buildings share the damage level of their neighbours, so overall scores are dominated by such buildings, whereas damaged buildings among intact ones are rare and easily overlooked. A high overall score therefore cannot tell whether a model judges the building itself or relies on its neighbourhood as a shortcut, and this study tests which of the two current models do. We stratify buildings by their agreement with their neighbours, move buildings into new neighbourhoods with their pixels unchanged, and diagnose nine models covering the main paradigms of BDA. All models miss a third to half of the isolated damaged buildings, and in non-flood events they call 73--87% of intact buildings damaged once these are moved into a damaged neighbourhood. We propose a building-evidence plug-in that adds two experts to an existing model without retraining it. One expert sees only the building, and the other sees its surroundings but is trained with seamlessly swapped neighbourhoods so that it learns not to trust them. With each of the nine models, the plug-in maintains or improves overall accuracy, reduces errors on buildings that disagree with their neighbours by 8--18%, and cuts by 40--69% the intact buildings that a damaged neighbourhood turns into damaged ones. It also raises the detection of synthetic isolated damage from 1--6% to 13--39% and carries over to optical--SAR data and to an unseen earthquake. High overall scores thus conceal a systematic reliance on the neighbourhood, which building-level evidence reduces at low cost.
  </details>

- **[REEL: Same Frames, Different Answers and Different Costs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38158)**  `arXiv:2610.38158`  `cs.CV`  
  _Xin Liang, Jiangpeng He_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Video understanding keeps moving toward longer recordings, and the longer a recording is, the more questions can be asked about it. But processing it again for every question does not scale, so many systems watch the video once, build a memory, and answer later questions from it. Yet what to remember is decided before anyone knows what will be asked, and whatever is left out cannot be recovered later.Moreover, whether the savings pay off is judged by token counts, which fall when the input gets shorter but not when the model reuses what it has already computed. We present REEL, an evaluation framework that compares three saved representations built from exactly the same observed frames, so that a change in answers can come only from what the system remembered. Text writes a short record of what happened, Visual keeps the encoder outputs, and Prefix State also keeps the language-model prefix. Each is compared with full reprocessing (Frames) by paired correctness changes, cumulative GPU time, and storage size. Across six vision-language models and two benchmarks, Text and Frames differ in correctness on 20\% to 29\% of questions, even when their mean accuracy is almost the same. By contrast, Visual and Prefix State stay much closer to Frames. With unlimited storage, Prefix State is the cheapest in total GPU time among memories that keep almost all of the answers Frames gets right, for every model on both benchmarks. The trade-off is storage: Prefix State keeps up to about 21 GiB per video, whereas Text needs only kilobytes. Reusing computation and discarding information both look like efficiency, but only the second removes evidence that later questions may need, and neither mean accuracy nor token count tells them apart. REEL makes that difference measurable for any system that answers many questions from one saved memory.
  </details>

- **[From Next Day Wildfire to Next Days Wildfire: A New Multi-Continent Dataset of Active-Fire Spread for Sequence-to-Sequence Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38086)**  `arXiv:2610.38086`  `cs.CV`  
  _Jose Guillen, Renzo Zanca, Valentin Barriere_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Benchmarks such as WildfireSpreadTS (WSTS) have enabled data-driven forecasting of wildfire spread from multimodal satellite time series, but they suffer from limitations: size restricts them to large fires that burn for weeks, and they are from similar environments. We introduce AFSeq, a multimodal dataset of 1,194 recent fire events (2023 to 2026) from 8 different countries across three regions, dominated by the short, small fires that existing benchmarks underrepresent. Our dataset is the largest and most diverse to date for time-series-based wildfire spread prediction. We also introduce UTAE-AR, an extension of a classical U-Net with Temporal Auto Encoder, enhanced with recurrent modules before the decoder. This allows the model to not only predict the next day but the whole sequence in an autoregressive way. Moreover, our model can feed its own predicted fire mask back one day at a time, producing the day-by-day forecasts that single-step models cannot. Our results show that UTAE-AR surpasses state-of-the-art on both WSTS, WSTS+ and AFSeq for next-day accuracy while having 21.5% of their parameters, and being able to predict confidently at a longer horizon.
  </details>

- **[Classifying Urban Locations by Cyclist Facility Usage and Directional Alignment: A Validated Camera-Based Framework from Multi-Site Observations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38045)**  `arXiv:2610.38045`  `cs.CV`  
  _Ao He, Mohit Gupta, Deborah Salon, Ricardo Eiris, Vivian W. H. Wong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Objectives: Agencies invest in bicycle facilities but lack tools to assess whether cyclists use them as designed. We examine whether usage patterns follow facility configurations and whether higher bike lane usage is associated with less wrong-way riding.Methods: Motion-triggered cameras covered 19 deployments at 17 locations in Tempe, Arizona, in 2023. Manual review identified 1,963 cycling events; excluding 20 crossings left 1,943 labeled events for analysis. An automated pipeline on the same captures counted 92.9 percent of the full manual total (per-deployment r = 0.99), and every automated wrong-way call was visually verified. Deployments were classified using thresholds on facility usage shares.Findings: Deployments were bike-lane dominant (9), sidewalk dominant (6), or mixed (4). Cyclists in bike lanes rode against the flow in 4.6 percent of events; on sidewalks, riders opposed the near-side traffic flow in 49.6 percent. Across deployments, bike lane share and wrong-way rate were negatively correlated (Spearman rho = -0.87, p < 0.001, n = 16). An independent volunteer count in the same city showed a similar sidewalk-direction pattern (rho = 0.79).Novelty: The study combines multi-site still-camera observations with a fully hand-verified benchmark to characterize facility use and travel direction.Practical Applications: The workflow offers planners a low-cost screen for locations with high sidewalk cycling and travel against the near-side traffic flow, supporting more detailed site investigation.
  </details>

- **[Venus-DefakerOne2: A Vision-Forensics Foundation Model with Broader Coverage](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37918)**  `arXiv:2610.37918`  `cs.CV`  
  _Chenfan Qu, Yachao Liang, Mingqi Fang, Jiangwei Xie, Zhenming Wang, Baoyue Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Real-world forgery is inherently heterogeneous: it manifests not only as digital manipulation but also as physical spoofing, and is carried not only by images but also by videos. Despite substantial progress, a unified anti-forgery foundation model covering such a broad threat spectrum remains absent. In this paper, we present Venus-DefakerOne2, which substantially extends the forensic capability boundary along both the forgery-form and media-type dimensions. Trained on tens of millions of open-source and in-house samples, it incorporates two key designs. Tide-Style-Tuning alternates LoRA and full-parameter fine-tuning to balance computational efficiency, generalization, and hard-sample fitting while mitigating catastrophic forgetting. Entropy-based Test Adaptation determines the optimal prediction modality by analyzing prediction entropy. Venus-DefakerOne2 achieves new state-of-the-art performance across a comprehensive suite of benchmarks, including DeepFake faces (96.7%), AI-generated images (93.3%), tampered documents (87.6%), manipulated natural images (89.6%), physical spoofs (93.1%), and AI-generated videos (96.0%). It significantly outperforms both specialized expert models and its predecessor, DefakerOne, establishing itself as a powerful and versatile foundation model for binary-decision forensic tasks.
  </details>

- **[FYC: FPR-Disparity Weight Perturbation and Youden Calibration for Fair Deepfake Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37882)**  `arXiv:2610.37882`  `cs.CV`  
  _Uzoamaka Ezeakunne, Taiwo Olabamipe, Deevesh Jain, Xiuwen Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deepfake detectors routinely achieve high aggregate accuracy while exhibiting substantial demographic disparities in their errors: false-positive rates differ by double digits across gender and skin tone. Most existing remedies require either full retraining with a fairness-aware objective or demographic-annotated training data, both impractical when only a pretrained checkpoint is available, while label-free post-hoc calibrators leave the detector's weights unchanged. We introduce FYC (FPR-disparity Weight Perturbation and Youden Calibration), a two-stage post-hoc method that perturbs a detector's weights under an FPR-disparity criterion, then calibrates a separate decision threshold for each demographic group - requiring no architectural changes and no full retraining. Evaluated against every detector benchmarked on AI-Face v2 across six intersectional gender x skin-tone groups, FYC outperforms the field on 18 of 21 reported fairness and utility metrics, including purpose-built fairness architectures, at a fraction of the training cost. We further identify why the choice of fairness criterion is critical: the original Demographic Parity objective used in prior weight-perturbation work is architecture-dependent and unpredictable, improving two of four evaluated architectures, leaving a third largely unchanged, and substantially degrading the fourth (PG-FDD), with no way to anticipate the outcome for a given checkpoint in advance. Conditioning the same objective on real images only - our FPR-disparity criterion - eliminates this unpredictability, improving fairness and accuracy jointly across all four architectures with no exceptions.
  </details>

- **[Intersectional Invariant Learning for Fair Deepfake Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37881)**  `arXiv:2610.37881`  `cs.CV`  
  _Uzoamaka Ezeakunne, Taiwo Olabamipe, Deevesh Jain, Xiuwen Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deepfake detection models achieve high overall accuracy but exhibit large performance disparities across demographic groups, causing some groups to experience substantially higher false-positive rates than others. Existing fairness-aware methods address this by constraining model outputs to equalize group predictions, but do not tackle the underlying feature-level bias. As a result, models may still rely on demographic characteristics, such as skin texture, as indirect cues for detecting manipulated content. To address this problem, we propose Intersectional Invariant Learning (IIL), a framework that improves feature learning rather than only adjusting model outputs. IIL alternates between identifying the most challenging demographic partitions and learning features that remain consistent across those partitions. The framework also includes a false-positive-rate fairness objective and group-balanced sampling across six gender x skin-tone intersectional groups. We evaluate IIL on a large-scale fairness benchmark containing over one million images, 10 baseline detectors, and 21 fairness and utility metrics. Results show that IIL achieves Pareto-optimal performance, simultaneously attaining an AUC above 99%, a false-positive rate under 2.5%, and the lowest intersectional fairness disparity (FMEO) among all compared methods. IIL outperforms prior fairness-aware methods on the large majority of the 21 evaluation metrics, while reducing both false-positive rates and intersectional fairness disparities by several-fold compared with the strongest competing method.
  </details>

- **[StreamVibe: Proactive Video Agents for Evolving User Intent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34662)**  `arXiv:2610.34662`  `cs.CV`  
  _Chengzhi Liu, Yuxiang Lai, Yuzhe Yang, Yanqing Liu, Jiheng Hou, Yue Cao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language models are shifting from offline passive understanding toward online proactive interaction. However, prevailing methods largely rely on explicit requests while overlooking implicit user intents (e.g., "where should I click next?" when a user is navigating a website) and fail to continuously update their understanding as user intents evolve. These limitations make them less suitable for intent-aware interaction in real-world scenarios. To address these challenges, we introduce StreamVibe-Bench, a new benchmark that serves as a testbed for user intent understanding and interaction, comprising 4.1K evaluation samples and 85K training samples spanning explicit, implicit, and dynamic intents. Building on this benchmark, we develop StreamVibe-VL, a streaming proactive model that infers implicit user intents from continuous observations and dynamically adapts its interactions as these intents evolve. We further introduce StreamVibe-Harness, which maintains user intents as persistent states with flexible lifecycles through hierarchical memory, enabling it to track the latest user intents and environmental states. Our experiments reveal three key findings: (i) StreamVibe-VL preserves strong general video understanding capabilities while improving response timing and answer quality by 10.7% over existing baselines; (ii) existing proactive models remain limited in understanding user intents and determining when to respond, especially under implicit and dynamic intents; and (iii) StreamVibe-Harness further improves response consistency and adaptation during sustained interactions, resulting in a 12.3% gain in proactive interaction performance.
  </details>

- **[TopX: Text-to-Motion for Unseen Skeletons from the Skeleton File Alone](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34321)**  `arXiv:2610.34321`  `cs.CV`  
  _Tengjiao Sun, Pengcheng Fang, Xiaoyu Zhan, Dongjie Fu, Xiaohao Cai, Hansung Kim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Text-to-motion models generate motion only for the skeletons they were trained on, while a new character arrives as a skeleton file and nothing else. We study text-to-motion for skeletons unseen in training, without clips of the character, templates, retargeting or fine-tuning. We present TopX, a 73M-parameter flow-matching transformer that generates joint rotations and a root trajectory for any skeleton from its kinematic tree, rest pose and joint names. Motion is normalised by the rest pose and the size of the skeleton, and the rest pose is encoded as a world-frame descriptor that is independent of how the rig's author oriented each joint, while rest conventions are randomised during training. Attention is factorised over the joints and the frames of a motion. Across joints, queries and keys are rotated by HK-RoPE, a heat-kernel rotary encoding of the skeleton graph that is defined for every tree and does not change with the order of its joints or the eigenbasis of its Laplacian; across frames, they are rotated by a rotary encoding of frame order. To evaluate this generalisation, we build SkelShift, which unifies four motion corpora under one codec and holds out whole skeletons, tree classes and species, so that each test bucket isolates one kind of skeleton shift: topology, joint count, body plan, species or rig source. It scores motion quality without a learned evaluator. A single model animates every benchmark skeleton (4--117 joints) directly from its skeleton file and generates up to 240 frames. On held-out game species its motion is about as close to the real clips as two halves of the real clips are to each other (kernel distance 0.127 against 0.124), and text-to-motion retrieval reaches R@1 0.953, compared with 0.929 for real motion. Project page:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[CropReward: A Human-Preference Benchmark and Reward Model for Aesthetic Image Cropping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34221)**  `arXiv:2610.34221`  `cs.CV`  
  _Xiangfei Sheng, Xiaofeng Pan, Leida Li, Qian Zhang, Biao Wang, Johan Wagemans, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Aesthetic image cropping (AIC) aims to improve image composition and aesthetic quality by removing extraneous or distracting content. Over the past decade, AIC methods have evolved from specialized vision models to multimodal large language models(MLLMs). Reliable evaluation that reflects human preferences is essential for benchmarking AIC methods and guiding model optimization. However, the current AIC evaluation relies largely on geometry-based metrics such as intersection over union (IoU), as well as image aesthetic assessment(IAA) models. Although inconsistencies with human preferences have long been observed, the robustness and reliability of these evaluation criteria remain underexplored. To bridge this gap, we first construct CropBench, a large-scale benchmark comprising 106,995 human preference pairs for 1,019 images, with each candidate set containing the original image and candidate crops produced by 14 AIC methods. A systematic study on CropBench reveals that the existing criteria, ranging from IoU to off-the-shelf IAA assessment models, only exhibit limited alignment with human preferences. Building upon CropBench, we further propose CropReward, an MLLM-based reward model that evaluates a candidate crop by comparing with the original uncropped image and outputs a score indicating explicit aesthetic improvement or degradation relative to the original. In addition, we employ CropReward to construct CropReward-300k, the largest AIC dataset to date, and further develop CropAlign, a preference-aligned AIC model trained solely with CropReward supervision, without human-annotated crop boxes. Experimental results demonstrate that CropReward aligns with human preferences significantly better than AIC existing criteria. CropAlign also surpasses the state-of-the-art AIC methods, demonstrating the advantage of CropReward in guiding AIC model optimization.
  </details>

- **[AnyGameRenderer: Entity-Consistent Generative Rendering Across Games and an Entity-Level Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31466)**  `arXiv:2610.31466`  `cs.CV`  
  _Ruicong Liu, Liangyang Ouyang, Fengbo Lan, Zhixiang Wang, Kaipeng Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI coding agents can now build small playable games in minutes, but the results usually look like programmer art: gray blockouts, colored geometric primitives, and flat semantic blobs. We present AnyGameRenderer, a video-to-video generative renderer that turns such footage into high-quality visuals with characters, motion detail, and effects. Throughout, it keeps the layout, camera, and every entity's position and timing intact. Our AnyGameRenderer generalizes across a wide range of genres that agents build, such as tower defense, auto-battlers, top-down survivors, turn-based battles, kart racing, and crowd runners. AnyGameRenderer takes plain video as its game-state input, with no complex representations such as engine G-buffers, per-object class labels, or low-poly proxies. Gray blockouts, colored primitives, semantic maps, and wireframes all work as input. It also preserves entity correspondence: group counts stay correct, and entities appear and disappear on the right frame. We implement AnyGameRenderer by LoRA fine-tuning MiniMax-H3 ref2va, a 33B reference-video-conditioned video generation model. Training uses ~10k paired clips from three sources: (i) multi-stream rendering data from 38 agent-made games; (ii) two-stream rendering data from Unreal Engine, with programmatically controlled crowds, count changes, and entity scales; and (iii) real gameplay recordings, with pairs reconstructed via 3D-box detection. To evaluate the entity correspondence, we propose an entity-level benchmark of 59 held-out cases with 753 entities, scored against ground-truth boxes. Compared with the base model, AnyGameRenderer better preserves entity correspondence: temporal match rises from 50.9% to 62.9%, missed count changes fall from 34.7% to 21.3%, and fewer entities are hallucinated or left unrendered. Project page:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Observation Quality in DSM-Assisted Aerial Localization: A Benchmark and Base-Anchored Uncertainty Calibration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18564)**  `arXiv:2610.18564`  `cs.CV`  
  _Pengyu Yin, Jie Pan, Junxiang Tan, Mengqi Fu, Kun Cheng, Yifan Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Aerial map matching provides position observations for navigation, but a position error alone does not determine how an observation should be weighted. We define five benchmark tasks: position availability and agreement, grouped error structure, predictive uncertainty, controlled updates, and temporal accumulation. We use digital-surface-model-assisted localization to test whether base-anchored uncertainty calibration improves the weighting of unchanged position estimates. The method combines propagated image and map sensitivities with the second moment of a compact configuration subset relative to the reported position. Scale is fitted using separate position references without local surface-height ground truth. Thirteen covariance models are evaluated on two held-out query sets from locations represented in the calibration data. Using reference residuals, we compare the method with a constant full-matrix baseline. With all localization failures included, the method reduces the normalized position-update risk, averaged equally across locations, by 7.70% on 728 conditions and 5.46% on a separate 142 conditions under an independent prior. On the primary set, the equal-location average negative log likelihood on reference residuals improves by 2.571 nat. At these seen locations, exploratory base-only quality calibration and conditional-addition controls leave the incremental update benefit of configuration disagreement unresolved. Navigation benefit and real temporal accumulation remain untested.
  </details>

- **[Meta-Video: Efficient Standardized Evaluation for Video Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18535)**  `arXiv:2610.18535`  `cs.CV`  
  _Soumya Shamarao Jahagirdar, Anna Kukleva, Vishaal Udandarao, Samuel Thomas, Brian Kingsbury, Rogerio Feris, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Video understanding benchmarks are the primary yardstick for measuring progress in video-language modeling, and a growing body of work relies on them to claim improvements. The reported benchmark scores in these works, however, are almost always produced by frameworks (e.g., lmms eval) used as black boxes: certain evaluation choices like frame sampling, preprocessing, and input formatting are inherited from preset defaults and seldom inspected carefully. We show these unexamined choices are a major source of variance. Even for the same model checkpoint, evaluation configuration alone can shift accuracy by up to 19.5pp. Hence, it is unclear whether reported gains reflect genuine improvements in capability or differences in evaluation setup. Moreover, many benchmark samples offer little evidence of visual capability, since they can be answered without the video, and the full suites are expensive to evaluate. We address these issues in two parts. First, we curate existing suites for discriminability and efficiency, producing Meta-Video, a balanced 1,000-question benchmark over 791 videos spanning diverse tasks and durations, which largely preserves model rankings, at 5 times inference speedup for a full-frame count sweep experiment (4-128 frames). Second, we establish a standardized evaluation protocol by sweeping the configuration space to isolate choices that measurably affect results, distill them into best practices and reporting standards, and release reproducible reference scores across five models and seven benchmarks. We hope that the proposed evaluation framework and the Meta-Video benchmark will support video understanding research by providing a reproducible and efficient foundation to measure progress in a reliable way. Project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[TKCAM: Text and Keyframe to Camera Trajectory Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.11105)**  `arXiv:2610.11105`  `cs.CV`  
  _Haozhe Yang, Zhiyang Dou, Zekai Gu, Cheng Lin, Wenping Wang, Yuan Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Generating high-quality and controllable camera motion is essential for AI-assisted cinematography, video synthesis, and 3D scene understanding. We introduce TKCAM, a Text- and Keyframe-conditioned CAMera-motion synthesis framework based on generative masked modeling. We represent camera dynamics using a 12-dimensional kinematic feature comprising position, velocity, and a continuous rotation representation and discretize them into hierarchical motion tokens via a Residual Vector Quantizer (RVQ). A two-stage masked transformer architecture then learns to reconstruct and refine these tokens, utilizing explicit self- and cross-attention modules for multimodal conditioning. A central feature of our framework is sparse visual keyframe conditioning: users can provide free-form text prompts together with RGB observations at selected timestamps, which provide temporally localized visual guidance for generating coherent in-between trajectories. Furthermore, to advance evaluation standards, we curate RealEstate10K-Cap, a large-scale text-camera dataset, and establish a cross-domain benchmark with a Universal CLaTr Evaluator. Extensive experiments demonstrate that TKCAM surpasses recent state-of-the-art baselines on Fréchet distance (FID), text-motion matching scores, and retrieval metrics (R@K), while additional analyses evaluate temporal smoothness and cross-domain generalization. Code and RealEstate10K-Cap dataset are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[OmniSeek: Native Tool Integration for Multi-turn Audio-Visual Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02181)**  `arXiv:2610.02181`  `cs.CV`  
  _Haibo Wang, Jiteng Mu, Jialu Li, Jingru Yi, Yuanjun Xiong, Jianming Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present OmniSeek, an agentic framework that transforms an Omni Large Language Model (Omni-LLM) into an active, multi-turn reasoning agent with native tool use. Rather than passively processing an entire audio-visual sequence in a single forward pass, OmniSeek makes evidence acquisition part of the reasoning process: it dynamically decides whether to look or listen, and over which temporal window, to retrieve sparse but critical evidence across different modalities within long contexts. Through an iterative multi-turn protocol, the retrieved raw audio or visual segments are appended back into the context to support subsequent reasoning. To cold-start this capability, we build a data engine that synthesizes OmniTraj-170K, a corpus of multi-hop Chain-of-Thought trajectories with interleaved audio and visual evidence. We first supervise the model on these trajectories to instill multi-turn tool-use behavior, and then further optimize the policy via a two-stage reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards. Moreover, we introduce an Audio-Visual Necessity objective that explicitly rewards successful trajectories whose reasoning depends on both modalities, discouraging single-modality shortcuts. Extensive experiments across a wide range of benchmarks demonstrate that OmniSeek learns adaptive cross-modal evidence seeking and consistently improves audio-visual reasoning performance.
  </details>

- **[Revisiting Multi-View Stereo: A Sequence-to-Sequence Formulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.24850)**  `arXiv:2609.24850`  `cs.CV`  
  _Aoxiang Fan, Corentin Dumery, Nicolas Talabot, Pascal Fua_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Computing accurate geometry from multi-view images is a fundamental problem in computer vision. Recent feed-forward (FF) models jointly estimate 3D geometry and camera parameters, but they typically suffer from geometry distortion caused by reconstruction ambiguity, even when ground-truth camera parameters are supplied. In this paper, we study the multi-view stereo (MVS) problem with known camera parameters and propose a novel approach that bridges conventional MVS and FF methods. Rather than casting MVS as a sequence-to-one mapping that predicts depth only for a single reference view, we reformulate it as a sequence-to-sequence task, akin to FF models, that jointly predicts geometry for all input views. We introduce a global transformer-based architecture with two components that explicitly exploit camera-induced priors: ray-map embeddings that inject camera parameters into image patch tokens, making the transformer camera-aware, and a unified global cost volume that replaces conventional per-view cost volumes to jointly capture 3D structure across all views. Extensive experiments on multiple public benchmarks show our approach achieves state-of-the-art performance, surpassing both MVS and FF reconstruction baselines.
  </details>

- **[FreeTransformSR: Efficient Lightweight Image Super-Resolution via Free Low-Rank Learnable Transform](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05912)**  `arXiv:2609.05912`  `cs.CV`  
  _Hongji Li, Yunhui Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Single image super-resolution aims to reconstruct high-resolution images from low-resolution inputs. This paper proposes FreeTransformSR, a novel lightweight super-resolution network based on a channel-wise free low-rank learnable transform. The transform learns task-adaptive basis functions in a data-driven manner, enabling adaptive feature modulation with minimal parameter overhead.
