To further enhance high-frequency detail recovery, we introduce a local feature modulation branch that complements transform-domain processing with depthwise convolution. In addition, a soft complexity adaptive module dynamically fuses the outputs of local convolution and window self-attention branches through a lightweight gating network, adaptively adjusting the fusion ratio based on regional texture characteristics. An adaptive intensity modulation strategy is also incorporated to adjust transform-domain response strength at the sample level, enabling the network to dynamically adjust processing intensity according to input features. Extensive experiments on five benchmark datasets demonstrate that FreeTransformSR achieves competitive PSNR/SSIM performance with significantly fewer parameters and FLOPs. Specifically, FreeTransformSR achieves 32.41 dB on BSD100 x2 and 27.00 dB on Urban100 x4 with only 595K parameters, while delivering faster inference speed than competing methods, making it well-suited for deployment in resource-constrained scenarios. Source code is available at:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Image Forgery Detection under Generative and Composited Background Replacement: A Benchmark with Processing-Matched Controls](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26232)**  `arXiv:2607.26232`  `cs.CV`  
  _Bugra Alperen Uluirmak, Rifat Kurban_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Consumer tools can now keep the subject of a photograph and replace, relight or edit everything behind it. Such edits place genuine people and objects in scenes they were never in, yet involve processing--resampling, harmonization, diffusion autoencoders--that also occurs in benign workflows. We introduce BG-REAL, a benchmark of 25,624 images built from 2,485 Open Images photographs with a dominant subject and real donor scenes. Each recipient is edited by donor compositing, learned harmonization, generative background inpainting, foreground-conditioned relighting and local background edits, and is paired with authentic controls that share the processing but not the semantic edit; all images of a recipient share file format and JPEG quality. Three generators (including FLUX.1 Fill), scene categories and subject classes are held out. Seven detectors published between 2022 and 2026 reach zero-shot AUROCs of at most 0.745 and have no useful operating point: manipulation localisers mark the preserved subject instead of the replaced background, and a detector of AI-generated images flags most authentic images passed through a diffusion autoencoder. After fine-tuning, the strongest detectors reach an AUROC of about 0.95, while re-implemented background-forgery detectors remain below 0.80. Controls show that full-background replacements by the training generators are detected from the edit itself, whereas local edits and SDXL and FLUX edits remain hard, and that full-background localisation largely reduces to subject segmentation. A model-agnostic training objective that uses the controls improves detection on unseen subjects for one detector and on local edits for another without changing in-distribution accuracy. The data are released.
  </details>

- **[What Pixels Are Enough? SEAMS: Sufficiency Saliency via MSE-Preservation Soft-Masks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09164)**  `arXiv:2607.09164`  `cs.CV`  
  _Magdalena Trędowicz, Łukasz Struski, Arkadiusz Lewicki, Karolina Pachota, Andrzej Grudzień, Mateusz Jagła, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Saliency maps are most useful when they identify the image regions that are sufficient to preserve a model's behaviour. We introduce SEAMS, a sufficiency-based saliency method that directly optimises a soft mask using a preservation objective. Given a frozen differentiable model output, such as a class probability, CLS embedding, or token representation, SEAMS searches for a compact mask that preserves the selected output. The approach relies on a simple optimisation framework based on soft masks, a learnable budget, and a three-way image composite generated entirely from the query image. As a result, it requires no auxiliary distractor dataset, architecture-specific attribution mechanism, or differentiable top-k relaxation. Experiments with frozen ViT-S/16 and ConvNeXt models show that the same optimisation pipeline can generate object-level, class-conditioned, and token-level explanations by changing only the preserved target. The resulting masks are compact, interpretable, stable across random initialisations, and competitive on insertion and deletion benchmarks. Our results also indicate that different architectures often rely on different sufficient evidence while achieving similar preservation fidelity, highlighting the architecture-dependent nature of visual explanations.
  </details>

- **[UniverSat: Resolution- and Modality-Agnostic Transformers for Earth Observation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23503)**  `arXiv:2606.23503`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yohann Perron, Guillaume Astruc, Nicolas Gonthier, Clement Mallet, Loic Landrieu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision Transformers (ViT) dominate computer vision. However, their reliance on rigid patch projectors hinders transfer to Earth Observation (EO), where input modalities, scales, and resolutions vary widely. We introduce UniverSat, a ViT-style backbone built around a Universal Patch Encoder that maps patches from arbitrary spatial, spectral, and temporal resolutions, and from both optical and non-optical sensors, into a shared embedding space with a shared set of weights. This enables training a single model on heterogeneous multimodal corpora via self-supervision, yielding robust, sensor-agnostic spatial features. We validate this approach with strong results across classification and segmentation on standard EO benchmarks from GeoBench, PANGEABench, and SpectralEarth. Our code and models are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Train the Agent, Not the Expert: Learning to Harness Heterogeneous Experts for Multi-Turn Visual Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29894)**  `arXiv:2605.29894`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yaowu Fan, Tao Han, Dazhao Du, Andy J. Ma, Jia Wan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in computer vision have produced powerful specialized models for detection, segmentation, counting, and other visual tasks. However, these models are typically optimized for isolated tasks, limiting their ability to support general-purpose visual intelligence, particularly when complex language understanding and dense small-object perception are required. We propose VisHarness, a trainable visual agent that decouples high-level perception, reasoning, and decision-making from low-level task execution. Rather than training a task-specific model, we train VisHarness to harness a set of carefully designed heterogeneous visual experts. This paradigm preserves the agent's general intelligence while leveraging the precision of specialized visual experts for specific tasks. With only lightweight training, VisHarness learns a generalizable policy for harnessing visual experts, enabling it to solve fundamental vision tasks in diverse complex scenarios through multi-turn interactions with these experts. To enable efficient on-policy reinforcement learning in a live environment, we introduce dynamic visual memory archiving to reduce the visual-token overhead that rapidly accumulates during multi-turn interactions with visual experts. Experiments on four representative benchmarks spanning reasoning segmentation, generalized referring expression segmentation, dense small-object detection, and referring expression counting show that VisHarness substantially outperforms existing general-purpose models and is competitive with or superior to task-specific models. Our code, model weights, and training data are publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Semantic-Enriched Latent Visual Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.19342)**  `arXiv:2605.19342`  `cs.CV`  
  _Tianrun Xu, Yue Sun, Qixun Wang, Jingyi Lu, Yuan Wang, Tianren Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal latent-space reasoning aims to replace explicit thinking with images by performing visual reasoning directly in a compact latent space. However, existing approaches largely rely on visual supervision and produce latent representations that lack sufficient semantic richness, limiting their ability to support diverse region-level reasoning tasks. In this work, we introduce Semantic-Enriched Latent Visual Reasoning (SLVR), a two-stage learning framework that enriches latent representations with attribute-level visual semantics and aligns them with diverse reasoning objectives. In the first stage, SLVR learns semantically enriched region-centric latents under fine-grained attribute supervision. In the second stage, we design Multi-query Group Relative Policy Optimization (M-GRPO) to align latent representations across multiple queries grounded in the same region. To support this framework, we construct SLV-Set, comprising approximately 400K region-level attribute annotations and 800K multi-query question answering samples, and introduce SV-QA, a benchmark that evaluates latent reasoning under semantic variation. Experiments demonstrate that SLVR improves the robustness and semantic consistency of latent visual reasoning compared to existing baselines.
  </details>

- **[EvoGround: Self-Evolving Video Agents for Video Temporal Grounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.13803)**  `arXiv:2605.13803`  `cs.CV`  
  _Minjoon Jung, Byoung-Tak Zhang, Lorenzo Torresani_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Video temporal grounding (VTG) takes an untrimmed video and a natural-language query as input and localizes the temporal moment that best matches the query. Existing methods rely on large, task-specific datasets requiring costly manual annotation. We introduce EvoGround, a framework of two coupled self-evolving agents, a proposer and a solver, that learn temporal grounding from raw videos without any task-specific annotations. The proposer generates query--moment pairs from raw videos, while the solver learns to ground them and feeds back signals that improve the proposer in return. Through this self-reinforcing reinforcement-learning loop, the two agents are initialized from the same backbone and mutually improve across iterations. Trained on 2.5K unlabeled videos, EvoGround matches or surpasses most fully supervised models across five VTG benchmarks, while emerging as a state-of-the-art fine-grained video captioner. We publicly release all resources atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Asymmetric Flow Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.12964)**  `arXiv:2605.12964`  `cs.CV`  
  _Hansheng Chen, Jan Ackermann, Minseo Kim, Gordon Wetzstein, Leonidas Guibas_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Flow-based generation in high-dimensional pixel spaces is difficult because velocity prediction requires modeling high-dimensional noise, even when data has strong low-rank structure. We present Asymmetric Flow Modeling (AsymFlow), a rank-asymmetric velocity parameterization that restricts noise prediction to a low-rank subspace while keeping data prediction full-dimensional. From this asymmetric prediction, AsymFlow analytically recovers the full-dimensional velocity, leaving the network architecture and the standard flow-matching training and sampling procedures unchanged. On ImageNet 256$\times$256, AsymFlow achieves a leading 1.57 FID, outperforming prior DiT/JiT-like pixel diffusion models by a large margin. AsymFlow also provides the first-ever route for finetuning pretrained latent flow models into pixel-space models: aligning the low-rank pixel subspace to the latent space gives a seamless initialization that preserves the latent model's high-level semantics and structure, so finetuning mainly improves low-level mismatches rather than relearning pixel generation. We show that the pixel AsymFlow model finetuned from FLUX.2 klein 9B establishes a new state of the art for pixel-space text-to-image generation, beating its latent base on HPSv3, DPG-Bench, and GenEval while qualitatively showing substantially improved visual realism.
  </details>

- **[LiBrA-Net: Lie-Algebraic Bilateral Affine Fields for Real-Time 4K Video Dehazing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.11508)**  `arXiv:2605.11508`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yongcong Wang, Chengchao Shen, Guangwei Gao, Wei Wang, Pengwen Dai, Dianjie Lu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Currently, there is a gap in the field of ultra-high-definition (UHD) video dehazing due to the lack of a benchmark for evaluation. Furthermore, existing video dehazing methods cannot run on consumer-grade GPUs when processing continuous UHD sequences of 3--5 frames at a time. In this paper, we address both issues with a new benchmark and an efficient method. Our key observation is that atmospheric dehazing reduces to a per-pixel affine transform governed by the low-frequency depth field, which can be compactly encoded in bilateral grids whose prediction cost is decoupled from the output resolution. Building on this, we propose LiBrA-Net, which factorizes the spatiotemporal affine field into a spatial--color and a temporal bilateral sub-grid predicted at a fixed low resolution, fuses their coefficients in the $\mathfrak{gl}(3)$ Lie algebra under group-theoretic regularization, maps the result to invertible $GL(3)$ transforms via a Cayley parameterization, and restores high-frequency detail through a lightweight input-guided branch. We further release UHV-4K, the first paired 4K video dehazing benchmark with depth, transmission, and optical-flow annotations on every frame. Across UHV-4K, REVIDE, and HazeWorld, LiBrA-Net sets a new state of the art among compared video dehazing methods while running native 4K at 25FPS on a single GPU with only 6.12M parameters. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Disambiguating 2D-3D Correspondences in Gaussian Splatting-based Feature Fields for Visual Localization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.07351)**  `arXiv:2605.07351`  `cs.CV`  
  _Miso Lee, Sangeek Hyun, Yerim Jeon, Jae-Pil Heo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While Gaussian Splatting-based Feature Fields (GSFFs) have shown promise for visual localization, this paper highlights that photometrically optimized GSFFs are inherently not suitable for 2D-3D matching. The volumetric extent of each Gaussian induces many-to-one pixel-to-point mappings that destabilize PnP-based pose estimation, while photometric optimization gives rise to superfluous Gaussians devoid of multi-view consistency. To address these issues, we propose SplitGS-Loc, a GSFFs construction framework for localization, which disambiguates 2D-3D correspondences by exploiting Gaussian attributes. Our key design, Mixture-of-Gaussians-based splitting, decomposes each Gaussian into smaller Gaussians, replacing ambiguous many-to-one with precise one-to-one correspondences. In parallel, we exploit composition weights from GS rasterization to select Gaussians that significantly and consistently contribute across multiple views and aggregate discriminative features through strong pixel-Gaussian associations. The resulting compact yet discriminative feature fields enable stable PnP convergence, achieving competitive performance on localization benchmarks. Extensive experiments validate that SplitGS-Loc extends the utility of photometric GSFFs to accurate and efficient localization by exploiting Gaussian attributes, without per-scene training for feature fields or iterative pose refinement. The code is available at:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Gen-Searcher: Reinforcing Agentic Search for Image Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.28767)**  `arXiv:2603.28767`  `cs.CV`  
  _Kaituo Feng, Manyuan Zhang, Shuang Chen, Yunlong Lin, Kaixuan Fan, Yilei Jiang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent image generation models have shown strong capabilities in generating high-fidelity and photorealistic images. However, they are fundamentally constrained by frozen internal knowledge, thus often failing on real-world scenarios that are knowledge-intensive or require up-to-date information. In this paper, we present Gen-Searcher, as the first attempt to train a search-augmented image generation agent, which performs multi-hop reasoning and search to collect the textual knowledge and reference images needed for grounded generation. To achieve this, we construct a tailored data pipeline and curate two high-quality datasets, Gen-Searcher-SFT-10k and Gen-Searcher-RL-6k, containing diverse search-intensive prompts and corresponding ground-truth synthesis images. We further introduce KnowGen, a comprehensive benchmark that explicitly requires search-grounded external knowledge for image generation and evaluates models from multiple dimensions. Based on these resources, we train Gen-Searcher with SFT followed by agentic reinforcement learning with dual reward feedback, which combines text-based and image-based rewards to provide more stable and informative learning signals for GRPO training. Experiments show that Gen-Searcher brings substantial gains, improving Qwen-Image by around 16 points on KnowGen and 15 points on WISE. We hope this work can serve as an open foundation for search agents in image generation, and we fully open-source our data, models, and code.
  </details>

- **[TAU-R1: Visual Language Model for Traffic Anomaly Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.19098)**  `arXiv:2603.19098`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yuqiang Lin, Kehua Chen, Sam Lockyer, Arjun Yadav, Mingxuan Sui, Shucheng Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Traffic Anomaly Understanding (TAU) is important for traffic safety in Intelligent Transportation Systems. Recent vision-language models (VLMs) have shown strong capabilities in video understanding. However, progress on TAU remains limited due to the lack of benchmarks and task-specific methodologies. To address this limitation, we introduce Roundabout-TAU, a dataset constructed from real-world roundabout videos collected in collaboration with the City of Carmel, Indiana. The dataset contains 342 clips and is annotated with more than 2,000 question-answer pairs covering multiple aspects of traffic anomaly understanding. Building on this benchmark, we propose TAU-R1, a two-layer vision-language framework for TAU. The first layer is a lightweight anomaly classifier that performs coarse anomaly categorisation, while the second layer is a larger anomaly reasoner that generates detailed event summaries. To improve task-specific reasoning, we introduce a two-stage training strategy consisting of decomposed-QA-enhanced supervised fine-tuning followed by TAU-GRPO, a GRPO-based post-training method with TAU-specific reward functions. Experimental results show that TAU-R1 achieves strong performance on both anomaly classification and reasoning tasks while maintaining deployment efficiency. The dataset and code are available at:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Tracking the Discriminative Axis: Dual Prototypes for Test-Time OOD Detection Under Covariate Shift](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.15213)**  `arXiv:2603.15213`  `cs.CV`  
  _Wooseok Lee, Jin Mo Yang, Saewoong Bahk, Hyung-Sin Kim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  For reliable deployment of deep-learning systems, out-of-distribution (OOD) detection is indispensable. In the real world, where test-time inputs often arrive as streaming mixtures of in-distribution (ID) and OOD samples under evolving covariate shifts, OOD samples are domain-constrained and bounded by the environment, and both ID and OOD are jointly affected by the same covariate factors. Existing methods typically assume a stationary ID distribution, but this assumption breaks down in such settings, leading to severe performance degradation. We empirically discover that, even under covariate shift, covariate-shifted ID (csID) and OOD (csOOD) samples remain separable along a discriminative axis in feature space. Building on this observation, we propose DART, a test-time, online OOD detection method that dynamically tracks dual prototypes -- one for ID and the other for OOD -- to recover the drifting discriminative axis, augmented with multi-layer fusion and flip correction for robustness. Extensive experiments on a wide range of challenging benchmarks, where all datasets are subjected to 15 common corruption types at severity level 5, demonstrate that our method significantly improves performance, yielding 15.32 percentage points (pp) AUROC gain and 49.15 pp FPR@95TPR reduction on ImageNet-C vs. Textures-C compared to established baselines. These results highlight the potential of the test-time discriminative axis tracking for dependable OOD detection in dynamically changing environments.
  </details>

- **[DC-ViT: Modulating Spatial and Channel Interactions for Multi-Channel Images](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.14281)**  `arXiv:2603.14281`  `cs.CV`  
  _Umar Marikkar, Syed Sameed Husain, Muhammad Awais, Sara Atito_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Training and evaluation in multi-channel imaging (MCI) remains challenging due to heterogeneous channel configurations arising from varying staining protocols, sensor types, and acquisition settings. This heterogeneity limits the applicability of fixed-channel encoders commonly used in general computer vision. Recent Multi-Channel Vision Transformers (MC-ViTs) address this by enabling flexible channel inputs, typically by jointly encoding patch tokens from all channels within a unified attention space. However, unrestricted token interactions across channels can lead to feature dilution, reducing the ability to preserve channel-specific semantics that are critical in MCI data. To address this, we propose Decoupled Vision Transformer (DC-ViT), which explicitly regulates information sharing using Decoupled Self-Attention (DSA), which decomposes token updates into two complementary pathways: spatial updates that model intra-channel structure, and channel-wise updates that adaptively integrate cross-channel information. This decoupling mitigates informational collapse while allowing selective inter-channel interaction. To further exploit these enhanced channel-specific representations, we introduce Decoupled Aggregation (DAG), which allows the model to learn task-specific channel importances. Extensive experiments across three MCI benchmarks demonstrate consistent improvements over existing MC-ViT approaches. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Rotation Equivariant Mamba for Vision Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.09138)**  `arXiv:2603.09138`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zhongchen Zhao, Qi Xie, Keyu Huang, Lei Zhang, Deyu Meng, Zongben Xu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Rotation equivariance is an important structural prior for a broad range of vision tasks, particularly in domains such as remote sensing and medical imaging where objects have no canonical orientation, yet it remains notably absent from current Mamba-based vision architectures. Despite the success of Mamba in natural language processing and its growing adoption in computer vision, existing visual Mamba models fail to account for rotational symmetry in their design. This omission renders them sensitive to image rotations, thereby constraining their robustness and cross-task generalization. To address this limitation, we incorporate rotation symmetry, an important geometric prior in many visual domains, into Mamba-based architectures. Specifically, we introduce EQ-VMamba, the first rotation equivariant visual Mamba architecture for vision tasks. The core components of EQ-VMamba include a carefully designed rotation equivariant cross-scan strategy and group Mamba blocks. Moreover, we provide a rigorous theoretical analysis of the intrinsic equivariance error, demonstrating that the proposed architecture enforces end-to-end rotation equivariance under the p4 rotation group. Extensive experiments across multiple benchmarks---including high-level image classification, mid-level semantic segmentation, and low-level image super-resolution---demonstrate that EQ-VMamba consistently improves rotation robustness and achieves superior or competitive performance compared to non-equivariant baselines on most benchmarks, while requiring 40-70% fewer parameters. These results indicate that embedding rotation equivariance not only effectively bolsters the robustness of visual Mamba models against rotation transformations, but also improves accuracy on data exhibiting rotational symmetry, with substantially improved parameter efficiency. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[LEADER: Lightweight End-to-End Attention-Gated Dual Encoder-Decoder Network for Robust Minutiae Extraction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.15493)**  `arXiv:2602.15493`  `cs.CV`  
  _Raffaele Cappelli, Matteo Ferrara_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This paper introduces LEADER (Lightweight End-to-end Attention-gated Dual Encoder-decodeR), a neural network that maps raw fingerprint images to minutiae descriptors, including location, direction, and type. The proposed architecture integrates non-maximum suppression and angular decoding to enable end-to-end inference using only 0.9M parameters. It employs a novel ``Castle-Moat-Rampart'' ground-truth encoding and a dual encoder-decoder architecture, interconnected through an attention-gating mechanism. Experimental evaluations demonstrate state-of-the-art accuracy on plain fingerprints and robust cross-domain generalization to latent impressions across two benchmark datasets. Specifically, LEADER outperforms top-tier commercial systems, achieving F1-score gains of up to 34% and 39% over the closest competitor on NIST SD27 and SD302, respectively. Qualitative visual analysis suggests that the internal representations learned by the model align with established fingerprint domain concepts, such as segmentation masks, orientation fields, frequency maps, and skeletons. Inference requires only 15ms on GPU (average latency per image at batch size 32) and 322ms on CPU, outperforming leading commercial software in computational efficiency. The source code and pre-trained weights are publicly released to facilitate reproducibility.
  </details>

- **[Benchmarking and Learning Object-Centric Procedural Understanding in Instructional Videos](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.03479)**  `arXiv:2512.03479`  `cs.CV`  
  _Wenliang Guo, Yu Kong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Procedural activities are fundamentally driven by object state transitions, yet existing instructional video benchmarks remain action-centric and cannot evaluate whether models reason about how objects evolve toward task completion. In this work, we introduce ProcObject-10K, the first benchmark that jointly evaluates object-centric reasoning and temporal evidence grounding in instructional videos, across both egocentric and exocentric views. It comprises 10,522 open-ended VideoQA pairs grounded in 1,799 videos, spanning 137 tasks across 9 domains and five reasoning types covering preconditions, state evolution, counterfactuals, mistakes, and readiness. Benchmarking 13 leading MLLMs reveals a substantial answering-grounding gap: models produce plausible answers while failing to localize the supporting evidence (mIoU < 45%), exposing their reliance on linguistic priors rather than recognizing and reasoning about the object dynamics. As a step toward closing this gap, we further provide an object-centric supervised fine-tuning baseline with pseudo object-level supervision and spatial-temporal constraints. Models fine-tuned on ProcObject-10K not only improve on the benchmark itself, but also transfer effectively to other grounded VideoQA and embodied planning tasks. The dataset, annotations, and evaluation toolkit have been publicly released on our project page to support future research on object-centric procedural understanding:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Inverse-and-Edit: Simple and Effective Framework for Fast Image Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.19103)**  `arXiv:2506.19103`  `cs.CV`  
  _Ilia Beletskii, Andrey Kuznetsov, Aibek Alanov_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion-based text-to-image models have recently been distilled into few-step samplers and fast generators, reducing inference cost while maintaining high image quality. However, text-guided editing with such accelerated backbones remains challenging: inversion in a small number of steps is unreliable, local errors accumulate along the trajectory, and existing fast-editing methods typically rely on backbone-specific inversion procedures, which limits their transferability. In this work, we propose a simple and universal framework for text-guided image editing with arbitrary accelerated diffusion and flow-matching models. Instead of optimizing local, per-timestep objectives, we directly minimize a global editing objective over the entire inversion-generation trajectory. The framework fine-tunes only 1.5-7 % of the base-model parameters through two lightweight LoRA adapters (forward and backward), trained on fewer than 10 K triplets from the publicly available HQ-Edit dataset. We validate our method on three architecturally distinct backbones: consistency-distilled SD1.5 LCM-Dreamshaper, adversarial-distilled SDXL-Turbo, and rectified-flow-distilled Z-Image-Turbo. On PIE-Bench, our method achieves the strongest editing alignment within each backbone family while ensuring a strong level of content preservation. Inference adds no extra steps beyond the standard inversion and editing passes. The project page with visual results is available atthis http URL.
  </details>

- **[EarthScape: A Multimodal Benchmark for Geologic Mapping and Surface-Aware Learning under Geographic Domain Shift](https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.15625)**  `arXiv:2503.15625`  `cs.CV`  
  _Matthew A. Massey, Nusrat Munia, Abdullah-Al-Zubaer Imran_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Understanding natural Earth surface processes requires integrating topography, imagery, hydrology, and human infrastructure while remaining robust to geographic variation, yet few benchmarks support both challenges. We introduce EarthScape, a multimodal benchmark for geologic mapping and surface-aware learning under geographic domain shift. EarthScape combines aerial imagery, LiDAR-derived elevation, multi-scale terrain derivatives, and contextual vector data into 37 co-registered input channels per sample, together with pixel-level geologic masks. Its geographically separated regions share a common label space, enabling controlled evaluation of in-domain and cross-region generalization under realistic class imbalance and spatial complexity. Benchmark experiments show that engineered terrain representations transfer more robustly than raw elevation, while spatial support and multimodal terrain inputs strongly influence performance. Fusion effectiveness depends on backbone and evaluation domain, with more complex cross-attention providing no consistent advantage over simpler strategies. These representation trends extend across geospatial foundation models and semantic segmentation, while class-level results show that no single terrain feature or scale is optimal across geologic classes. Together, EarthScape provides a benchmark for studying representation design, multimodal fusion, and geographic robustness in surface-aware geospatial learning. Our source code is available atthis https URLand the dataset atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[PixelPonder: Dynamic Patch Adaptation for Enhanced Multi-Conditional Text-to-Image Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.06684)**  `arXiv:2503.06684`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yanjie Pan, Qingdong He, Zhengkai Jiang, Pengcheng Xu, Chaoyi Wang, Jinlong Peng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in diffusion-based text-to-image generation have demonstrated promising results through visual condition control. However, existing ControlNet-like methods struggle with compositional visual conditioning - simultaneously preserving semantic fidelity across multiple heterogeneous control signals while maintaining high visual quality, where they employ separate control branches that often introduce conflicting guidance during the denoising process, leading to structural distortions and artifacts in generated images. To address this issue, we present PixelPonder, a novel unified control framework, which allows for effective control of multiple visual conditions under a single control structure. Specifically, we design a patch-level adaptive condition selection mechanism that dynamically prioritizes spatially relevant control signals at the sub-region level, enabling precise local guidance without global interference. Additionally, a time-aware control injection scheme is deployed to modulate condition influence according to denoising timesteps, progressively transitioning from structural preservation to texture refinement and fully utilizing the control information from different categories to promote more harmonious image generation. Extensive experiments demonstrate that PixelPonder surpasses previous methods across different benchmark datasets, showing superior improvement in spatial alignment accuracy while maintaining high textual semantic consistency.
  </details>

- **[Transferable Multiform Attack for Cross-Modal Person Re-Identification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2409.17977)**  `arXiv:2409.17977`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yunpeng Gong, Can Yang, Qingyuan Zeng, Dejun Xu, Zhiming Luo, Zhenzhong Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Cross-modal person re-identification (ReID) adversarial attacks face a transferability challenge: existing methods may exhibit source-pair overfitting, where learned perturbations become overly dependent on the feature statistics of the source modality pair, thereby limiting their transferability under heterogeneous domain shifts. We argue that a key challenge is reducing source-pair-specific bias while maintaining attack effectiveness. To address this, we propose Multiform Attack (MA). The framework first learns a universal perturbation via Mahalanobis-guided gradient optimization. Since this perturbation may still retain source-pair-specific bias, the second stage performs sparse refinement through attention-shift-filtered multi-objective evolutionary search, leveraging heterogeneous source distributions in transfer settings. By separating universal perturbation learning from sparse evolutionary refinement, MA aims to reduce reliance on source-pair-specific patterns and improve transferability to unseen domains. Extensive experiments demonstrate strong attack effectiveness on cross-modal ReID benchmarks and improved transferability in the evaluated held-out-domain settings, with additional evaluations across model architectures.
  </details>

- **[Latent Path Exploration via Potential-Guided Subtrajectory Balance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40283)**  `arXiv:2610.40283`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Xiaoling Zeng, Qi Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Continuous latent reasoning enables language models to perform intermediate computation without generating explicit reasoning traces. Advances in stochastic latent reasoning allow multiple paths to be sampled in continuous space, but stochasticity alone does not ensure effective exploration. A key challenge is guiding stochastic transitions between intermediate latent states toward useful reasoning paths. We introduce SubLAT, which couples a relative answer potential with Subtrajectory Balance to learn stochastic latent exploration. The relative potential evaluates the answer support of sampled states against replacement states, with a correctness signal at the terminal state. Subtrajectory Balance relates potential differences to forward and backward transition densities across subtrajectories of different lengths. The model is jointly trained with answer supervision, CoT supervision, and representation distillation. Experiments on arithmetic and deductive reasoning show increasing solution coverage with larger sampling budgets across in-domain and out-of-domain benchmarks, as well as improvements on several structural OOD benchmarks. Trajectory analyses reveal progressive divergence among sampled latent paths and higher mean relative potentials for correct trajectories across arithmetic benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[Blind Spots: Where Small Language Models Fall Behind Frontier Models on Terminal Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39006)**  `arXiv:2610.39006`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Ashima Suvarna, Ruta Desai, Ahmed Elgohary_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Small language models (SLMs) offer an attractive alternative to frontier models (FMs) because they can be deployed with lower cost and latency. This makes SLMs especially appealing for terminal tasks on edge devices, where responsiveness, local execution, and limited connectivity are important considerations. However, they continue to lag behind FMs on terminal tasks, as measured by standard task success rates. Yet these rates conflate several capabilities that may contribute to this gap. In this work, we characterize the SLM-FM gap along three axes: solvability, whether additional test-time compute allows an SLM to reach a correct solution; verification, whether it can identify a successful solution among candidate trajectories; and confidence, whether it can accurately anticipate its probability of success. Across two standard terminal benchmarks, we find that repeated sampling narrows, but does not eliminate, the gap in solvability. In contrast, a persistent gap remains in verification. Given the same candidate trajectory pool, FMs recover substantially more of the gap between random and oracle selection than SLMs. SLMs are also more overconfident in their prospective success estimates and exhibit poorer calibration. These results suggest that the SLM-FM gap is multidimensional, spanning not only the ability to generate successful solutions, but also the ability to recognize successful solutions and anticipate success. We hope this decomposition can guide future work on developing stronger and smaller terminal agents.
  </details>

- **[FAD-KV: Adaptive Dictionary KV Caching for Efficient Reasoning Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37921)**  `arXiv:2610.37921`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Yuxuan Zhu, David H. Yang, Parisa Shiravani, Mohammad Mohammadi Amiri, Tejaswini Pedapati, Subhajit Chaudhury, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Key-value (KV) caching is a core mechanism in large language models, where intermediate key and value representations are stored to accelerate autoregressive decoding. As generation length increases, KV cache memory and computation grow linearly, making compression critical for efficient long-output reasoning. Dictionary-based sparse KV compression is appealing because it represents each token's KV states as a sparse combination of learned basis elements (atoms) in a dictionary, preserving token-level structure without enforcing a global low-rank constraint. However, existing dictionary-based methods face two practical limitations during decoding: iterative sparse coding methods such as Orthogonal Matching Pursuit add decoding-time overhead, and static dictionaries may mismatch evolving KV distributions under test-time generation. While adaptive dictionary updates can reduce this mismatch, naive append-only strategies lead to dictionary growth during generation. We propose FAD-KV, a \textbf{F}ast \textbf{A}daptive \textbf{D}ictionary KV caching framework for efficient reasoning decoding. FAD-KV integrates (i) a screening-based sparse coding scheme, where a one-shot Top-\(n\) selection identifies a small candidate pool followed by lightweight coefficient refinement, and (ii) a fixed-size dictionary adaptation mechanism that reuses inactive atoms instead of expanding the dictionary. By controlling both sparse coding cost and adaptive dictionary growth, FAD-KV provides a memory-bounded and computation-efficient KV compression framework for long reasoning workloads. Experiments on reasoning and long-context benchmarks show that FAD-KV achieves a strong quality-efficiency trade-off while avoiding unbounded dictionary growth during decoding.
  </details>

- **[The Best Second Opinion Is Right Where the Primary Is Wrong: Partner Selection for Selective Span Tagging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37678)**  `arXiv:2610.37678`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Varun Kotte_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A span tagger that ranks its outputs by risk can defer the riskiest ones to a person, and a second model can sharpen this ranking. Which second model should it be? Partner selection is conditional, not global: the best second opinion is the model that is right where the primary is wrong. Across eight tagger types, three seeds and three English benchmarks (576 evaluated primary-partner pairings), choosing the partner by held-out accuracy on the primary's errors recovers 82% of the best partner's gain, against 69-70% for span F1 or a random pick. Frozen before a fourth dataset was chosen, the rule replicated there (Spearman 0.40 vs. 0.04 for span F1). Partner value is also directional. The predictive distribution is the second opinion: hard labels discard half of a partner's value, and scoring the served label improves 94% of pairings. Fixed before the fourth dataset was chosen, this score also replicated there. For LoRA-tuned decoders, independently trained partners beat every readout of the primary network. Applied zero-shot to 51 MASSIVE locales, every primary has a helpful trained partner in every locale, and the preferred partner follows competence.
  </details>

- **[Measuring Data Diversity with Embeddings: A Taxonomy and Benchmark for Diversity Measures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24888)**  `arXiv:2610.24888`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Cantao Su, Anna Wegmann, Esther Ploeger, Dong Nguyen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diversity is a critical property of text corpora, with direct implications for training data curation, test set design, and model generalization. However, its measurement remains fragmented and poorly understood. Existing embedding-based diversity measures vary widely in how they are computed and can yield substantially different diversity scores on the same data, yet no systematic comparison of their empirical behaviour exists. We make two contributions toward addressing this gap. First, we present a taxonomy of 21 embedding-based diversity measures, organizing them into four families based on their underlying computations. Second, we introduce EmbDivBench, a benchmark that evaluates these measures along three conceptually distinct components (variety, balance and disparity) on both simulated and natural text data. Although Mean Pairwise Distance is the most commonly used measure, our experiments show that it is outperformed by several alternatives on the three diversity components, with Graph Entropy being the best choice overall. Lastly, we distil these findings into practical recommendations for diversity measure selection.
  </details>

- **[MAVEN: A Macro-Societal Value Evaluation Framework of Multimodal Content with Compact Aligned Evaluators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18096)**  `arXiv:2608.18096`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Zijuan Zhao, Zheren Fu, Hou Xia, Licheng Zhang, Yi Liu, Zhendong Mao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Assessing whether multimodal content aligns with macro-societal values, such as peace, justice, and freedom, has become an increasingly urgent challenge. Existing frameworks are largely confined to safety-oriented taxonomies, text-only psychometric probes, or single-label classification. Therefore, we propose MAVEN, a hierarchical framework for macro-societal value evaluation of multimodal content, grounded in international human-rights instruments and cultural value theory. MAVEN organizes values into 6 primary dimensions and 72 secondary indicators, supporting multi-level quantitative scoring. Building on MAVEN, we construct a human-verified multimodal benchmark and a soft-match metric to evaluate VLMs' assessments across value dimensions. For evaluator optimization, we propose a span-adaptive variant of multi-level preference optimization for evaluator distillation, together with a training-free multi-role consensus strategy at inference time. We evaluate existing open- and closed-source VLMs on our benchmark, revealing shared tendencies and clear differences in macro-societal value judgments. Experiments show that our compact 2B evaluator matches its 8B counterpart in the same family and approaches frontier closed-source VLMs, offering a practical path toward scalable macro-societal value evaluation. Our SA-MDPO implementation and MacroValue-Bench are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Unlimited OCR Works 2](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39308)**  `arXiv:2610.39308`  `cs.CL` `cs.CV`  
  _Shu Wei, Jingjing Wu, Qunyi Xie, Lingshu Zhang, Xiangcheng Du, Yannian Fu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Unlimited OCR ushers the era of long-horizon parsing, substantially mitigating the growth of working memory as document lengthens. However, as document length scales to hundreds of pages, the visual context grows substantially, sharply increasing the computational cost of each decoding step and limiting the scalability of ultra-long document parsing. Inspired by human book transcription via localized focus without re-scanning preceding pages, we introduce Unlimited OCR 2, a document parsing model that looks only at what it transcribes. Building on Unlimited OCR, we upgrade all the decoder attention modules to Sparse Reference Sliding Window Attention (SR-SWA), bounding active reference tokens to a fixed capacity and thus sustaining constant computational costs across arbitrarily long documents. By combining SR-SWA with Unlimited-Horizon Decoding Paradigm, Unlimited OCR 2 enables document parsing over virtually unlimited lengths without prohibitive memory overhead. At the hundred-page scale, Unlimited OCR 2 achieves 7.0$\times$ and 5.6$\times$ per-step speedups over Full Attention and R-SWA, respectively. Furthermore, it delivers state-of-the-art end-to-end performance on both MPDocBench and MarathonDocBench, our newly proposed benchmark targeting hundred-page document parsing. Ultimately, the capacity for uninterrupted, one-shot document parsing signifies a fundamental paradigm shift into the OCR 3.0 era.
  </details>

- **[Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38095)**  `arXiv:2610.38095`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _NVIDIA, Aastha Jhunjhunwala, Aayushi Ashokkumar Srivastava, Abhibha Gupta, Abhinav Garg, Abhinav Khattar, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce Nemotron 3.5 Super and Nemotron 3.5 Lightning, open and efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer models designed for reasoning, long-context understanding, and agentic workloads. Super is a 120B-parameter, 12B-active multimodal model, while Lightning is a smaller 30B-parameter, 3B-active text model; both designed for efficient deployment. Both models combine large-scale distillation, long-context training, and native speculative decoding, while Super further incorporates Indic continued pretraining and agentic software-engineering mid-training. We develop a comprehensive post-training pipeline spanning supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning (RLVR and RLHF), complemented in Super by specialized teacher training and multi- teacher on-policy distillation (MOPD). This transforms Super from a text-only base model into a multimodal agent capable of reasoning over text, images, video, and interactive tool environments. We further explore test-time scaling through parallel agent execution and self-verification. Across training and deployment, we jointly optimize capability and efficiency. Indic continued pretraining improves Indian-language benchmarks by up to 9.82 percentage points, while agentic mid-training strengthens software-engineering capabilities and robustness across agent harnesses. The post-trained Super model achieves 51.78% on SWE-bench Pro, surpassing Nemotron 3 Ultra despite using approximately 4.6x fewer active parameters. Post-training MTP boosting improves speculative- decoding acceptance length by up to 21.8% under probabilistic draft sampling, while NVFP4 quantization achieves 99.62% median accuracy recovery relative to BF16. Inference optimizations enable up to 4,856 output tokens/s/GPU on GB200 while sustaining at least 50 tokens/s/user.
  </details>

- **[Artifact Distillation: Training-Free Transfer from Frontier to Small Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37680)**  `arXiv:2610.37680`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Mustafa Safa Ozdayi, Somin Wadhwa_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Closing the gap between frontier and smaller language models is usually approached by distillation: fine-tuning the small model (student) on a frontier model's (teacher) outputs, which binds transfer to training compute and leaves the transferred knowledge largely opaque. We propose Artifact Distillation: a teacher solves training instances and externalizes its problem-solving procedure into an artifact bank that a student queries at inference time, with no gradient updates. Across four LiveBench tasks, a bank written by Claude Opus 4.5 lets Claude Haiku 4.5 outperform unaided Claude Sonnet 4.5 on spatial reasoning (0.707 vs. 0.647) at 60% of its cost, and reach Sonnet 4.5's level on table joining at 89% of its cost. The same raw bank raises Qwen3.5-27B's pooled score from 0.730 to 0.800, showing that transfer is not confined to the teacher's model family.
  </details>

- **[Evaluating the Robustness of Proof Autoformalization in Lean 4](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14867)**  `arXiv:2606.14867`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Zhengtao Gui, Sheng Yang, Zhouxing Shi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Proof autoformalization aims to translate a mathematical informal proof in natural language into a formal proof in a formal language such as Lean 4. Existing LLM-based models for proof autoformalization are typically evaluated only on well-formed informal proofs from curated datasets. We argue that a robust proof autoformalizer must remain faithful even for informal proofs that diverge from these idealized ones, and we present the first study on the robustness of proof autoformalization models. We formulate two categories of perturbations: a global perturbation paraphrases the informal theorem-proof pair in a different style, under which the formalization should remain consistent; a local perturbation alters a value, symbol, or proof step, possibly in a counterfactual way, and a robust formalization should faithfully reflect the perturbation rather than reverting to the original or inferring a different one on its own. We build a benchmark with both perturbations on miniF2F and MATH-500, and automatically measure how stable a proof autoformalization's correctness is under global perturbations and how faithfully its output reflects local perturbations. We evaluate seven recent models: their correctness varies under global perturbations, and they mostly fail to remain faithful under local perturbations.
  </details>

- **[Self-Augmenting Retrieval for Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06474)**  `arXiv:2606.06474`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Paul Jünger, Justin Lovelace, Linxi Zhao, Dongyoung Go, Kilian Q. Weinberger_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion language models generate text by iteratively denoising an entire response in parallel, holding a noisy draft of the answer at every step. We show that these intermediate states surface salient entities early in the denoising trajectory, making them a useful lookahead signal for retrieval-augmented generation (RAG). We further demonstrate that grounding generation in retrieved evidence sharply reduces inter-token dependence, making RAG particularly well suited to parallel decoding. We exploit both through Self-Augmenting Retrieval for Diffusion Language Models (SARDI), a dynamic RAG framework that uses speculative token predictions to guide retrieval during denoising. SARDI is training-free, retriever-agnostic, and applicable to any reasoning-capable discrete diffusion language model. Across five multi-hop QA benchmarks, SARDI outperforms training-free diffusion and autoregressive retrieval baselines at up to 4x higher throughput.
  </details>

- **[Executable Schema Contracts: From Automatic Ingestion to Multi-Source Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05415)**  `arXiv:2606.05415`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Padmaja Jonnalagedda, Yuguang Yao, Xiang Gao, Hilaf Hasson, Kamalika Das_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Real-world data spans tables, documents, and semi-structured files with implicit semantics. Querying this data requires integrating evidence across inconsistent schemas and formats, yet existing approaches either demand costly manual engineering or bypass structure entirely. We present a system that automatically discovers an executable schema from raw multi-source data and uses it as a shared contract for knowledge graph construction and query-time retrieval. A closed-world field catalog constrains LLM-based schema discovery to attested fields; deterministic structural analysis infers identity keys, foreign keys, and source hierarchy; and the resulting schema drives extraction, deduplication, and cross-source linking into a provenance-aware knowledge graph. At query time the schema -- optionally extended via a monotonic protocol -- conditions a multi-tool agent routing retrieval across structured lookup, graph traversal, and vector search, returning grounded answers with traceable citations. In controlled zero-shot comparisons using the same LLM, data, and evaluation harness, the system improves over retrieval-only and decomposition-based baselines across four QA benchmarks, with ablations showing that schema-conditioned routing, structural intelligence, and schema-guided construction each contribute to the gains.
  </details>

- **[Scaling Mathematical Proof Supervision from Structured Textbooks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40394)**  `arXiv:2610.40394`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Youqing Fang, Zhouqi Hua, Yinhao Tang, Yuzhe Gu, Lingkai Kong, Bin Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Mathematical supervision for language models is typically flattened into isolated problem-solution pairs, discarding the curricular organization and evidential structure of the original sources. We introduce TOME-Math (Textbook-Oriented Mathematical Evidence), a large-scale corpus that converts 2,118 textbooks spanning the core areas of university-level mathematics subjects into approximately 380K structured proof-supervision records while preserving chapter order, proposition-proof links, dependency references, and source provenance. Leveraging these structural signals, we explore curriculum learning across multiple mathematical subjects and characterize scaling patterns with supervision volume and source breadth. At full scale, fine-tuning on TOME-Math raises the unweighted mean score across our held-out evaluations and established public benchmarks by 16.7 percentage points over the base model, while exceeding all three public instruction-data baselines evaluated in aggregate. Our findings highlight structured textbooks as both a scalable source of mathematical supervision and an empirical framework for studying the impact of data organization on mathematical reasoning. We will release the corpus to facilitate further research on structured mathematical supervision.
  </details>

- **[Decision and System One models in rheumatology: accuracy and calibration on specialty-training examination questions and diagnostic case vignettes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40318)**  `arXiv:2610.40318`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Alfredo Madrid-García, Diego Benavent, Fabian Lechner, Phillip Kremer, Johannes Knitza, Beatriz Merino-Barbancho_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Objectives: To evaluate the accuracy and calibration of Decision and System One models on rheumatology examination questions and diagnostic vignettes. Methods: Two benchmarks were analysed: 172 rheumatology questions from Spanish specialty-training examinations (expanded RheumaMIR dataset) in Spanish and English, and 70 English diagnostic vignettes from RheumBench with 5-18 candidate diagnoses, 52 of which included the reference diagnosis. Four System One models (Jev 1.13, Clef, Microsoft-Decision-1, Laya) and GPT-6 Luna (OpenAI Decisions API) were compared with GPT-6 Sol and two local generative models. The primary outcome was accuracy; secondary outcomes included calibration, discrimination, accuracy of answers with probability >=0.90 and language differences. Results: In RheumaMIR, Jev's accuracy was 93.0% in Spanish and 91.9% in English, below GPT-6 Sol (99.4%; Holm-adjusted P<=0.005); GPT-6 Luna reached 97.7%, Clef 91.9-93.0% and Microsoft-Decision-1 86.0-87.8%, without detectable language differences. Jev's answers with probability >=0.90 (76-77% of answers) were 98.5% correct. In RheumBench, accuracy was lower: GPT-6 Sol 76.9%, GPT-6 Luna 63.5%, Clef 59.6%, Jev 50.0% and Microsoft-Decision-1 40.4% (each System One model below GPT-6 Sol, Holm-adjusted P<=0.045; chance 9.6%). Jev became overconfident (mean probability 0.659; over/underconfidence index +0.159), and its answers with probability >=0.90 (25% of cases) were 84.6% correct, against 95.5% for GPT-6 Luna (42%). Laya performed near chance in both benchmarks. Conclusion: The best-performing System One models were highly accurate on examination questions but less accurate on diagnostic vignettes, remaining below GPT-6 Sol. Jev's high-confidence predictions were less reliable on vignettes, highlighting the need for task-specific evaluation and calibration.
  </details>

- **[Raincheck: Deferred Multimodal Prefill for Efficient Multi-Turn MLLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39562)**  `arXiv:2610.39562`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Wenjie Huang, Jun Zhang, Hongyaoxing Gu, Haoyang Huang, Tianqi Xu, Xinyue Hao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-turn conversations with multimodal large language models (MLLMs) reuse input context, but long multimodal inputs increase time to first token (TTFT). Query-aware hard pruning reduces prefill tokens but permanently excludes information that later questions may require. We propose Raincheck, a framework for deferred multimodal prefill that decouples initial token selection from later information availability. An existing pruning method selects tokens for initial prefill; Raincheck preserves and progressively prefills unselected tokens in source order using unused compute capacity during decode. Unfinished prefill continues after answer return during available scheduling gaps. Attention visibility control and affine state mapping enable context updates after generation begins in full-attention and hybrid recurrent models. Prefill-decode fusion reduces repeated weight reads. We evaluate Qwen3-VL-8B and Qwen3.5-9B with 30% initial multimodal-token retention. Across both models and four pruning methods, Raincheck recovers on average 77.7% and 60.4% of the quality lost to matched hard pruning on MultiVerse and ConvBench, respectively. On MultiVerse, the SGLang implementation reduces mean first-turn TTFT by up to 24.1% relative to full prefill, with less than 0.5% increase in mean time per output token (TPOT).
  </details>

- **[Cord: Confidence-Anchored Order Residual Decoding for Masked Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39372)**  `arXiv:2610.39372`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Chengwei Zhou, Zhaoyan Jia, Massoud Pedram, Gourav Datta_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The decoding order of masked diffusion language models (dLLMs) can substantially affect generation quality, yet common decoders typically apply the same reveal rule throughout generation. Confidence provides a strong default, but it measures how decisively a position can be predicted now, not how useful revealing that position will be for subsequent decoding. We propose Cord, a lightweight prompt-conditioned policy that retains confidence decoding as a stable reference and learns when and how to depart from it through geometric residuals over generation direction, proximity to revealed context, and two-sided infilling. The residuals adapt to both the prompt and the evolving partial response. Rather than sampling a new policy action at every decoding step, Cord samples a single low-dimensional coefficient matrix per rollout, which induces an entire state-dependent reveal trajectory. This yields a contextual-bandit formulation trained directly from sequence-level rewards, without step-level credit assignment or backbone updates. Across three dLLMs and five reasoning, code, and constraint-satisfaction benchmarks, Cord improves average task success from 32.0% under confidence decoding to 48.2%, and further improves over the best per-domain fixed residual. Behavioral audits show that the learned residuals induce substantial, task-dependent changes in reveal locality and two-sided infilling. Cord adds only $0.53$M trainable parameters, requires no additional backbone forward passes, and incurs only 5.39% inference latency overhead.
  </details>

- **[Benchmarking decision models against generative large language models on medical multiple-choice questions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38448)**  `arXiv:2610.38448`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Oscar Freyer, Daniel Glauert, Moritz Hofmann, Max Ostermann, Stephen Gilbert, Jakob Nikolas Kather_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Medical workflows often require selecting among predefined options. We compared 13 decision models with 15 generative large language model configurations, including different reasoning settings, using 1,273 four-option MedQA questions under text-only conditions. Accuracy ranged from 67.71% to 89.08% for decision models and from 78.32% to 96.86% for generative configurations. Among the tested configurations, Mercury Decide had the lowest mean final-attempt cost (USD 0.0069 per 1,000 questions), while Clef Omni had the shortest median request duration (0.291 seconds). Decision-model probabilities distinguished correct from incorrect answers, but error rates among answers assigned probabilities of at least 0.90 ranged from 1.71% to 12.77%. Answer agreement across three runs ranged from 92.30% to 100%, while probability repeatability varied. Decision models offered resource advantages alongside lower peak accuracy; selecting models for medical workflows requires considering probability quality and repeatability together with accuracy and the resources needed to detect and correct errors.
  </details>

- **[Statutory Article Retrieval from Arabic Judicial Narratives: Family Law and Law of Evidence in Saudi Arabia](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38336)**  `arXiv:2610.38336`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Zarah Shibli, Areej Alhothali, Lulwah Alharigy_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reliable legal information retrieval must ground legal reasoning in authoritative statutory sources. In Arabic judicial settings, long factual narratives must be mapped to concise articles that express abstract legal rules, creating a narrative-to-legal-rule abstraction gap. Using a benchmark of 200 synthetic Saudi Arabic judicial cases and 381 articles from the Family Law and the Law of Evidence, we compare word-level and char-4 BM25, zero-shot dense encoders, per-law retrieval, dense-sparse reciprocal rank fusion (RRF), and a proposed Decompose-and-Refine (DaR) pipeline. In DaR, a large language model splits each narrative into disputed legal issues and generates, for each issue, three retrieval channels: a statute-style paraphrase, legal keywords, and a candidate taxonomy chapter. On the whole corpus, the instructed Qwen3-Embedding-8B dense encoder reaches an MRR@10 of 0.849, the best result of any non-decomposed system. Under per-law scoring, this advantage reflects Family Law only: restricted to Evidence articles, the same encoder drops to an MRR@10 of 0.087, and fusion with BM25 raises it only to 0.207. DaR changes this picture, raising Evidence MRR@10 to 0.696 and Family MRR@10 to 0.864. Without decomposition, the two laws favor different strategies; under DaR, the same retriever achieves the best MRR@10 on both.
  </details>

- **[EPSBench: Benchmarking LLM Agents for Earnings Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38293)**  `arXiv:2610.38293`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Yanbin Yin, Chuyu Xu, Liqin Ye, Jing Peng, Chao Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Quarterly earnings forecasting requires an agent to connect financial evidence to the income-statement line items that produce a company's reported earnings. We introduce EPSBench, a benchmark for studying this ability across 149 firm-quarters from 86 US companies. Agents read a fixed collection of filings, earnings-call transcripts, and news, then estimate company-specific income-statement drivers with cited evidence. A shared accounting calculator converts these estimates into GAAP diluted earnings per share (EPS), providing a common basis for comparing forecast assumptions. We evaluate six models under a common harness, assessing point forecast error relative to date-matched analyst consensus and checking whether their prediction intervals achieve the stated coverage. Three models show no detectable paired difference in point forecast error from consensus, and three have higher error. Across all six models, nominal 80% intervals contain reported EPS in only 37--70% of runs that return an interval, and most misses fall above the upper bound because the forecasts run low. The driver records trace most of the error gap between models to operating-income estimates.
  </details>

- **[Protegrity-PII Technical Report](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30518)**  `arXiv:2610.30518`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Hardeep Bassi, Hui Ma, Luís Seabra, Koowum Joshi, Daniel Pereira, Averell Gatton_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present Protegrity-PII, a new PII detection model for structured and unstructured text. Protegrity-PII achieves a typed recall of 0.910 with a precision of 0.902 on a new benchmark we propose, PII-SCOPE. Our model processes text in 0.49 seconds per million characters at \$0.0005 per million characters on a single RTX 6000 using batched inference. Due in part to the expansive nature of the entity type taxonomy used in training, Protegrity-PII achieves superior out-of-distribution generalization when benchmarked against a selection of state of the art models and datasets.
  </details>

- **[Temporal Evidence-Parameter Co-Alignment for LLM Reasoning over Temporal Knowledge Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28176)**  `arXiv:2610.28176`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Jiapu Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models (LLMs) often struggle with temporally grounded reasoning because their static parametric memory is misaligned with the dynamic evolution of real-world knowledge. Although RAG and Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning (PEFT) provide practical ways to enhance LLMs, they remain insufficient for temporal reasoning. Existing retrieval methods typically prioritize semantic relevance while overlooking temporal validity, event order, and evolutionary dependencies, resulting in evidence that is relevant in isolation but temporally fragmented. Meanwhile, conventional PEFT methods usually employ fixed and input-agnostic low-rank updates, making it difficult to capture time-dependent entity semantics and relation drift. To address these limitations, we propose Temporal Evidence-Parameter Co-Alignment (TEPCA), a framework for LLM reasoning over TKGs. TEPCA aligns LLM reasoning with temporal knowledge at both the evidence and parameter levels. At the evidence level, it introduces time-constrained Dynamic Relevance-Guided Path (DRGP) pruning to preserve globally coherent temporal paths, together with Mutual-Information (MI)-based pruning to retain query-informative local facts under temporal constraints. At the parameter level, it integrates Dynamic-Gated LoRA (DG-LoRA), a timestamp-conditioned low-rank adaptation mechanism that dynamically modulates the effective adaptation subspace according to temporal context. This design enables LLMs to better capture fine-grained entity evolution and time-dependent relation semantics while preserving parameter efficiency. Experimental results on two TKG benchmarks demonstrate that TEPCA achieves state-of-the-art performance. Additional evaluations of DG-LoRA on eight commonsense QA datasets further verify its general input-adaptive capacity, scalability across LLM backbones, and interpretability.
  </details>

- **[Evaluating and Benchmarking the System One Model Jev](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.37647)**  `arXiv:2609.37647`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Tobias Deußer, Rajkumar Ramamurthy, Lorenz Sparrenberg, Rafet Sifa_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Jev is a commercial "System One" model from TypeSafe AI that does not generate text: given a state and typed questions, it returns a choice from fixed options, a position on a rubric, or the probability that a statement is true, with probabilities described as calibrated. Such models target small decisions in information access pipelines. We evaluate Jev (jev-1.13.0) zero-shot on 37 datasets, from classification and routing to moderation and rubric scoring, with one frozen template per dataset and full evaluation splits: 346,009 requests for under US$10. For reference, we score Qwen3.8-27B and Gemma-4-E4B on identical requests via their exact option probabilities. Jev reaches 95-99% accuracy on IMDB, SST-2, HellaSwag and ARC, beats Qwen on 27 of 37 datasets, with none of Qwen's leads outside the bootstrap intervals, and Gemma on all 37, and its choice probabilities are well calibrated and support selective prediction. All three models degrade on low-resource languages, fine-grained or noisy labels, and rubric-based judgments, and binary probabilities need tuned thresholds, which raise micro-F1 on UNFAIR-ToS from 0.50 to 0.75. Jev answers MMLU's calculation-heavy questions more accurately than other MMLU questions, unlike both open models; probes rule out shallow memorization but not memorized question-answer pairs. Extending the protocol to sequential decisions, where the model chooses the next action at every step, Jev completes 35% of ALFWorld's unseen tasks, 42% of 90 TextWorld games and 21% of BabyAI-Text missions, chooses the optimal Blackjack action in 90% of decisions, and on FrozenLake almost never moves away from the goal to avoid holes. It often repeats actions that had no effect. Qwen completes more ALFWorld and TextWorld games than Jev (54.5% and 51.1%) but shares its failures on BabyAI-Text and FrozenLake. We release the code, harness and all raw responses.
  </details>

- **[EHRNote-ChatQA: A Benchmark for Evidence-Grounded Multi-Turn Clinical Question Answering over Longitudinal Discharge Summaries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.15735)**  `arXiv:2606.15735`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Jiyoun Kim, Muhan Yeo, Eunhye Jang, Jeewon Yang, Hangyul Yoon, Su Ji Lee, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Discharge summaries are crucial clinical documents containing the context of a patient's overall hospital stay, and are routinely reviewed by medical experts for patient readmission, ongoing care, and diagnostic decision-making. When reviewing them, medical experts often must iteratively synthesize information across multiple discharge summaries while verifying the evidence supporting each answer. Although large language models (LLMs) are increasingly explored for clinical question answering, existing benchmarks do not sufficiently reflect this setting: they often evaluate exam-style medical knowledge or focus on single-turn question answering with limited evidence-grounding evaluation. We introduce EHRNote-ChatQA, the first benchmark for evidence-grounded multi-turn clinical question answering over patients' multiple discharge summaries. Built from de-identified MIMIC-IV discharge summaries, EHRNote-ChatQA contains 967 patient-level multi-turn samples spanning one to five notes and 16,072 medical-expert-verified QA pairs (8,036 content questions, each paired with an evidence-grounding question) across eight clinical categories. The benchmark is constructed through an expert-informed pipeline combining discharge-summary structuring schema, expert-curated multi-turn QA templates, and LLM-based generation, followed by review and revision of every single QA sample by 12 medical experts (11 physicians and 1 nurse). Benchmarking 24 proprietary and open-weight LLMs reveals several challenges, including that LLMs struggle more with evidence grounding than content answering, multi-turn errors compound across turns, and single-turn clinical QA performance does not reliably transfer to this setting. These findings establish EHRNote-ChatQA as a rigorous and practical benchmark for evaluating clinical QA systems.
  </details>

- **[JANUS: Evaluating Goal-Conditioned Distortion in Factually Grounded LLM Communication](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.10852)**  `arXiv:2606.10852`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Polydoros Giannouris, Mohsinul Kabir, Sophia Ananiadou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM deception is often evaluated through fabricated claims, explicit lies, or strategic concealment. Yet misleading communication can also arise from selectively presenting true facts, for example, by omitting adverse evidence, softening unfavorable details, emphasizing favorable information, or replacing precise qualifications with vague language. Existing benchmarks largely overlook this subtler failure mode. We introduce JANUS, a benchmark for measuring goal-conditioned pragmatic distortion in fact-grounded LLM outputs. Each of its 160 scenarios, spanning 8 domains, provides a fixed pool of favorable and adverse facts and contrasts a neutral decision support with institutional conditioning, such as increasing adoption, or support despite potential harm to affected individuals or groups. By constraining outputs to the same fact pool, JANUS isolates misleading net impressions from hallucination and fabrication. Experiments across 12 LLMs reveal consistent goal-conditioned distortions, showing that current models remain sensitive to incentives and framing and lack robust safeguards against selectively misleading communication. We publicly release our corpus and code.this https URL
  </details>

- **[Can I Take Another Dose? DoseBench: Evaluating LLM Decision-Making in OTC Dosing QA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.04262)**  `arXiv:2606.04262`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Maroof Kousar, Jinsung Han, Mohit Chandra, Munmun De Choudhury, Yibo Hu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to answer everyday health questions online, including whether someone can take another dose of an over-the-counter (OTC) medication. Yet this common safety-relevant setting remains underexplored in medical QA. Answering these questions requires checking previous doses, timing, and product-specific dosing limits, while recognizing when important information is missing. We introduce DoseBench, a focused benchmark of 230 consumer-style acetaminophen and ibuprofen dosing questions with human-annotated YES, NO, and AMBIGUOUS labels, and evaluate eight LLMs with five responses per question. Overall accuracy ranges from 44.8% to 62.2%, while models incorrectly approve 14.0% to 60.5% of prohibited doses. Models with similar accuracy can therefore make very different dosing decisions. Additional analyses show that these failures are not easily fixed by giving models more guidance. Asking models to check dose, timing, and missing information makes them more cautious, while providing drug facts improves accuracy but can make them overlook missing information. These findings highlight a broader challenge in health QA: applying rules correctly and recognizing when information is missing.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Questions: Evaluating the Stated Knowledge of LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.26937)**  `arXiv:2605.26937`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Luca Giordano, Simon Razniewski_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Parametric knowledge in large language models (LLMs) is a cornerstone of their success, yet remains poorly understood. Existing knowledge benchmarks typically rely on predefined questions (e.g., "What is the birth date of M.L. King?"), evaluating only knowledge that benchmark designers explicitly choose to query, a problematic availability bias. In this paper, we introduce open knowledge evaluation, a new paradigm for benchmarking the stated knowledge of LLMs. Instead of asking narrow questions, it evaluates models on the knowledge they choose to surface in response to open-ended elicitation prompts (e.g., "Tell me everything you know about M.L. King"). This shifts the focus from predefined answer retrieval toward characterizing the knowledge models naturally express. We instantiate this paradigm with BeQu (Beyond Questions), a benchmark of 10,000 entities paired with reference corpora for statement verification. Using BeQu, we evaluate a broad range of language models and analyze the effects of reasoning effort, model scale, prompt format, and knowledge domain. Data and leaderboard are available on this work's GitHub repository and atthis http URL, the benchmark website.
  </details>

- **[CHI-Bench: Can AI Agents Automate End-to-End, Long-Horizon, Policy-Rich Healthcare Workflows?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16679)**  `arXiv:2605.16679`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Haolin Chen, Deon Metelski, Leon Qi, Tao Xia, Joonyul Lee, Steve Brown, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  End-to-end automation of realistic healthcare operations stresses three capabilities underrepresented in current benchmarks: policy density, decisions must be grounded in a large library of medical, insurance, and operational rules; Multi-role composition: a single task requires the agent to play multiple roles with handoffs; and multilateral interaction: intermediate workflow steps are multi-turn dialogs, such as peer-to-peer review and patient outreach. We introduce $\chi$-Bench, a benchmark of long-horizon healthcare workflows across three domains: provider prior authorization, payer utilization management, and care management. Each task hands the agent a clinical case in a high-fidelity simulator of 20 healthcare apps exposed via 87 MCP tools, which it must drive to a terminal status through tool calls and writing the role's artifacts, guided by a 1,290+ document managed-care operations handbook skill. Across 30 agent harness/models configurations, the best agent resolves only 28.0% of tasks, no agent clears 20% on strict pass^3, and executing all tasks in a single session slumps the performance to 3.8%. These results raise the hypothesis that similar gaps are likely to surface in other policy-dense, role-composed, irreversible enterprise domains.
  </details>
