- **[SAFE: An LLM-as-Verifier Framework for Evidence-Grounded Multi-Hop Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.01993)**  `arXiv:2604.01993`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Daeyong Kwon, Soyoung Yoon, Seung-won Hwang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-hop QA benchmarks often reward Large Language Models (LLMs) for spurious correctness, where models reach correct answers through invalid intermediate reasoning. We propose SAFE, an LLM-as-verifier framework for evidence-grounded multi-hop QA. Rather than judging only the final answer after generation, SAFE verifies reasoning during generation by checking intermediate steps against the provided passages and previous reasoning trajectory. To make this process checkable, SAFE decomposes reasoning into atomic, evidence-grounded units represented with Knowledge Graph (KG) triples. At train-time, SAFE verifies benchmark supervision under KG-grounded constraints and constructs reliable verifier training data. At inference-time, an external verifier checks each generated step, identifies invalid reasoning, and provides correction feedback before errors propagate. Across three multi-hop QA benchmarks, SAFE improves accuracy by 8.8 pp on average. These results show that evidence-grounded multi-hop QA benefits from shifting LLM-based evaluation from post-hoc answer judgment to stepwise reasoning verification.
  </details>

- **[The System Hallucination Scale (SHS): A Human-Centered Instrument for Assessing Perceived Reliability of Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.09989)**  `arXiv:2603.09989`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Heimo Müller, Dominik Steiger, Markus Plass, Andreas Holzinger_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models routinely produce factually incorrect or misleading outputs, yet conventional automated benchmarks overlook how users perceive reliability during interaction. We introduce the System Hallucination Scale (SHS), a lightweight instrument modeling the famous System Usability Scale (SUS) and System Causability Scales (SCS). SHS measures perceived reliability across five dimensions: factual accuracy, source reliability, logical coherence, deceptiveness of presentation, and responsiveness to user guidance, using ten balanced Likert-scale items that provide a directional score and consistency diagnostic. In a supervised validation study with 193 participants (386 evaluations across 47 trained experimenters), SHS demonstrated strong internal consistency and high clarity, completing in an average of 4.2 minutes. Participants rated source verifiability and presentation deceptiveness worse than factual accuracy, indicating perceived vulnerabilities lie primarily in traceability and framing. As a proof of concept, SHS assesses human-perceived reliability rather than ground-truth correctness, providing open-source multilingual implementations and an interactive calculator.
  </details>

- **[Anagent For Enhancing Scientific Table & Figure Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.10081)**  `arXiv:2602.10081`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Xuehang Guo, Zhiyong Lu, Tom Hope, Qingyun Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In scientific research, analysis requires accurately interpreting complex multimodal knowledge, integrating evidence from different sources, and drawing inferences grounded in domain-specific knowledge. However, current artificial intelligence (AI) systems struggle to consistently demonstrate such capabilities. The complexity and variability of scientific tables and figures, combined with heterogeneous structures and long-context requirements, pose fundamental obstacles to scientific table \& figure analysis. To quantify these challenges, we introduce AnaBench, a large-scale benchmark featuring $63,178$ instances from nine scientific domains, systematically categorized along seven complexity dimensions. To tackle these challenges, we propose Anagent, a multi-agent framework for enhanced scientific table \& figure analysis through four specialized agents: Planner decomposes tasks into actionable subtasks, Expert retrieves task-specific information through targeted tool execution, Solver synthesizes information to generate coherent analysis, and Critic performs iterative refinement through five-dimensional quality assessment. We further develop modular training strategies that leverage supervised finetuning and specialized reinforcement learning to optimize individual capabilities while maintaining effective collaboration. Comprehensive evaluation across 170 subdomains demonstrates that Anagent achieves substantial improvements, up to $\uparrow 13.43\%$ in training-free settings and $\uparrow 42.12\%$ with finetuning, while revealing that task-oriented reasoning and context-aware problem-solving are essential for high-quality scientific table \& figure analysis. Our project page:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Jacobian Scopes: A Unified Geometric Framework for Token-Level LLM Attribution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.16407)**  `arXiv:2601.16407`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Toni J.B. Liu, Baran Zadeoğlu, Gurbir Arora, Raphaël Sarfati, Nicolas Boullé, Christopher J. Earls_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) make next-token predictions based on clues present in their context, such as semantic descriptions and in-context examples. Yet, elucidating which prior tokens most strongly influence a given prediction remains challenging due to the proliferation of layers and attention heads in modern architectures. We propose Jacobian Scopes, a suite of gradient-based, token level causal attribution methods for interpreting LLM predictions. Grounded in perturbation theory and information geometry, Jacobian Scopes quantify how input tokens influence various aspects of a model's prediction, such as specific logits, the full predictive distribution, and model uncertainty (effective temperature). Through case studies spanning instruction understanding, translation, and in-context learning (ICL), we demonstrate how Jacobian Scopes reveal implicit political biases, uncover word- and phrase-level translation strategies, and shed light on recently debated mechanisms underlying in-context time-series forecasting. On token-ablation faithfulness benchmarks (LAMBADA and IWSLT2017) across six LLMs, the distribution-level Fisher and Temperature Scopes significantly outperform established gradient-based attribution methods in 10 of 12 model-dataset settings. To facilitate exploration of Jacobian Scopes on custom text, we open-source our implementations and provide a cloud-hosted interactive demo atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Elucidating the Design Space of LM Data Attribution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40655)**  `arXiv:2610.40655`  `cs.CL`  
  _Steven Yu, Sheng-Yu Wang, Kimia Hamidieh, Antonio Torralba, David Bau_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Data attribution methods for Language Models (LMs) are typically evaluated using downstream tasks like fact retrieval. However, existing evaluations vary multiple dataset properties simultaneously, making it impossible to isolate exactly why an attribution algorithm fails. To diagnose these failures, we explore the design space of LLM data attribution, with a primary focus on evaluation. We introduce the Controlled Attribution Benchmark (CATT) for fact tracing, in which we fine-tune LLMs on entirely fictitious knowledge, providing unambiguous ground truth for attribution. This synthetic setup allows us to systematically alter one dataset configuration at a time, such as document redundancy and lexical distractors. With this configurable evaluation, we demonstrate that redundancy and distractors consistently degrade attribution performance. Rather than proposing a new estimator, we examine two design choices that are underexplored: the loss used to compute gradients and how per-token attribution scores are aggregated into a document score. We find that the true causal evidence within a training document is highly localized, but standard methods dilute this evidence into noise by averaging attribution scores across the entire document. We find that replacing standard cross-entropy loss gradients with the negative log-odds gradients surfaces the critical tokens from other uninformative ones. We can then aggregate only the top token scores to improve ground truth recall by 35 to 72 percentage points across multiple baselines on our diagnostic benchmark. The negative log-odds correction also generalizes to FTRACE-TREX, improving counterfactual retraining performance for every method we test, by up to 4.9x.
  </details>

- **[ShallowDefend: Harnessing Shallow Safety Alignment for Multi-Turn Defender](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40627)**  `arXiv:2610.40627`  `cs.CL`  
  _Qiyao Ma, Xiaodong Liu, Weiwei Yang, Mingqian Feng, Chengquan Guo, Jialin Song, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Safety alignment has enabled large language models to reliably refuse harmful requests directly, yet the same request distributed across a multi-turn conversation still succeeds far too often. This brittleness has a mechanistic root: Alignment is shallow, with safety behavior concentrated in a model's first few output tokens, and existing defenses inherit the weakness, supervising the surface form of the response without ever giving the model an explicit safety decision to learn. We take the opposite view of shallowness: if a short span determines the trajectory of an entire response, that span is the highest-leverage place to intervene, and the defender can occupy it first. We propose Assess $\to$ Decide $\to$ Respond, a framework in which the defender assesses the user's intent, commits to an explicit, parseable safety decision, and only then generates a reply conditioned on it, with the commitment trained by reinforcement learning at the payload turn. Because the decision is a token span, the reward can be a rule: a self-verified reward checks the committed label against the conversation's known nature, requiring no judge and no annotation, while the same format also supports an LLM-as-judge reward over a graded decision space with safe redirection. Across two model families and fourteen benchmarks spanning safety, utility, and helpfulness, our defenders achieve the strongest multi-turn safety in a controlled comparison, maintaining their lead in safety while preserving utility at a modest cost to helpfulness. A per-token KL analysis and interventional probes confirm why this works: training concentrates its effect on the brief deliberation that sets a response's trajectory, and the decision committed there is a causal control point. The very shallowness attackers exploit becomes the mechanism of defense.
  </details>

- **[PIVOT: Progressive Intervention via Online Teacher Correction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40576)**  `arXiv:2610.40576`  `cs.CL`  
  _Zhihao Yang, Boyuan Zheng, Puhui Tan, Yukun Chen, Kuan Zhang, Longze Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning from verifiable rewards (RLVR) is limited by sparse outcome-level feedback on hard reasoning tasks: when all rollouts in a group fail, rewards provide no learning signal. Stronger teachers can provide additional guidance, but this raises a central question: how can such signals be incorporated into RLVR to support successful rollouts while remaining learnable for the student? We distinguish two complementary mechanisms: trajectory-level intervention, which improves access to reward-bearing trajectories, and token-level supervision, which provides dense targets to guide student learning. Our controlled experiments show that either mechanism alone can remain insufficient, motivating their combination. We therefore introduce PIVOT, which inserts teacher-generated corrections into student rollouts and applies token-level KL supervision to these segments alongside the RLVR objective. Beyond their combination, we systematically analyze where to intervene and how to supervise, yielding a recipe for effective guidance. Across hard logic games and mathematical reasoning benchmarks, PIVOT consistently outperforms existing methods, nearly doubling mean avg@8 on logic games from 19.5% for the best-performing baseline to 35.6%.
  </details>

- **[IndicIPR-QA: A Benchmark for Extractive Question Answering on Indian IPR Laws](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40518)**  `arXiv:2610.40518`  `cs.CL`  
  _M. Mohaiminul Islam, Muhammad Abulaish_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Legal Question Answering (QA) over statutory texts is a challenging task in Natural Language Processing (NLP), especially in low-resource settings with complex legal systems. It requires answers that are precise and grounded in the law. However, to the best of current knowledge, no existing extractive QA benchmark provides exact answer spans from statutory texts across all seven Indian Intellectual Property Rights (IPR) Acts. This paper presents IndicIPR-QA1, an extractive QA benchmark for Indian IPR laws. The proposed dataset contains 4,462 QA pairs from 1,116 statutory contexts across seven Acts. Questions and answers are generated using GPT-4o and validated through nine automatic quality checks, single and multi-model LLM evaluations, and a 200-pair human audit. Section-level partitioning prevents source-context overlap across the training, validation, and test sets. Further analysis examines answer span length, lexical overlap, and multi-clause structure across the corpus and individual Acts. BM25 achieves a token-level F1 score of 20.21, while fine-tuned InLegalBERT reaches 85.02 under a unified sliding-window inference protocol. The large performance gap between BM25 (20.21 F1) and InLegalBERT (85.02 F1) shows that simple word matching is not enough to answer these legal questions. Performance also varies across the seven Acts, indicating substantial differences in answer-extraction performance.
  </details>

- **[Don't Lose the Thread: Empowering Long-Horizon LLM Agents with Cognitive Resource Self-Allocation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40350)**  `arXiv:2610.40350`  `cs.CL`  
  _Zheng Zhu, Xiaozhuan Liang, Xi Chen, Bei Yu, Jiaya Jia_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agents powered by large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable progress in solving complex reasoning tasks. However, LLM agents often falter on long-horizon tasks due to cognitive overload, as their working memory becomes cluttered with expanding and irrelevant information, which dilutes their attention and hinders effective planning and reasoning. To mitigate this challenge, we introduce Cognitive Resource Self-Allocation (CORAL), a novel reasoning paradigm that empowers agents to proactively optimize their context. Implemented as an agent-callable working memory management toolset, CORAL allows an agent to checkpoint its task progress and verified facts, and adaptively initiate a new problem-solving episode by resetting cluttered context while preserving only the checkpointed information, enabling it to resume reasoning from a clean state. We further enhance the agent's checkpointing capabilities using a Multi-episode Agentic Reinforced Policy Optimization algorithm. On the GAIA benchmark, CORAL with an 8B model achieves 42.8% average accuracy, with particularly strong gains on the most challenging long-horizon tasks (Level 2 and Level 3). Crucially, these gains come at a substantially lower inference cost: CORAL consumes 6-7$\times$ fewer input tokens than a ReAct agent. It also generalizes to multi-hop Knowledge QA benchmarks and to the considerably harder BrowseComp, where it improves a strong proprietary agent by 8.0 points.
  </details>

- **[AI Cosmos: An Agentic Infra for Situated Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40031)**  `arXiv:2610.40031`  `cs.CL`  
  _AI Cosmos Team, Jinfeng Zhou, Yuxuan Chen, Fangming Chang, Mingyu Su, Yifan Zhu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) can improve task performance during learning, yet these gains do not necessarily reflect learners' ability to thereafter apply the knowledge and skills independently. We present AI Cosmos, an agentic infrastructure for situated learning that organizes learning around two complementary principles: scenario-based participation and cumulative assets of practice. AI Cosmos organizes learners and role-specialized agents within learning scenarios that integrate task-relevant knowledge and, when needed, executable workbenches. An asset-memory loop preserves the processes and outcomes of practice as reusable assets with explicit provenance and dependencies. We evaluate scenario-based interaction through two preregistered randomized controlled studies examining the acquisition of legal reasoning and programming competence, holding the same underlying LLM across conditions. AI Cosmos achieves greater learning gains than a conventional LLM in legal reasoning and higher mastery of unseen programming problems than an LLM-as-Teacher baseline.
  </details>

- **[Tokenizer Tokens Tell Training Tales](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39919)**  `arXiv:2610.39919`  `cs.CL`  
  _Nishita Jain, Parth Upman, Shreyank N Gowda_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tokenizers are usually treated as fixed preprocessing tools, but they are trained on text and can retain traces of that text in their vocabularies. We ask whether a BPE tokenizer can reveal if a candidate dataset was part of its training corpus. The key observation is simple. Rare strings become tokens only when enough corpus frequency pushes them over the merge threshold. This suggests that membership signal should appear in rare tokens used by the candidate dataset, rather than in the order in which merges were made. We formalize this under a threshold model of BPE inclusion, showing that the likelihood-ratio test assigns its largest weights to tokens that are rare in the background but frequent in the candidate dataset. The same model suggests that merge rank and merge-graph position should not add a first-order membership signal once token existence and dataset frequency are known. We instantiate this view as RTAM-Fusion, which calibrates rare-token mass with shadow tokenizers and combines signals across rarity levels. On the tokenizer membership benchmark, RTAM-Fusion improves AUC and balanced accuracy over Vocabulary Overlap while using fewer shadow tokenizers. Existing defenses also continue to leak membership signal against adaptive attackers at production vocabulary sizes. Code:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Incentivizing Self-Learning in Large Language Models via Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39671)**  `arXiv:2610.39671`  `cs.CL`  
  _Lingkai Kong, Yuzhe Gu, Haiteng Zhao, Zijian Wu, Youqing Fang, Zhouqi Hua, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) draw on parametric knowledge and retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) to solve knowledge-intensive tasks. Yet problem solving in unfamiliar domains calls for tightly coupled knowledge acquisition and reasoning, beyond factual retrieval and aggregation or learning solely from a preassembled context. We study self-learning -- autonomously retrieving, studying, and applying knowledge from long-form reference materials on the fly. To investigate this capability, we formulate a textbook-based mathematical problem-solving task. LLMs must navigate textbook exposition, learn and apply relevant principles, and construct proofs for exercises, with reference solutions withheld. We introduce a BookNavigator interface to support this human-inspired study process and incentivize self-learning through reinforcement learning (RL) with fine-grained rewards. Experiments show that our method improves performance across in-domain and out-of-domain textbooks and subjects, raising Intern-S2-Preview-35B's overall proof accuracy from 51.5% to 79.5% and outperforming RL-trained textbook-free and fixed-retrieval baselines. These gains extend to complex long-context benchmarks involving different task types, with improvements over the base model of 1.7 and 2.3 percentage points on BrowseComp-Plus and CL-bench, respectively. Together, these findings suggest that RL can foster a domain-agnostic self-learning strategy, highlighting the potential of self-learning from long-form corpora to strengthen broader problem-solving capabilities on complex, long-context tasks.
  </details>

- **[Grounding Multimodal Reasoning with Evidence-Ablated Negatives](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39237)**  `arXiv:2610.39237`  `cs.CL`  
  _Chenxi Liu, Tianyi Xiong, Yanshuo Chen, Heng Huang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) has become a powerful tool for improving multimodal reasoning. However, standard outcome supervision suffers from credit assignment ambiguity, as a model may receive positive rewards by exploiting language priors or dataset shortcuts rather than grounding its reasoning in the requisite visual evidence. This makes high-quality negative responses critical, since effective negatives should remain plausible under the current policy while exposing the shortcut that should be suppressed. In this paper, we propose EVAN, a multimodal reinforcement learning framework utilizing EVidence-Ablated Negatives to train LMMs against their own language-prior failures. EVAN first identifies highly visually dependent examples using a scaled image information gain metric and preserves data diversity with a mixed-ranking strategy. It then constructs evidence-ablated images by masking the most discriminative image patches, eliciting plausible but visually ungrounded responses from the current policy. During policy optimization, these negative responses are incorporated as bounded auxiliary signals under the clean image, penalizing shortcut learning while maintaining RL stability. Experiments across challenging multimodal reasoning benchmarks demonstrate that EVAN mitigates modality imbalance and improves over strong RLVR baselines. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[HEARSAYBENCH: Can AI Navigate from Abstract Human Rights to Lived Lives?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39062)**  `arXiv:2610.39062`  `cs.CL`  
  _Ava Iranmanesh, Sobhan Lotfi, Ali Iranmanesh, Liwei Jiang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As large language models (LLMs) reach users worldwide, personal counseling has emerged as one of their most consequential applications, sometimes in life-critical situations. Yet broader access does not mean equal support. The text these models learn from disproportionately reflects people with the freedom to act, embedding an implicit assumption that users can leave, report, seek help, or appeal. Existing benchmarks largely inherit this assumption, overlooking users whose agency is constrained by structural conditions such as war, statelessness, and displacement. Meanwhile, models are increasingly shaped by normative frameworks such as Constitutional AI, often grounded in ideals like human rights, even as many users live where those rights offer little protection or recourse.We introduce HEARSAYBENCH, a human-authored dataset of 400 test cases with in-region human verification grounded in scenarios where structural barriers stand between a person and the exercise of their rights. Built from United Nations and other human rights reports, the scenarios span over 80 regions and cover social, environmental, and personal constraints. Drawing on Nobel laureate Amartya Sen's Capabilities Approach, HEARSAYBENCH tests whether models can distinguish what a person is formally entitled to do from what they are actually able to do. Across 14 proprietary and open-weight models, every model infers a user's situation more accurately than it advises on it. Stronger models narrow this gap, but their recommendations remain unsafe to follow. Models may understand lives shaped by constraint, yet that knowledge remains hearsay if it cannot translate into advice people can act on. Those people are not alignment's long tail; they are among the users for whom good guidance matters most. HEARSAYBENCH makes this failure measurable, a step toward systems that serve the world as it is rather than as assumed.
  </details>

- **[HEDGE: Gated Hypergraph Memory for Scalable Multi-Agent Debate](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38902)**  `arXiv:2610.38902`  `cs.CL`  
  _Amirhossein Abaskohi, Mahdi Mostajabdaveh, Zirui Zhou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-agent debate systems require agents to retain and revisit arguments across multiple rounds, yet limited context windows and unstructured memories make relevant information increasingly difficult to recover. Existing flat and pairwise-graph memories also struggle to capture higher-order relations, including multi-agent agreement, conflicting evidence, and evolving consensus. We introduce HEDGE, a selective memory framework that represents debate trajectories as semantic hypergraphs. Hyperedges connect related claims across agents, while intra-hyperedge relational graphs preserve fine-grained argumentative structure. A learned memory selection module (MSM), trained through supervised learning and reinforcement learning, routes each memory candidate to STORE, BACKUP, or OMIT, retaining useful information while limiting redundancy. Across seven diverse benchmarks, HEDGE consistently outperforms strong single-agent, multi-agent, and memory baselines, improving accuracy by 5.0 points on average over the strongest multi-agent system with GPT-4o-mini. The gains reach 8.2 points on conflict-heavy tasks, while HEDGE reduces memory usage by 48% and the number of debate rounds by 34%.
  </details>

- **[Gold vs.~Translation: Are We Measuring Language, Knowledge, or Locality in Translated Benchmarks?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38788)**  `arXiv:2610.38788`  `cs.CL`  
  _Klaudia-Doris Thellmann, Bernhard Stadler, Dan Saattrup Nielsen, Michael Färber, Jens Lehmann_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Translated versions of English-origin benchmarks such as MMLU are widely used to evaluate large language models in non-English languages, yet it remains unclear whether they measure the same constructs as natively created benchmarks. We frame this as a proxy validity question and compare DeepL-translated MMLU proxies against six native benchmarks in seven pairings across five Nordic and Baltic languages, evaluating 29 models on each pairing. Beyond ranking agreement, we test incremental proxy validity: does the translated score add predictive value for native performance after controlling for model size and language proficiency? Proxy validity varies substantially across benchmark pairings. Applying the same robustness criteria to all seven pairings, we find four robust effects, two marginal effects, and one null result. The strongest evidence occurs for curriculum-overlap benchmarks such as Skolprov ($\Delta R^2$=.128, 95% CI [.038,.253]), whereas translated MMLU provides no significant incremental value for Sweden-specific factual knowledge in SwedishFacts (p=.242). Because SwedishFacts is also the only open-ended benchmark, we cannot fully separate the effects of local content and response format. Overall, the results indicate a clear pattern: translated benchmarks provide a better proxy when their source content overlaps with the native evaluation target, while locally anchored knowledge is less reliably captured.
  </details>

- **[Implicit Minor Detection from Multi-Turn Dialogue in High-Risk AI Interactions: A Constrained Self-Evolving LLM Harness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38546)**  `arXiv:2610.38546`  `cs.CL`  
  _Xiaohan Zhang, Ying Zhao, Ruilin Guo, Xuanyuan Yu, Kaibo Huang, Yukun Wei, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In high-risk text interactions, a system may need to estimate whether the current speaker is likely to be a minor before applying age-conditioned safeguards. Many text-based dialogue systems lack strong identity signals such as account information, verified identity, facial data, or device data, and must rely on weak cues that emerge across multiple turns. We formulate implicit minor detection under weak identity information as a high-risk user-attribute inference task and propose a constrained framework comprising a large language model, a Harness, and a Maker-Checker protocol. The framework controls when inference is invoked, requires structured dialogue-linked evidence, and subjects validation-selected Harness revisions to version-level review. On a controlled Chinese benchmark comprising 8,328 multi-turn dialogues, our method improves average precision and minor-class F1 by 7.60 and 6.37 percentage points, respectively, over direct inference using the same LLM family.
  </details>

- **[When Support Crosses the Line: Evaluating Ethical Boundaries in LLM-Based Mental Health Support in the Chinese Context](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38452)**  `arXiv:2610.38452`  `cs.CL`  
  _Bichen Wang, Yufeng Jin, Yixin Sun, Yanyan Zhao, Bing Qin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly explored for mental health support, yet existing benchmarks primarily focus on supportive capabilities rather than whether models consistently adhere to professional ethical boundaries. This issue is particularly important in multi-turn interactions, where users may introduce new information or exert emotional pressure over time. We introduce MindEth-Bench, a benchmark for evaluating ethical boundary adherence in multi-turn mental health interactions. Grounded in professional ethical standards for mental health services in China, MindEth-Bench covers multiple dimensions of ethical conduct and uses Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS) to systematically explore interaction trajectories that stress-test model boundaries. Experiments across nine LLMs show that models that initially respond appropriately can shift toward ethical violations over extended interactions, with substantial variation across models and ethical dimensions. These results show that single-turn evaluations may overestimate the ethical reliability of LLM-based mental health support systems and highlight the need to evaluate ethical behavior throughout multi-turn interaction.
  </details>

- **[CoSTA: Co-Evolving Self-Play Timeline Agents for Complex Temporal Knowledge Graph Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38450)**  `arXiv:2610.38450`  `cs.CL`  
  _Haikun Liu, Zhichao Yan, Shaoru Guo, Jiawei Gu, Victor Gutierrez Basulto, Jiapu Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Complex Temporal Knowledge Graph Question Answering (TKGQA) involves more than retrieving facts: the timestamp or validity interval of one fact can constrain the search for another, while multiple intervals may jointly determine the answer. Existing single-agent TKGQA methods typically investigate these facts within a single reasoning context, where evidence from different event histories can become interleaved and distractors can misdirect subsequent retrieval. Distributing investigations across agents can separate event-specific evidence into distinct contexts, but effective collaboration must still be learned because question decomposition, subtask investigation, and evidence synthesis are interdependent. To address these limitations, we propose \textbf{Co}-Evolving \textbf{S}elf-Play \textbf{T}imeline \textbf{A}gents (CoSTA), which organizes facts into navigable timelines, delegates investigations to workers, and uses a coordinator to synthesize their original timestamped observations. CoSTA initializes a shared policy through role-conditioned distillation and jointly optimizes both roles through co-evolving self-play with final-answer rewards. Across three benchmarks, CoSTA achieves state-of-the-art overall performance, with particularly strong gains on complex multi-fact questions, while its compact variants outperform the evaluated closed-source LLM agents.
  </details>

- **[Holistic Diversity Management in LLM-Guided Evolutionary Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38407)**  `arXiv:2610.38407`  `cs.CL`  
  _Seockbean Song, Youngsik Yoon, Sungjae Lee, Siwei Wang, Wei Chen, Jungseul Ok_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM-guided evolutionary search iteratively selects existing programs, uses LLMs as mutation operators to generate a new program, and evaluates it to guide subsequent search. To preserve diversity and reduce the risk of premature convergence, it often adopts the island model, in which groups of programs, called islands, evolve separately. However, this structure alone does not guarantee distinct search directions, as islands may start from similar programs or converge toward similar solutions during evolution. This suggests that diversity must be considered throughout the search process, from initializing islands with distinct programs to developing island trajectories and sharing discoveries across islands. To this end, we propose HoliDivEvolve , a holistic approach to establishing and effectively utilizing island diversity throughout evolutionary search. Specifically, we introduce diverse initialization to establish different initial programs, trajectory adaptive island selection to allocate computation based on current score, recent growth, and the remaining search budget, and growth adaptive migration to share alternative ideas across islands. Experiments across diverse benchmarks show that HoliDivEvolve achieves competitive performance on most evaluated tasks. We further conduct controlled analyses to examine how our design choices affect search behavior and final performance.
  </details>

- **[TCC-RAG: Temporal Causal Chain-Guided RAG for Temporal Knowledge Graph Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38366)**  `arXiv:2610.38366`  `cs.CL`  
  _Xinxun Zhang, Yuxiang Wang, Shanshan Feng, Jun Zhang, Tieyun Qian, Binhang Yuan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) have spurred growing interest in temporal knowledge graph question answering (TKGQA). Most existing methods rely on semantic retrieval augmented generation (RAG), which suffers from fundamental limitations in complex temporal reasoning. On the one hand, semantic-based RAG methods struggle to capture the multi-hop causal knowledge required for complex temporal reasoning. On the other hand, these methods introduce excessive redundant knowledge, trapping LLMs in spurious reasoning chains that potentially result in erroneous answers. To address these problems, we propose a Temporal Causal Chain-guided RAG (TCC-RAG) to retrieve a concise yet sufficient set of causal knowledge to support complex temporal reasoning. Specifically, we reformulate the temporal knowledge graph into a multi-view causal cue graph, with causal cues inducing connections that explicitly encode temporal evolution and causal dynamics. Based on the causal cue graph, we construct a query-specific temporal causal chain to guide the retrieval process, enabling the model to focus on a concise set of causally relevant knowledge. Finally, we introduce a causal evidence filter to refine the retrieved knowledge, ensuring its causal consistency with the underlying temporal logic of the query. Experiments show that TCC-RAG achieves substantial performance gains of 10.72% and 8.40% on two TKGQA benchmarks, and delivers a remarkable 62.97% improvement for multi-step temporal reasoning.
  </details>

- **[Fu-TCM: a multimodal large language model that learns traditional Chinese medicine diagnostic reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38333)**  `arXiv:2610.38333`  `cs.CL`  
  _Jiahang Xie, Tong Jin, Lingfei Wu, Yaping You, Jie Huang, Yuanchang Jin, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are advancing in multimodal understanding and reasoning, accelerating medical model development. Traditional Chinese medicine (TCM) uses evidence from the four examinations to determine disease location and nature, differentiate syndromes and guide treatment. This diagnostic process constitutes a complete chain of reasoning. We present Fu-TCM, a multimodal TCM LLM trained on data retained after cleaning and filtering an initial collection of approximately 7.12 million examples derived from classical texts, textbooks, medical images, clinical cases and publicly available datasets. The model was further optimized using Bianzheng-Grounded Policy Optimization (BGPO), which explicitly targets syndrome differentiation through rewards for response format, intermediate TCM reasoning fields and final syndrome predictions. Fu-TCM-27B led all six benchmarks spanning TCM reasoning, text and vision, achieving a macro-average accuracy of 81.01% and surpassing the strongest reference model with complete results across all six benchmarks by 7.13 percentage points. Its largest margin was 11.90 points on Fu-bianzheng. Case-level analyses further showed a positive association between intermediate-field accuracy and final syndrome-selection accuracy in most evaluated models. These findings suggest that TCM syndrome differentiation may be computationally learnable and that structured reasoning supervision can improve performance across TCM tasks.
  </details>

- **[Keepace.ai: A Technical Report on a Large Language Model for Sports and Health](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38249)**  `arXiv:2610.38249`  `cs.CL`  
  _Keepace.ai Team, Bowen Wu, Heng Wang, Jufeng Mi, Kang Liu, Kun Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models hold promise for delivering safe, scientifically grounded, and personalized exercise guidance at scale, yet the sports-and-health domain still lacks curated training corpora and rigorous evaluation benchmarks.this http URLis a domain-specialized LLM built on Qwen3.5-27B through post-training, currently optimized for Chinese-language exercise guidance across three tasks: workout plan generation, sports-and-health knowledge QA, and personal sports data analysis. In internal rubric-based evaluations designed with domain experts,this http URLoutperforms its base model and larger general-purpose baselines, with the most pronounced improvements in exercise safety, boundary compliance, structured output completeness, and scientific accuracy. This report summarizes the current design, data pipeline, safety-first evaluation framework, and evaluation results ofthis http URL.
  </details>

- **[Two Birds with One Stone: Skill-Augmented Generation via Unified Retrieval and Utilization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38204)**  `arXiv:2610.38204`  `cs.CL`  
  _Zihan Luo, Jiahui Kang, Wei Zhou, Qilin Fan, Mingyan Li, Chao Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Skills, as reusable external knowledge, have demonstrated substantial potential to enhance the ability of Large Language Models (LLMs) to solve complex tasks. However, existing skill-augmented generation methods typically rely on separate models for skill retrieval and skill utilization, resulting in disconnected optimization of these two processes. To address this gap, we propose Skill-Uni, a unified reinforcement learning framework that jointly optimizes skill retrieval and utilization. Specifically, we adopt a generative skill retrieval paradigm that enables a single model to both learn how to retrieve relevant skills and leverage them for task solving. To effectively train this unified framework, we introduce a two-stage training strategy consisting of warm-up instruction tuning and GRPO, allowing the model to progressively improve its skill retrieval and utilization capabilities. Extensive experiments on five skill-related benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness of Skill-Uni, yielding average performance gains of 66.59% and 34.82% over the base Qwen3-4B and LLaMA3.1-8B models, respectively. Besides, Skill-Uni is compatible with various skill retrieval engines, demonstrating its flexibility and generalizability. Additional in-depth analyses also provide insights into the effectiveness of Skill-Uni.
  </details>

- **[ATI-OPD: Adaptive Teacher Intervention for Stable Long-Sequence On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38160)**  `arXiv:2610.38160`  `cs.CL`  
  _Jiannan Guan, Youxin Jiang, Yutai Hou, Qiguang Chen, Yirong Zeng, Haitao Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  On-policy distillation (OPD) trains a student on its own responses using token-level feedback from a teacher. As responses grow longer, the student's prefix can depart from the teacher's own reasoning, weakening its feedback on later tokens. Updates based on this feedback can reinforce the same deviations in later responses and destabilize training. Prior approaches such as length control and loss reweighting limit learning from problematic responses but leave their prefixes unchanged. Fixed cutoffs can also discard later reasoning. We propose \textbf{Adaptive Teacher Intervention for On-Policy Distillation (ATI-OPD)} to improve subsequent teacher guidance by revising the student's reasoning prefix. ATI-OPD replaces the earliest high-disagreement segment with a short teacher segment, then lets the student resume its own reasoning from the revised text. To handle renewed disagreement, it excludes persistently divergent suffixes from the distillation loss while retaining useful student continuation. Training diagnostics show less teacher-student disagreement, fewer gradient spikes, and fewer extremely long responses. Across five mathematical reasoning benchmarks and two teacher-student pairs, ATI-OPD improves average student-only accuracy over standard OPD.
  </details>

- **[Human-Residual Steering: Aligning Frozen LLM Judges with Human Judgment Distributions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37868)**  `arXiv:2610.37868`  `cs.CL`  
  _Chen Lyu, Phong Do, Gabriele Pergola_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are widely used as automated judges, yet most alignment methods target aggregated human judgments and overlook variation among individual annotators. Existing approaches to matching the full distribution of human ratings mainly rely on output-level calibration or parameter fine-tuning. We propose Human-Residual Steering (HRS), to our knowledge the first method to align frozen LLM judges with human judgment distributions through learned hidden-state interventions. An oracle analysis shows that low-rank corrections at a single layer remove most of the discrepancy between judge and human distributions. HRS learns to predict such corrections from the judge's hidden representation and rating logits, training on individual annotations with proper scoring rules for ordinal and nominal judgments and optimising through the frozen downstream layers. Across 14 benchmarks and four judges from three model families, HRS outperforms all evaluated frozen-weight baselines. With Qwen2.5-7B, it achieves the lowest Jensen-Shannon distance on 13 of 14 benchmarks, reducing the mean from 0.565 to 0.352 versus 0.377 for the strongest calibrator. HRS also raises mean Spearman correlation with aggregate human scores from 0.449 to 0.579, improves population-level estimation under limited annotation budgets, and trains about 88 times fewer parameters than distributional fine-tuning at a modest cost in alignment.
  </details>

- **[Scoring Explanations Without Reading Them: Structural Shortcuts in Automated-Interpretability Evaluation Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37676)**  `arXiv:2610.37676`  `cs.CL`  
  _Xupeng Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Automated interpretability scores an explanation of a sparse-autoencoder (SAE) feature by asking a model to predict, token by token, where the feature fires on evaluation examples or to judge whether marked tokens fit it. We find that the evaluation data standard pipelines build can be scored without reading the explanation. On Neuronpedia's stored fuzzing runs for 500 random GPT-2 small features, 91.3% of activating examples are cropped with the peak on the centre token, and a rule that says "yes" when the centre token is marked reaches balanced accuracy 0.979, against 0.838 for the stored LLM scorers (recomputed from the stored per-item verdicts, on marks rebuilt from the pipeline's code); a rule reading only the mark count reaches 0.969. The cue changes form across pipelines: current delphi always marks the same slot in its fuzzing negatives, and full-context runs mark negatives with more tokens. OpenAI's simulation benchmark lists each neuron's top-activating sequences first; a rule that predicts activity on the first five scores 0.227 in pooled correlation against 0.160 for GPT-4's published simulator (post hoc). We give closed forms for what such rules can score; show that one scorer's simulation scores are higher on stored snippets than on natural windows but not shown to be reordered; and build windows that remove the position cue, derive but do not build negatives that remove the mark cues, and separate the sequence cue by decomposition. On natural-text windows cut at random offsets, the best fixed-offset prior shared by all features scores 0.015. On them, that scorer, a typed decision model (Jev), reaches a pooled correlation of 0.331 over 49 features, against 0.013 with shuffled explanations, and an open 8B simulator with the original read-out reads the explanation there (0.194 against 0.019). Scores should be reported beside the best explanation-free rule on the same items.
  </details>

- **[ETC-Bench: A Multilingual Benchmark for Evaluation-Side Transcript Canonicalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36527)**  `arXiv:2610.36527`  `cs.CL`  
  _Zhiheng Qian, Junhao Du, Jing Peng, Hanqi Li, Kunyang Peng, Feng Xue, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Written transcripts of the same speech can differ in ways that are not uniquely determined by the speech signal. We formulate evaluation-side transcript canonicalization (ETC), which maps multiple surface forms to a single canonical form when their differences are not determined by speech. To our knowledge, ETC-Bench is the first and most comprehensive multilingual benchmark for evaluation-side transcript canonicalization, covering 181 phenomenon types across 12 languages. Each instance is a minimal pair that shares a sentence context and differs only in a target span exhibiting one phenomenon. To evaluate both canonicalization success and semantic preservation, we propose Semantic-preserving Exact-match Normalization Accuracy (SENA), which combines exact agreement between independently canonicalized pair members with a pair-relative BERTScore constraint against information loss. We further introduce the Taxonomy-guided Agent Canonicalization Skill (TACS), which provides LLM agents with phenomenon descriptions and explicit canonicalization contracts. Experiments with five baseline configurations and three TACS-based agents show that the agents outperform the baseline systems on shared languages. ETC-Bench provides a systematic testbed for evaluation-side transcript canonicalization across languages.
  </details>

- **[Understanding Jev-Style System One Models: Does Structure Reduce Selection Bias?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35826)**  `arXiv:2610.35826`  `cs.CL`  
  _Mingyang Song, Mao Zheng, Zhiyuan Ma, Xinpeng Zhou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Selection bias in large language models (LLMs) can persist despite instructions to ignore presentation order or prompts that elicit reasoning. Recent efforts to reproduce Jev-style System One behavior have produced a growing family of models that predict probabilities directly over supplied candidates. Yet systematic comparisons of selection bias across Jev versions, Jev reproductions, and generative LLMs remain limited in multi-option tasks. This leaves a central question: does Jev-style decision structure reduce selection bias? We address this question with a simple test that shuffles options while keeping question and candidate content fixed. Across two Jev versions, twelve Jev reproductions, and six generative LLMs, we trace individual answers through eight presentations and compare them with fixed-order repetition. On our primary collection of 1,700 questions from two benchmarks, Jev's accuracy varies by less than two percentage points, yet its cumulative failure set expands by 27.3-28.5% relative to the original order, with first failures continuing into the final presentation. Reordering loses substantially more initial successes than fixed-order repetition, a gap that also appears in highly repeatable Jev reproductions. Nor does Jev show a general advantage over the LLM baselines in preserving correct choices. Aggregation offers limited protection: voting yields modest gains for Jev, while even unanimous answers are frequently wrong. These results expose a gap between repeatable decisions and decisions that remain correct across equivalent presentations, showing why stable accuracy alone cannot establish resistance to selection bias.
  </details>

- **[SEMA-Auto: Sighted and Efficient Multi-Turn Attack with Autonomy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35743)**  `arXiv:2610.35743`  `cs.CL`  
  _Mingqian Feng, Xiaodong Liu, Weiwei Yang, Chenliang Xu, Jialin Song, Qiyao Ma, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing jailbreak attackers fall into two classes: static planners fix their single- or multi-turn adversarial plans in advance, remaining simple but losing sight of the victim's replies, wasting turns and limiting transferability; interactive generators read each victim reply to generate the next-turn attack but require in-loop evaluators for success signals or hints, incurring substantial costs and inflated performance. Bridging this gap, we propose SEMA-Auto, a sighted and efficient multi-turn attacker with autonomy, interacting with the victim model and terminating the session by itself upon success. To stabilize its behaviors and boost its capabilities, SEMA-Auto is trained in two stages. Synthetic bootstrap tuning installs the notion of attack targets and conforming decisions by fine-tuning the base model with bootstrapped trajectories. Decision-supervised reinforcement learning then optimizes two objectives jointly yet separately, reinforcing the attack with higher jailbreak reward while supervising the decision at each turn against an absolute target. Our method achieves state-of-the-art (SOTA) attack success rates (ASR) across multiple datasets, victims, and judges, outperforming all static and interactive baselines, within almost the fewest turns, demonstrating both effectiveness and efficiency. For example, SEMA-Auto performs a $76.0\%$ ASR@1 within an average of $3.0$ turns against DeepSeek-V4-Flash on AdvBench, while the best static baseline is $54.8\%$ with fixed $7$ turns and the best interactive baseline is $43.8\%$ in $3.7$ turns. Against safety-hardened frontier models, GPT-oss-120B, GPT-5.4, Claude-Haiku-4.5, and GPT-6-Astra, our counter-trained attacker achieves an average $59.6\%$ ASR@1 on HarmBench, $13.8\%$ over prior SOTA. Our code is available at:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Multimodal Filesystem Memory For Long-Horizon Embodied Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22686)**  `arXiv:2610.22686`  `cs.CL`  
  _Chloe Su, Jiahan Li, Zhenting Qi, Chaoran Cheng, Haoyu Chen, Sejoon Jun, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agents operating over long horizons need an effective memory that allows them to reuse their experience throughout long interactions. If the entire history of an interaction is retained, it becomes difficult to find the relevant experience, while compressing it into summaries may result in the loss of visual and spatial details that are required later on. We present \textbf{MFM}, a multimodal filesystem memory architecture that keeps complementary types of agent memory. \textbf{Episodic memory} stores the observations, actions, and outcomes, and \textbf{spatial memory} associates experiences with locations. \textbf{Semantic and procedural memory} build up general knowledge and reusable methods of acting, and \textbf{working memory} holds the current plans. MFM makes use of searchable textual records to connect these different representations to the underlying multimodal episodes. The agents are able to find previous experiences by means of contextual cues and to examine the original observations in order to recover details that are missing from their textual descriptions. Through consolidation, the agents convert experience into higher-level knowledge and procedures while still keeping references to the original episodes. In the case of long-horizon Minecraft and computer-use tasks, MFM leads to improved performance with three base models. With GPT-6-Astra, MFM completes \emph{Mine Diamond from Scratch} and reaches 16 out of 24 milestones on the \emph{Ender Dragon} task, as against 12.7 for the best baseline. On CUABench-KiCad it attains a mean reward of 0.440, as compared to 0.400 for the best baseline and 0.267 in the case of no persistent memory.
  </details>

- **[When Charts Deceive Web Agents: A Paired Benchmark for Misleading Visualization-Grounded Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12823)**  `arXiv:2610.12823`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yisheng Li, Dongming Han, Hongkun Pan, Bingcheng Mao, Zijian Wang, Yunlong Mao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Web agents increasingly browse, interpret, and act on web content on behalf of users. In many realistic workflows, however, the visual evidence they consume is not neutral: charts and dashboards can be misleading, shaping judgments without issuing any explicit malicious instruction. Unlike chart question answering, Web Agents treat charts as intermediate evidence whose misinterpretation can propagate into concrete downstream actions --- a reliability risk that remains underexplored. We introduce MisVisAgentBench, the first paired executable benchmark for evaluating how misleading visualizations affect Web Agent execution. It comprises 280 task instances in matched clean--misleading pairs that differ only in chart evidence. We further provide a diagnostic framework that isolates misleading-induced failures from baseline task difficulty and captures execution-level effects such as extra browsing steps and decision hesitation. Across thirteen vision-language Web Agents, replacing clean charts with misleading counterparts reduces the average task success rate by more than 34 percentage points. Moreover, these failures predominantly follow the targeted misleading action branch, suggesting that deceptive charts systematically redirect rather than randomly disrupt agent decisions. These results reveal that visualization robustness is a critical yet understudied dimension of Web Agent safety and reliability.
  </details>

- **[UBTree: Parallel Tree Drafting via Unigram and Bigram Models for Speculative Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39972)**  `arXiv:2609.39972`  `cs.CL`  
  _Chumeng Liang, Linxuan Wang, Xinyu Peng, Huabin Liu, XiaoXu Guo, Yuxin Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Speculative decoding accelerates language model inference by verifying multiple draft tokens in a single target-model pass. Recent parallel drafters have achieved breakthrough performance in frontier production models, but their effectiveness deteriorates as the entropy of target distributions increases due to insufficient draft diversity. To overcome this bottleneck without sacrificing parallelism, we introduce UBTree, a parallel drafter that couples a Unigram proposer with a Bigram selector to construct drafting Trees. The unigram proposer is trained with the standard cross-entropy objective to generate candidate tokens independently for each position, while a lightweight bigram selector predicts transition scores between adjacent candidate pairs. Unlike the proposer, the selector is trained with a renormalized KL objective on high-temperature data. This tree-native training broadens the supervision beyond the greedy path, encouraging plausible alternative branches that improve the chance of accepting additional tokens during tree verification. Across seven standardized benchmarks with Qwen3-4B and Qwen3-8B, UBTree achieves an average speedup of $5.84$--$6.94\times$ over autoregressive decoding and outperforms DARTree in all 28 comparisons. Production-scale evaluation further demonstrates UBTree's advantage over frontier baselines such as DSpark.
  </details>

- **[CARDAMOM: A Micro-Dialectal Arabic Speech Dataset for ASR](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34481)**  `arXiv:2609.34481`  `cs.CL`  
  _Bashar Talafha, Samar M. Magdy, Aisha Alansari, Alaa Alkhawaldeh, Abdurrahman Juma, Sharaf Makahleh, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present Cardamom, a micro-dialectal Arabic speech dataset designed to support fine-grained evaluation and adaptation of automatic speech recognition (ASR) systems. Community-curated by native speakers familiar with the represented varieties, Cardamom contains approximately 40 hours of transcribed YouTube speech spanning 21 micro-dialects across Egypt, Jordan, Lebanon, Mauritania, Palestine, and Saudi Arabia. Each segment is annotated with one or more operational micro-dialect labels, code-switching information, and utterance-level perceived gender, enabling analysis of sub-country variation that is obscured by conventional country-level labels. We describe the collection and annotation process, motivate the micro-dialect inventory linguistically, and benchmark four multilingual ASR systems in zero-shot and adapted settings. The strongest zero-shot system obtains 43.47% aggregate WER, with particularly high error rates on Mauritanian and Lebanese varieties; adaptation on Cardamom reduces its WER to 35.21%. Audio-based identification experiments further highlight the difficulty of fine-grained micro-dialect recognition: across three training seeds, a dedicated classifier obtains 46.85% accuracy and 31.95% F1. Cardamom provides a resource for studying localized dialectal variation and developing Arabic speech systems with broader regional coverage.
  </details>

- **[Verification-Aware Training for Speculative Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30135)**  `arXiv:2608.30135`  `cs.CL`  
  _Geonmo Gu, Byeongho Heo, HeeJae Jun, Yoohoon Kang, Sangmin Lee, Sangdoo Yun, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Speculative decoding accelerates large language model inference by using a draft model to generate candidate tokens, which are verified by the target model in a single forward pass. Verification proceeds sequentially and discards every position from the first rejection onward, yet existing draft training relies on token-level imitation of the target with a fixed per-position weighting that reflects neither property. We introduce Verification-Aware Training (VAT), a plug-in framework that simulates verification at every training step and turns the resulting accept and reject patterns into supervision. VAT consists of two components: (i) a verification head, a lightweight jointly trained binary classifier that supervises the draft model on whether each position survives sequential verification; (ii) verification-adaptive weighting, which replaces the fixed weighting schedule by keeping full weight up to each sample's first rejection point and re-anchoring the decay to start there. VAT modifies only the training objective, so it can be layered on top of existing methods without changing the draft architecture, the target model, or the inference procedure. Applied to EAGLE-3 and DFlash on Qwen3-4B, Qwen3-8B, and LLaMA-3.1-8B, VAT improves average acceptance length by up to 11.4% and wall-clock speedup by up to 8.7%, with consistent gains across math, code, and chat benchmarks. Code will be available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[Budget-Aware Compression Pipeline for Single-GPU LLM Inference: Methods, Trade-offs, and Coupling Effects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30076)**  `arXiv:2608.30076`  `cs.CL`  
  _Hongyu Yu, Yifei Shen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Single-GPU deployment of 70B-parameter language models on an NVIDIA GPU is constrained by device memory, long-context throughput, and engineering integration cost. We cast single-GPU inference as a budget-aware design problem over these three axes and study how pruning, quantization, and KV-cache compression interact under realistic execution. Controlled ablations show that layer-wise pruning makes weight quantization more robust. KV-cache sparsification complements INT8 KV quantization by reducing memory without hurting decoding speed, while static vector quantizers often conflict with dynamic caching. Guided by these coupling results and explicit budget tracking, we assembled a practical pipeline and compressed a 70B model to about 33 GB, sustained about 57 tokens/s on 10k token prompts on a single A40, and kept absolute accuracy within 5% on common and reasoning benchmarks. We contribute design rules and a reproducible evaluation protocol that jointly report quality, memory, and end-to-end speed, and we provide a foundation for automated pipeline search under realistic single-GPU constraints.
  </details>

- **[Hierarchical Acoustic-Semantic Modeling: Modality Separation and Semantic Coherence for Full-Duplex SLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06540)**  `arXiv:2607.06540`  `cs.CL`  
  _Zhenyu Liu, Xuanyu Zhang, Yunxin Li, Qixun Teng, Shenyuan Jiang, Haolan Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Developing seamless, high-performance, native intelligent full-duplex Spoken Language Models (SLMs) remains a critical challenge and long-standing goal for the speech and NLP community. Despite notable progress, recent endeavors are fundamentally constrained by severe modality interference, which causes substantial knowledge degradation and compromises semantic integrity -- ultimately making full-duplex SLMs feel unnatural and unintelligent. In this paper, through an exhaustive fine-grained analysis of model optimization dynamics, we uncover the root cause of such performance degradation, revealing that modality interference arises from inherent gradient conflicts between acoustic and semantic modeling when the two modalities are forced to share a deep parameter space. Guided by this key insight, we introduce Lychee-FD, a native end-to-end full-duplex framework designed to mitigate modality interference. Importantly, we propose a hierarchical parameter separation strategy that decouples conflicting modalities in deep layers while preserving cross-modality coherence via a dedicated semantic alignment channel. Extensive experiments on multiple full-duplex benchmarks demonstrate that our method significantly advances the state of the art, yielding substantial improvements in both speech intelligence (+7.4% on Spoken QA) and full-duplex interaction fluidity (+28.5% on FullDuplexBench 1.5) without compromising inference efficiency. To the best of our knowledge, this work is the first to achieve two key advances: 1) uncovering and elucidating the root cause of modality interference in full-duplex SLMs, and 2) designing an elegant hierarchical model together with a practical solution for seamless, high-performance, native intelligent full-duplex SLMs.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Surface Forms: A Comprehensive, Mechanism-Oriented Taxonomy of Indirect Linguistic Encoding for LLM-Based Coded Language Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.27314)**  `arXiv:2606.27314`  `cs.CL`  
  _Hamid Reza Firoozfar, Mohammadsadegh Abolhasani, Reza Mousavi, Paul Jen-Hwa Hu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  To avoid moderation and surveillance on social media, some users routinely invent indirect linguistic expressions (ILE) that camouflage sensitive meanings. Such disguised expressions surface as algospeak, euphemisms, and adversarial obfuscation, depending on intent and context, and often involve recurring encoding mechanisms. We propose a comprehensive, mechanism-oriented taxonomy of ILE that abstracts away from communicative goals and instead categorizes the underlying operations through which meaning is encoded and recovered. We evaluate the taxonomy by incorporating it into large language model (LLM) prompts and comparing it with four existing taxonomies and a no-taxonomy baseline, using 2,000 manually annotated TikTok and Bluesky posts. The proposed taxonomy attains the strongest document- and span-level performance across three LLMs. Compared with the best-performing benchmark, its document-level gains are +3.8 accuracy/+4.3 macro-F1 percentage points for GPT-5.4, +0.9/+1.3 for Sonnet 4.6, and +2.1/+2.2 for DeepSeek V4. While the direction of improvement is consistent, the magnitude is model-dependent. The span-level gains are mainly driven by higher recall, sometimes at the cost of precision. In an additional cross-lingual evaluation of 800 Chinese codedlanguage reviews, the unadapted taxonomy also achieves the highest document- and span-level F1 across the three LLMs. The empirical results reveal the importance of a comprehensive, mechanism-oriented taxonomy as a stable scaffold for detecting emerging coded language and a useful input to content moderation. Disclaimer: This paper contains content that may be profane, vulgar, or offensive.
  </details>

- **[QuechuaTok: Morphological Boundary Accuracy as a Necessary Metric for Tokenizer Evaluation in Agglutinative Low-Resource Languages](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23943)**  `arXiv:2606.23943`  `cs.CL`  
  _Maria Contreras_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tokenization is a foundational step in NLP pipelines, yet standard evaluation metrics such as fertility rate fail to capture morphological correctness for agglutinative languages. We present QuechuaTok, a systematic benchmark comparing four tokenization strategies - BPE, Unigram LM, WordPiece, and a morphology-aware PRPE tokenizer - for Southern Quechua (quz), a low-resource agglutinative language spoken by 8-10 million people in South America. Using a 200k-sentence corpus and the SQUOIA finite-state morphological analyzer (Rios, 2016) as silver standard, we evaluate three metrics: fertility rate, OOV rate, and morphological boundary accuracy (MorphAcc). Our results show that BPE achieves the lowest fertility rate (1.636 at 16k vocab) by memorizing surface word forms, while achieving only 6.67% MorphAcc. PRPE achieves 83.33% MorphAcc - the highest of all systems - demonstrating that fertility rate alone is insufficient to evaluate tokenizers for agglutinative languages. We further validate this finding extrinsically: on a Spanish-to-Quechua (Chanka) neural machine translation task, a model trained with PRPE outperforms an identical BPE-trained model by +68.7% BLEU and +17.0% chrF, showing that morphological boundary accuracy translates into measurable downstream gains. All code and models are publicly available atthis http URL
  </details>

- **[PsychoSafe: Eliciting Psychologically-Informed Refusals in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.09697)**  `arXiv:2606.09697`  `cs.CL`  
  _Gianluca Barmina, Federico Torrielli, Sven Harms, Jacob Nielsen, Flavia Filesi, Felix Mächtle, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) routinely face requests that should be refused, creating a trade-off between helpfulness and harm prevention. However, refusals themselves can be helpful. In high-risk interactions involving crisis, coercion, or escalating intent, blunt non-compliance may prevent direct harm while still failing to support the needs of the person behind the request. We present PsychoSafe, a psychologically-informed refusal framework that reframes refusal as structured supportive communication grounded in evidence-based intervention strategies. To develop PsychoSafe, we construct a corpus of 8,019 prompt-response pairs spanning five psychologically salient risk domains and apply prompting and parameter-efficient fine-tuning to Qwen 3.5 27B. On a balanced validation set of 500 prompts, evaluated with an LLM judge and validated through human ratings, PsychoSafe prompting improves overall refusal quality by 28.1% over a generic baseline, with particularly strong gains in external resource referral (+46.8%) and psychological grounding (+34.8%), while preserving downstream performance on non-refusal tasks. Fine-tuning achieves near-perfect refusal and resource-referral rates but reduces response relevance. Additional evaluations on SORRY-Bench and XSTest show strong in-domain robustness but limited out-of-domain generalization, suggesting that future work should diversify fine-tuning data to help models apply interventions selectively rather than schematically.
  </details>

- **[Kairos: Semantics-Aware Orchestration of Planning Engines across Classical, Numeric, Temporal and Hybrid PDDL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39454)**  `arXiv:2610.39454`  `cs.AI` `cs.RO`  
  _Francisco Martín Rico, Esther Aguado González, Francisco Miguel Moreno Olivo, Rodrigo Pérez Rodríguez, José Miguel Guerrero Hernández, Francisco J. Rodríguez Lera_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Planning at run time, as in robots that re-plan while they act, needs a planner that reads the PDDL its users write (durative actions, numeric resources, time windows, continuous change), answers quickly, and delivers only validated plans. Specialised planners are strong on individual fragments of PDDL, but they differ in coverage, latency and in what their failures mean. We study whether selecting heterogeneous engines from the semantics of each task can give broad coverage of PDDL, from STRIPS to PDDL+, while remaining competitive with specialised planners and making explicit what each answer guarantees. Kairos profiles each task to decide which of five engines apply (ground heuristic search, compressed and snap-action temporal search, time steps for hybrid dynamics, lifted search) and schedules them under one budget of time and memory. Every candidate plan is checked by a validator independent of the planner's transformations, and "unsolvable" is reported only when a search whose exhaustion is a proof exhausts its space. On 1,756 tasks from nine comparative suites, Kairos solves more tasks at the full limits than the strongest evaluated planner of each suite, with the largest differences on numeric, temporal, PDDL3 and PDDL+ planning, and it also solves the most tasks at 100 ms and 1 s. Resampling over domains, the gain is above zero in six of the nine suites, while on classical planning against LAMA it is within the noise. A further 450 feature-oriented tasks and 26 one-feature probes show that Kairos handles all seventeen features probed, where no other planner evaluated handles more than eight. Ablations identify compressed temporal search and the novelty fallback as major contributors, and a comparison with VAL traces every disagreement to a PDDL+ convention or to a documented VAL defect. Planner and benchmark are open source.
  </details>

- **[Long-Term Agent Memory through Complementary Curation and Consolidation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39790)**  `arXiv:2610.39790`  `cs.AI` `cs.MA`  
  _Abhishek Gupta, Dongge Han, Daniel Madrigal Diaz, Wei-Ning Chen, Mayukh Das, Victor Rühle, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-horizon dialogue agents require effective memory systems to recall facts, events, and relationships from interaction histories. In this work, we propose a hybrid-memory architecture comprising episodic memories and structured relational memories and a consolidation mechanism which preserves efficiency. We conduct an empirical study across two benchmarks: LoCoMo and LongMemEval-S. Our experiments demonstrate that the two memory types are consistently complementary, combining both outperform state-of-the-art memory frameworks and achieves 90.2% accuracy on LoCoMo and 85.5% on LongMemEval-S.
  </details>

- **[Deogen: Automatic Benchmark Generation for Reasoning over Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38687)**  `arXiv:2610.38687`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Sam Kouteili, William Liang, Keeran Murray, Adriana Zhou, Ramesh Viswanathan, Scott Shapiro, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Language models are increasingly being deployed in regulated settings, where a model must apply given rules to the facts of a situation to determine a verdict. Benchmarking this capability, known as deontic reasoning, remains a costly and manual process, with existing datasets largely hand-crafted. In this paper, we present DeoGen, a scalable, automatic, and parametrizable benchmark generator for deontic reasoning. By autoformalizing policy rules to first-order logic, we can construct decision trees that capture possible outcome trajectories. Each path down a tree becomes a benchmark entry, with each tree producing combinatorially many entries relative to tree depth. Paths ending at a leaf entail a verdict, while non-leaf paths measure a model's ability to abstain when presented with insufficient information. Evaluations show frontier models return the correct verdict 89-92% of the time. Notably, performance degrades (22-40% correct) on entries without sufficient information to claim a verdict, even with explicit instructions to abstain in such cases. In all, DeoGen presents an automatic, scalable, and parametric method for generating deontic reasoning benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[Post-Training Large Language Models with LLMON](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38120)**  `arXiv:2610.38120`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Basel Shbita, Michael Hind, Chad DeLuca, Nathan Fulton, Shubhi Asthana, Farhan Ahmed, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models (LLMs) treat all context tokens uniformly, lacking explicit separation between executable instructions and passive data. This leads to prompt injection vulnerabilities, ambiguous instruction binding, and unreliable execution control. We present a post-training methodology using LLMON (LLM Object Notation), a framework that enables separating instructions from data and requires explicit identifier-based execution. We construct a large-scale structured training corpus of 4.6B tokens spanning general, RAG (Retrieval-Augmented Generation), and tool-calling data, augmented with distractor-infused variants in which competing instructions and irrelevant data segments are present in context but not referenced. Post-training two Granite-4.1 base models (3B and 8B) through supervised fine-tuning followed by reinforcement learning, we show that LLMON-structured supervision reduces attack success rates on indirect prompt-injection benchmarks from 53-100% to 0.02-5.6% while preserving general capabilities. We further introduce attention-based interpretability metrics that diagnose failure modes at individual transformer layers for structured inputs, revealing how our approach reshapes internal attention patterns. Our results demonstrate that explicit execution semantics can be taught through standard post-training without architectural modification.
  </details>

- **[Evaluating Causal Reasoning in LLMs: A Case Study in the Challenges of Procedural Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38100)**  `arXiv:2610.38100`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Justin Clarke, Isha Karn, Patrick Murray, KJ Hill, David Jensen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) appear to represent and reason about a large range of phenomena. In particular, recent studies have examined whether LLMs can accurately reason about causation. One of the most commonly used benchmarks for evaluating formal causal reasoning in LLMs is CLadder, a framework for procedural generation of benchmark data sets that was released in 2023. Experiments with CLadder appear to indicate that LLMs can perform formal causal reasoning in a large percentage of generated examples. In this paper, we examine what can be reliably inferred when LLMs perform well on test sets produced by CLadder. We identify several examples of superficial and highly predictive patterns that the CLadder generator embeds in data sets it generates. We find that even simple models such as random forests perform well on CLadder-generated data sets after training on a sample of generated data. We also find that the performance of many commercial LLMs on CLadder test sets is extremely brittle and that removing large sections of the text that are critical for formal causal reasoning has almost no effect on performance. Given these results, the most likely hypothesis for the strong performance of state-of-the-art LLMs on CLadder is that they are exploiting superficial patterns learned from exposure to training data from the generator. Any inference that LLMs are exhibiting more complex causal reasoning must rule out this hypothesis.
  </details>

- **[Evaluation Metrics for Safe Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15315)**  `arXiv:2609.15315`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Lindsay Spoor, Aske Plaat, Thomas Moerland_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Safe reinforcement learning (RL) is commonly formalized as a Constrained Markov Decision Process (CMDP), in which an agent maximizes expected reward while keeping its expected cumulative cost below a specified safety bound. Existing safe RL benchmarks predominantly report whether an algorithm is safe on average, following this expectation-based guarantee. We argue that this convention is insufficient to reliably characterize an algorithm's true safety: it fails to capture how often and how severely the safety bound is violated, whether this holds consistently across tasks and safety bounds, and whether training-time behavior is representative of behavior of the final converged policy. Therefore, we introduce (i) evaluation metrics for safe RL that address each of these concerns and in addition allow for aggregation across tasks and safety bounds. We furthermore define (ii) a safety tier system to systematically categorize and compare algorithms in terms of safety and reliability at both training and for a final policy. Using this framework, we provide (iii) an empirical safety evaluation across multiple safety navigation tasks. Our results show that aggregate metrics, distributional reporting, and task- and safety bound-specific results each reveal information the other metrics cannot. We therefore recommend reporting all three jointly, rather than compressing this information into a single value, as is common practice. We provide SafeRLEval, an open-source evaluation suite to support the reliable characterization of safety in future safe RL research.
  </details>

- **[VoiceLongMemEval: Do Assistants Remember How You Sounded?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00570)**  `arXiv:2609.00570`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Ramit Pahwa, Parivesh Priye, Apoorva Beedu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  With the growing scale of multi-agent architectures and large language models, deployed AI assistants are increasingly tasked with reasoning over long, continuous, multi-session conversation histories. Current benchmarks evaluate this dialogue history as information retrieval over long horizon, temporal reasoning, or knowledge updates, while crucially ignoring the fundamental dynamics of human-agent interaction, i.e. how they said it.
